Cobordism Theory — Cobordism Groups, Thom Spaces & the Pontryagin-Thom Construction

Cobordism theory studies smooth manifolds up to the equivalence relation of cobordism: two closed n-manifolds M and N are cobordant if their disjoint union M⊔N bounds a compact (n+1)-manifold W. This notion, introduced by Pontryagin and systematized by Thom, turns the set of equivalence classes into a graded ring Ω_* under disjoint union (addition) and Cartesian product (multiplication). Thom's theorem (1954) identifies the unoriented cobordism ring Ω_*^O with a polynomial ring over ℤ/2, a landmark result that initiated the modern theory of cobordism. The Pontryagin-Thom construction reduces the computation of cobordism groups to stable homotopy theory via the Thom spectrum MG. Complex cobordism (MU) connects to formal group laws through Quillen's theorem: π_*(MU) is the Lazard ring. The Adams-Novikov spectral sequence uses MU to compute stable homotopy groups of spheres. Cobordism also underpins the Hirzebruch signature theorem, genera (L-genus, Â-genus, Todd genus, Witten genus), and the theory of topological modular forms (TMF).

"Cobordism is the most computable of all generalized cohomology theories."
— John Milnor
Part 1

📏 Cobordism Groups

Foundations
Cobordism Groups, the Cobordism Ring, and Thom's Theorem
A closed smooth n-manifold M is null-cobordant (or bounds) if M = ∂W for some compact (n+1)-manifold W. Two closed n-manifolds M, N are cobordant (written M ~ N) if M⊔N is null-cobordant — i.e., there is a compact (n+1)-manifold W with ∂W = M⊔N. The unoriented cobordism group Ω_n^O is the set of equivalence classes; the operation is disjoint union, which makes each group a vector space over ℤ/2 because every manifold is its own inverse (M⊔M = ∂(M×[0,1])). The oriented cobordism group Ω_n^SO requires all manifolds and bordisms to be oriented. Examples: Ω_0^O ≅ ℤ/2 (a point is not a boundary since the boundary of a 1-manifold has an even number of points), Ω_1^O = 0 (the circle S^1 bounds the disk D^2), Ω_2^O ≅ ℤ/2 (the real projective plane ℝℙ² is not orientable and does not bound a 3-manifold; but 2ℝℙ² ≅ ℝℙ²#ℝℙ² bounds). The graded abelian group Ω_*^O = ⊕_{n≥0} Ω_n^O becomes a cobordism ring with multiplication given by Cartesian product. Thom's theorem (1954): Ω_*^O ≅ ℤ/2[x_2, x_4, x_5, x_6, x_7, x_8, ...] where there is one generator in each degree n not of the form 2ᵏ−1. The generator in degree 2 is represented by ℝℙ²; in degree 4 by ℝℙ⁴; and so on. For oriented cobordism, Ω_*^SO ⊗ ℚ ≅ ℚ[ℂℙ², ℂℙ⁴, ...] — a polynomial ring over ℚ on generators in even degrees.
Cobordism groups classify manifolds up to the relation of bounding a common manifold; the unoriented cobordism ring is a polynomial algebra over ℤ/2 with one generator in most degrees.
Part 2

🌍 The Pontryagin-Thom Construction

Thom Spaces
The Pontryagin-Thom Map, Thom Spectra, and MG
The Pontryagin-Thom construction is a bridge between manifold geometry and stable homotopy theory. Given a closed smooth n-manifold Mⁿ, embed it in ℝ^{n+k} for large k. The normal bundle ν has a tubular neighborhood T ≅ D(ν) (the disk bundle of ν). Collapse the complement of T to a point: this yields the Pontryagin-Thom map τ: S^{n+k} → Th(ν), where Th(ν) = D(ν)/S(ν) is the Thom space of ν (disk bundle modulo boundary sphere). For a G-structure on M (e.g., orientation, spin, complex structure), the normal bundle is classified by a map ν: M → BG(k). The resulting map S^{n+k} → Th(γ_k) (where γ_k is the universal bundle over BG(k)) represents an element of π_{n+k}(Th(γ_k)). Taking k → ∞ gives the Thom spectrum MG: the sequence of Thom spaces {Th(γ_k)} with suspension maps Σ Th(γ_k) → Th(γ_{k+1}). The Pontryagin-Thom theorem states: π_n(MG) ≅ Ω_n^G — the nth stable homotopy group of the Thom spectrum MG is precisely the cobordism group of n-dimensional G-manifolds. For unoriented cobordism, G = O and π_*(MO) ≅ Ω_*^O. For oriented cobordism, G = SO. For complex cobordism, G = U. The spectrum MO has π_*(MO) ≅ ℤ/2[x_2, x_4, x_5, ...] as a graded ring. The Thom isomorphism theorem: H^*(Th(γ_k)) ≅ H^*(BG(k)) (a shift), which is used to compute the cohomology of Thom spectra via the Serre spectral sequence.
The Pontryagin-Thom construction translates the geometric problem of classifying manifolds up to cobordism into the homotopy-theoretic problem of computing stable homotopy groups of Thom spectra.
Part 3

🧮 Complex Cobordism and Formal Group Laws

MU
Complex Cobordism, the Lazard Ring, and Quillen's Theorem
Complex cobordism MU is the Thom spectrum for the unitary group U — that is, the spectrum whose spaces are Th(γ_k) for the universal complex k-plane bundles over BU(k). The coefficient ring MU_* = π_*(MU) is a graded ring concentrated in even degrees: MU_* ≅ ℤ[x_1, x_2, ...] with |x_i| = 2i. Quillen's theorem (1969): the coefficient ring π_*(MU) is canonically isomorphic to the Lazard ring L — the universal ring for 1-dimensional formal group laws. A 1-dimensional formal group law over a ring R is a power series F(x,y) = x + y + Σ_{i,j≥1} a_{ij} x^i y^j ∈ R[[x,y]] satisfying associativity, commutativity, and unit axioms. The Lazard ring L is the polynomial ring ℤ[a_{ij}]/(relations from formal group law axioms), graded such that |a_{ij}| = 2(i+j−1). Quillen proved that the formal group law on MU comes from the universal formal group law via the canonical map L → MU_*. The formal group law of a complex-oriented cohomology theory E is encoded by its formal group law F_E(x,y) = sum_{i,j} a_{ij}^E x^i y^j, where the coefficients are images of the Lazard generators. The Conner-Floyd theorem: MU^*(X) ⊗_{MU_*} ℤ ≅ H^*(X, ℤ). More generally, any complex-oriented cohomology theory E is a module over MU via the universal property of the Lazard ring. The Adams-Novikov spectral sequence (ANSS) is an important computational tool: E₂^{s,t} = Ext_{MU_*MU}^s(MU_*, MU_*(X)) ⇒ π_{t−s}(X). For X = S⁰, the ANSS computes the stable homotopy groups of spheres. The MU_*MU-comodule structure encodes deep number-theoretic information — the chromatic filtration organizes stable homotopy theory by heights of formal group laws (the Morava stabilizer group at each prime and height n).
Complex cobordism MU carries the universal formal group law; Quillen's identification with the Lazard ring makes MU the universal complex-oriented cohomology theory and the foundation for chromatic stable homotopy theory.
Part 4

🔥 Cobordism in Algebraic Topology and Applications

Genera
The Hirzebruch Signature Theorem, Genera, and Topological Modular Forms
A genus is a ring homomorphism φ: Ω_*^SO → R from the oriented cobordism ring to a commutative ring R. Each genus is determined by its values on ℂℙ^{2k} (the even-dimensional complex projective spaces). The Hirzebruch signature theorem: for a closed oriented 4k-manifold M, the signature sign(M) satisfies sign(M) = ⟨L(p), [M]⟩, where L(p) is the Hirzebruch L-genus — a certain multiplicative sequence in the Pontryagin classes of M. The Â-genus (A-hat genus) is the genus corresponding to the Dirac operator index; it vanishes on manifolds that admit a Riemannian metric of positive scalar curvature (Lichnerowicz theorem). The Todd genus Td(M) is the genus associated to complex cobordism; for a complex manifold, Td(M) = χ(M, O_M) by the Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch theorem. The Witten genus is a genus for string manifolds (those with w₁ = 0, w₂ = 0, and p₁/2 = 0) taking values in modular forms; it is the ∫-genus in elliptic cohomology. The Ochanine elliptic genus is a multiplicative genus with values in the ring of modular forms. The Stolz-Teichner program proposes that supersymmetric quantum field theories provide cocycles for cohomology theories: 0-dimensional SUSY field theories → K-theory; 1-dimensional SUSY field theories → TMF (topological modular forms). TMF is the spectrum of topological modular forms constructed by Hopkins-Miller and Goerss-Hopkins-Miller: it is the universal elliptic cohomology theory and its coefficient ring π_*(TMF) is the ring of integral modular forms. The Ando-Hopkins-Rezk orientation MString → TMF lifts the Witten genus to a map of ring spectra, showing that every string manifold has a TMF-orientation. The Hopkins-Mahowald theorem relates the Hurewicz image of the sphere spectrum in TMF to modular forms. These ideas interconnect cobordism, number theory, and mathematical physics at the deepest level.
Genera are cobordism invariants that connect manifold topology to number theory and modular forms; topological modular forms (TMF) represent the universal elliptic cohomology theory and receive an orientation from string cobordism.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Math Map

Unoriented Cobordism and the Thom Spectrum MO

The unoriented cobordism ring Ω*O was the first cobordism ring to be completely computed. Thom proved in 1954 that the Pontryagin–Thom construction yields an isomorphism ΩnO ≅ πn(MO), where MO is the Thom spectrum of the universal real vector bundle over BO. The homotopy groups of MO are known to be a polynomial algebra over the field 𝔽2 on one generator in each dimension that is not a power of two. Concretely,

Ω*O ≅ 𝔽2[xi | i ≠ 2k], |xi| = i.

The generators can be represented by the real projective spaces ℝℙi for i not a power of two. The Stiefel–Whitney numbers give a complete set of invariants: two closed smooth manifolds are unoriented cobordant if and only if all their Stiefel–Whitney numbers agree. This result makes unoriented cobordism a purely algebraic theory over 𝔽2 and provides the foundation for the Adams spectral sequence based on MO.

Key computational tools

  • The Thom isomorphism H*(MO; 𝔽2) ≅ H*(BO; 𝔽2) identifies the cohomology of MO with the polynomial algebra 𝔽2[w1, w2, …] on the universal Stiefel–Whitney classes.
  • The Steenrod algebra action on H*(MO) is determined by the Wu formula, allowing the Adams spectral sequence to collapse at the E2-page and yield the polynomial description above.
  • The Conner–Floyd theorem for MO states that MO*(X) ⊗MO* 𝔽2 ≅ H*(X; 𝔽2), showing that unoriented cobordism is the universal 𝔽2-oriented cohomology theory.

Oriented Cobordism and the Thom Spectrum MSO

Oriented cobordism Ω*SO is richer because the coefficient ring contains torsion. Thom’s Pontryagin–Thom construction gives ΩnSO ≅ πn(MSO), where MSO is the Thom spectrum of the universal oriented bundle over BSO. Rationally, the ring is a polynomial algebra on the even‑dimensional complex projective spaces:

Ω*SO ⊗ ℚ ≅ ℚ[ℂℙ2, ℂℙ4, ℂℙ6, …].

Integrally, the structure was determined by Wall (1960) and later refined by Milnor and Stong. The ring Ω*SO is a polynomial algebra over ℤ on generators in dimensions 4k, together with ℤ/2‑torsion in dimensions 8k+1 and 8k+2. The Pontryagin numbers and the Stiefel–Whitney numbers (mod 2) together form a complete set of invariants for oriented cobordism. In particular, a closed oriented manifold bounds an oriented manifold if and only if all its Pontryagin numbers and all its Stiefel–Whitney numbers vanish.

Generators and relations

  • For each k ≥ 1 there is a generator y4k which can be taken as the Milnor hypersurface H2k,2k ⊂ ℂℙ2k × ℂℙ2k.
  • The 2‑torsion generators are detected by the Kervaire invariant and the Arf invariant of the associated quadratic forms on middle‑dimensional homology.
  • The Hirzebruch signature theorem expresses the signature of a 4k‑manifold as a polynomial in Pontryagin numbers; this polynomial is the L‑genus and gives the image of the signature homomorphism Ω4kSO → ℤ.
Worked example: computing an oriented cobordism class
Determine whether the product manifold ℂℙ2 × ℂℙ2 is zero in Ω8SO.
The oriented cobordism group Ω8SO is free abelian of rank two, generated by ℂℙ4 and ℂℙ2 × ℂℙ2. The Pontryagin numbers of ℂℙ2 × ℂℙ2 are p12 = 4 and p2 = 0, while for ℂℙ4 they are p12 = 25 and p2 = 10. Since the Pontryagin numbers of ℂℙ2 × ℂℙ2 are not all zero, the manifold is not null‑cobordant. Hence its class in Ω8SO is non‑zero.
Exam strategy
When a question asks for the cobordism class of a product, compute the Pontryagin numbers using the Whitney sum formula for tangent bundles. Compare the resulting numbers with the known basis of Ω*SO ⊗ ℚ; if they are linearly independent from the basis elements, the class is non‑zero. Memorise the first few Pontryagin numbers of ℂℙ2k and of the Milnor hypersurfaces to speed up calculations.

Spin Cobordism and the Anderson–Brown–Peterson Spectral Sequence

Spin cobordism Ω*Spin classifies manifolds equipped with a spin structure, i.e. a lift of the structure group from SO to Spin. The Pontryagin–Thom isomorphism identifies ΩnSpin with πn(MSpin), the homotopy groups of the Thom spectrum of the universal spin bundle over BSpin. Anderson, Brown, and Peterson (1966) constructed a spectral sequence

E2s,t = ExtA(1)s,t(H*(MSpin; 𝔽2), 𝔽2) ⇒ πt−s(MSpin)̂2,

where A(1) is the subalgebra of the Steenrod algebra generated by Sq1 and Sq2. This spectral sequence computes the 2‑completed spin cobordism groups and reveals the close relationship between MSpin and the real K‑theory spectrum KO. In low dimensions the groups are:

  • Ω0Spin ≅ ℤ, generated by a point.
  • Ω1Spin = Ω2Spin = 0.
  • Ω3Spin = 0.
  • Ω4Spin ≅ ℤ, generated by the K3 surface (signature −16).
  • Ω5Spin = Ω6Spin = 0.
  • Ω7Spin = 0.
  • Ω8Spin ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ, generated by the K3 surface squared and the Bott manifold.

The Â‑genus gives a ring homomorphism Ω*Spin → ℤ which is an isomorphism after tensoring with ℚ. The Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence also shows that the 2‑primary torsion in spin cobordism is detected by the KO‑theory orientation MSpin → KO.

Stratified Cobordism and Manifolds with Singularities

Classical cobordism theory assumes smooth closed manifolds. In many geometric problems one encounters spaces with singularities, such as algebraic varieties or orbit spaces of group actions. Stratified cobordism, developed by Quinn (1988) and extended by Siegel and others, provides a framework where the objects are stratified spaces (e.g. Whitney stratified sets) and the cobordisms are stratified maps satisfying transversality conditions on each stratum. The resulting cobordism groups Ω*strat fit into a long exact sequence relating them to the ordinary cobordism groups of the links of the strata.

Key features include:

  • The Thom–Mather isotopy theorem ensures that a stratified cobordism can be decomposed into elementary cobordisms along each stratum.
  • For a cone on a manifold L, the stratified cobordism class is determined by the ordinary cobordism class of L together with a choice of normal data.
  • The intersection homology groups of Goresky–MacPherson provide invariants of stratified spaces that are compatible with stratified cobordism; in particular, the signature of a Witt space is a stratified cobordism invariant.
  • Applications include the classification of singular algebraic varieties up to cobordism and the study of the topology of moduli spaces with boundary strata.

Surgery Theory and the Classification of High‑Dimensional Manifolds

Surgery theory, created by Browder, Novikov, Sullivan, and Wall in the 1960s, uses cobordism as a tool to classify manifolds up to diffeomorphism or homeomorphism in dimensions ≥ 5. The central object is the surgery exact sequence

⋯ → Ln+1(π1X) → 𝒮(X) → [X, G/O] → Ln(π1X) → ⋯

where 𝒮(X) is the structure set of manifolds homotopy equivalent to a given Poincaré complex X, [X, G/O] is the set of normal invariants (a cobordism group of degree‑one normal maps), and L*(π1X) are the Wall surgery obstruction groups. The map [X, G/O] → Ln(π1X) is the surgery obstruction; its kernel consists of normal invariants that can be realized by a homotopy equivalence.

Important consequences:

  • The s‑cobordism theorem: an h‑cobordism between simply‑connected manifolds of dimension ≥ 5 is trivial if and only if its Whitehead torsion vanishes.
  • The classification of exotic spheres: the group Θn of homotopy n‑spheres up to h‑cobordism fits into an exact sequence involving the stable homotopy groups of spheres and the image of the J‑homomorphism.
  • For simply‑connected manifolds, the surgery obstruction groups Ln(ℤ) are 8‑periodic: L4k ≅ ℤ (signature), L4k+2 ≅ ℤ/2 (Arf invariant), and the odd groups vanish.
  • The Novikov conjecture on homotopy invariance of higher signatures is equivalent to the injectivity of the assembly map in the surgery exact sequence for the group C*‑algebra.
  • Confusing unoriented and oriented cobordism invariants; Stiefel–Whitney numbers alone do not detect oriented cobordism.
  • Assuming the Pontryagin numbers generate all torsion in Ω*SO; 2‑torsion requires Stiefel–Whitney numbers as well.
  • Overlooking the role of the Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence in computing spin cobordism; it is not merely the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence.
  • Treating stratified cobordism as ordinary cobordism of the underlying space; the link data and normal structures are essential.
  • Misapplying the surgery exact sequence to low‑dimensional manifolds (n ≤ 4) where the theory fails without additional hypotheses.
  • Neglecting the distinction between the structure set 𝒮(X) and the set of normal invariants [X, G/O]; they are related but not identical.
  • Forgetting that the Â‑genus vanishes on manifolds admitting positive scalar curvature, a key obstruction in spin geometry.

Mastering these advanced topics equips you to tackle the deepest problems in manifold topology and stable homotopy theory.

Equivariant Cobordism and Group Actions

Equivariant cobordism extends the classical theory by requiring that manifolds and cobordisms carry a smooth action of a compact Lie group G and that the cobordism respects the action. The equivariant cobordism groups ΩG* form a module over the non‑equivariant cobordism ring Ω* and are computed via the Borel construction: ΩGn(X) ≅ Ωn(EG ×G X). For finite groups the theory was developed by tom Dieck, who showed that the equivariant cobordism ring of a point splits as a direct sum of ordinary cobordism rings of the fixed‑point sets of subgroups. In particular, ΩG*(pt) ≅ ⊕(H) Ω*(BWGH) where the sum runs over conjugacy classes of subgroups H ≤ G and WGH = NGH/H is the Weyl group. This decomposition reveals that equivariant cobordism detects not only the underlying manifold but also the isotropy data of the action.

For circle actions the theory simplifies dramatically. The localization theorem of Atiyah–Bott and Berline–Vergne implies that the equivariant cobordism class of a compact S1‑manifold is determined by its fixed‑point components together with the weights of the S1 representation on the normal bundles. Consequently, the forgetful map ΩS1* → Ω* becomes injective after inverting the Euler class of the standard representation. This injectivity is the algebraic shadow of the fact that a circle action with isolated fixed points can be reconstructed from the weights at those points.

Applications of equivariant cobordism include the classification of Hamiltonian torus actions on symplectic manifolds via the moment‑map image, the computation of the equivariant Chern numbers that appear in the Atiyah–Singer index theorem for families, and the study of transformation groups on exotic spheres. The tom Dieck splitting also provides a practical algorithm: to compute ΩGn for a given finite group G, one computes the ordinary cobordism groups of the classifying spaces of the Weyl groups, which are often accessible via the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence.

The Cobordism Hypothesis and Topological Quantum Field Theory

The cobordism hypothesis, formulated by Baez and Dolan in 1995 and proved in full generality by Lurie in 2009, asserts that the (∞,n)‑category of n‑dimensional cobordisms with a given tangential structure is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category with duals generated by a single object. In concrete terms, an n‑dimensional topological quantum field theory (TQFT) with values in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category C is completely determined by the image of the positively framed point, which must be a fully dualizable object of C. This equivalence between TQFTs and fully dualizable objects transforms the classification of TQFTs into a problem of higher algebra.

For oriented cobordisms the hypothesis says that the (∞,n)‑category Bordnor is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category with duals on one object. The proof uses the theory of (∞,n)‑categories, factorization homology, and the classification of manifolds with corners. A crucial technical ingredient is the existence of a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category of “higher vector spaces” (e.g. n‑vector spaces) in which every object is fully dualizable; this provides a large supply of examples of TQFTs. The hypothesis also extends to other tangential structures such as spin, string, or framing, by replacing the generating object with a point equipped with the corresponding structure.

One immediate consequence is the classification of 2‑dimensional TQFTs: they correspond exactly to commutative Frobenius algebras in the target category. In dimension 3, the hypothesis predicts that 3‑dimensional TQFTs with values in the category of tensor categories are classified by modular tensor categories, recovering the Reshetikhin–Turaev construction. In higher dimensions the hypothesis organizes the search for extended TQFTs, for instance those arising from the representation theory of quantum groups at roots of unity or from the geometry of moduli stacks of local systems. The cobordism hypothesis thus sits at the intersection of stable homotopy theory, higher category theory, and mathematical physics.

Technique: Using the Cobordism Hypothesis to Identify a TQFT
Suppose you are given a symmetric monoidal (∞,2)‑category C and an object X ∈ C that is fully dualizable. Explain why the assignment that sends the positively framed point to X extends uniquely to a 2‑dimensional framed TQFT Z : Bord2fr → C.
By the cobordism hypothesis for n = 2 and tangential structure “framing”, the (∞,2)‑category Bord2fr is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,2)‑category with duals on a single object. The universal property of a free object states that any symmetric monoidal functor out of Bord2fr is uniquely determined by the image of the generator, provided that image is fully dualizable in the target. Since X is fully dualizable in C, there exists a unique symmetric monoidal functor Z with Z(pt+) = X. This functor automatically assigns to every 1‑manifold the corresponding tensor power of X, to every 2‑dimensional cobordism the morphism obtained by composing the evaluation and coevaluation maps coming from the duality data of X, and respects gluing because the duality axioms in C match the gluing axioms in Bord2fr. Hence the extension exists and is unique.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks you to “classify” a low‑dimensional TQFT, first identify the tangential structure (oriented, framed, spin, etc.) and the target category. Then recall the cobordism hypothesis: the classification reduces to describing fully dualizable objects in that target. For 2‑dimensional theories, fully dualizable objects are exactly commutative Frobenius algebras; for 1‑dimensional theories they are just dualizable objects (i.e. finite‑dimensional vector spaces). Write down the algebraic data explicitly — multiplication, unit, comultiplication, counit, and the Frobenius condition — and verify that the axioms match the gluing of cobordisms. This algebraic translation is what examiners expect.

The Adams–Novikov Spectral Sequence and Complex Cobordism

The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (ANSS) is the primary computational tool for the stable homotopy groups of spheres using complex cobordism MU as the coefficient ring. Its E2‑page is ExtMU*MU>s,t(MU*, MU*) and it converges to the 2‑completed stable homotopy groups πt‑sŜ2. The Hopf algebroid (MU*, MU*MU) encodes the formal group law of MU; the cobar complex computing the Ext groups is built from the comodule structure induced by the coproduct on MU*MU. The ANSS improves on the classical Adams spectral sequence by replacing mod‑p homology with MU‑homology, thereby capturing all primes simultaneously and providing a chromatic filtration.

Chromatic homotopy theory organizes the ANSS by height of formal groups. At height 1 the E2‑term recovers the image of J and the Adams spectral sequence for p‑local spheres. At height 2 the Morava K‑theories K(2) and the associated Morava E‑theories E2 detect the first layer of v2‑periodic phenomena, such as the Greek letter elements βi in the stable stems. The nilpotence theorem of Devinatz–Hopkins–Smith shows that any map between finite spectra that is zero in MU‑homology is nilpotent, which implies that the ANSS detects all non‑nilpotent stable homotopy classes. The periodicity theorem then guarantees that for each height n there is a vn‑self map on a finite type n complex, giving rise to infinite families in the ANSS.

Computations of the ANSS have been carried out through a range of stems using the May spectral sequence, the algebraic Novikov spectral sequence, and computer algebra systems. The results reveal that the 2‑primary stable stems up to dimension 60 are largely accounted for by the image of J, the α, β, and γ families, and a handful of sporadic elements. The ANSS also provides a conceptual proof of the telescope conjecture for finite spectra of type n, linking the Bousfield localization with respect to MU to the chromatic localization functors Ln. Mastery of the ANSS requires fluency with the structure of MU*MU, the Landweber exact functor theorem, and the theory of formal groups over graded rings.

String Cobordism and Higher Structures

String cobordism is the cobordism theory associated to the 3‑connected cover BString → BO, i.e. manifolds equipped with a lift of the stable normal bundle to a string structure (vanishing of the first two fractional Pontryagin classes). The Thom spectrum MString fits into a tower of maps MString → MSpin → MSO → MO. The homotopy groups π*MString are the string cobordism groups ΩString*. A fundamental result of Stolz and Teichner relates MString to topological modular forms (tmf): there is a map of ring spectra MString → tmf which induces an isomorphism on homotopy groups after inverting 2 and 3, and the kernel is precisely the torsion detected by the Witten genus. The Witten genus is a ring homomorphism ΩString* → MF*(SL2(ℤ)) taking values in integral modular forms; it vanishes on manifolds admitting a metric of positive Ricci curvature, providing a powerful obstruction.

The computation of ΩString* in low dimensions uses the Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence for MString together with the known homotopy of tmf. The groups are: ΩString0 ≅ ℤ, ΩString1 = ΩString2 = ΩString3 = 0, ΩString4 ≅ ℤ generated by the K3 surface, ΩString5 = ΩString6 = ΩString7 = 0, ΩString8 ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ generated by K3² and a certain Bott manifold, and ΩString12 contains a ℤ/2 summand detected by the Witten genus. Higher string structures (Fivebrane, Ninebrane) correspond to further connected covers of BO and their Thom spectra are related to higher chromatic cohomology theories such as tmf1(3) and the elliptic cohomology of higher level.

String cobordism also appears in the physics of the heterotic string, where the anomaly cancellation condition is precisely the requirement that the spacetime manifold admit a string structure. The Stolz–Teichner program aims to construct a 2‑dimensional supersymmetric conformal field theory for each string manifold, whose partition function is the Witten genus. This geometric interpretation of tmf as a cobordism invariant of string manifolds is a central theme in modern topological quantum field theory.

Cobordism Invariants from the Index Theorem

The Atiyah–Singer index theorem provides a bridge between analysis and cobordism by expressing the index of an elliptic operator on a closed manifold as a topological invariant — a characteristic number. For a Dirac operator coupled to a vector bundle E on a spin manifold M, the index is the Â‑genus Â(M) twisted by the Chern character of E. Since the index is an integer, any characteristic number that can be realized as the index of a geometric operator is a cobordism invariant. This principle yields the classic theorem: the Pontryagin numbers and Stiefel‑Whitney numbers are the complete set of invariants for oriented and unoriented cobordism respectively, because they arise as indices of the signature operator and the de Rham operator with appropriate coefficients.

In the spin setting, the Â‑genus gives a ring homomorphism ΩSpin* → ℤ which is an isomorphism after tensoring with ℚ. The kernel consists of torsion elements, many of which are detected by the KO‑theory orientation MSpin → KO. The index theorem for families extends this to a map of spectra MSpin → KO, and the Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence computes the homotopy groups of MSpin by filtering through KO. For string manifolds, the Witten genus is the index of the Dirac operator on the free loop space, formally the index of a hypothetical “Dirac–Ramond” operator. Its rigidity under S1 actions (the Witten rigidity theorem) implies that the Witten genus takes values in modular forms and vanishes on manifolds with positive Ricci curvature.

These index‑theoretic invariants are not merely abstract; they provide concrete obstructions to the existence of metrics with special curvature properties. For example, a spin manifold with non‑zero Â‑genus cannot admit a metric of positive scalar curvature. Similarly, a string manifold with non‑zero Witten genus cannot admit a metric of positive Ricci curvature. The interplay between the index theorem, cobordism, and geometry is a recurring theme in high‑dimensional topology and global analysis.

  • Confusing the equivariant cobordism ring of a point with the ordinary cobordism ring of the classifying space BG; the tom Dieck splitting involves Weyl groups, not BG itself.
  • Assuming the cobordism hypothesis classifies all TQFTs without specifying the tangential structure and the target (∞,n)‑category; the hypothesis only applies to fully extended theories valued in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category with duals.
  • Treating the Adams–Novikov E2‑page as a simple Ext group over a ring; it is an Ext over the Hopf algebroid (MU*, MU*MU), and the comodule structure is essential.
  • Overlooking that the Witten genus is a ring homomorphism only on string cobordism, not on spin cobordism; on spin manifolds it lands in a larger ring of modular forms with possible denominators.
  • Believing that the Â‑genus detects all torsion in spin cobordism; it detects only the rational part, while 2‑torsion requires the KO‑orientation and the Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence.
  • Neglecting the distinction between the free loop space Dirac operator (Witten genus) and the ordinary Dirac operator (Â‑genus); they live in different analytical settings and give different invariants.

Relative Cobordism and the Long Exact Sequence of a Pair

Relative cobordism extends the absolute theory by allowing manifolds with boundary to be cobordant relative to a fixed submanifold. Given a pair (X, A) where A ⊂ X is a closed submanifold, a relative cobordism is a compact manifold W with ∂W = M₀ ∪ M₁ ∪ (A × I) such that the restriction to A × I is the product cobordism. The set of relative cobordism classes forms a group Ωⁿ(X, A) which fits into a long exact sequence of the pair: … → Ωₙ(A) → Ωₙ(X) → Ωₙ(X, A) → Ωₙ₋₁(A) → … . The connecting homomorphism is realised geometrically by taking the boundary of a relative cobordism and restricting to A. This sequence is natural with respect to maps of pairs and is a fundamental computational tool; for example, the relative groups Ωⁿ(Dⁿ, Sⁿ⁻¹) are isomorphic to the absolute groups Ωₙ₋₁(point) via the Thom isomorphism of the normal bundle of the inclusion Sⁿ⁻¹ Dⁿ. In practice, one often computes Ωⁿ(X) by filtering X with a CW‑structure and using the associated Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence whose E²‑page is Hₚ(X; Ω_q(point)). The relative groups appear as the homology of the chain complex of this filtration, and the long exact sequence of a pair is precisely the long exact sequence of the pair of chain complexes. A classic application is the computation of the unoriented cobordism ring Ω_*^O: the groups Ωₙ(BO(k), BO(k−1)) detect the Stiefel‑Whitney numbers of the universal bundle, and the long exact sequence of the pair (BO(k), BO(k−1)) yields the recursive determination of the polynomial generators x_i in Ω_*^O ≅ ℤ/2[x_i | i ≠ 2^j−1]. The same pattern underlies the oriented and spin calculations, where the relative groups for the inclusions BSO(k) BSO(k+1) and BSpin(k) BSpin(k+1) control the Pontryagin and Â‑genera respectively.

The Thom Isomorphism and Its Role in Computing Cobordism Rings

The Thom isomorphism is the bridge between the geometry of vector bundles and the algebra of cobordism. For a real vector bundle ξ of rank n over a base B, the Thom space Th(ξ) = D(ξ)/S(ξ) carries a fundamental class u_ξ ∈ Hⁿ(Th(ξ); ℤ/2) (or in oriented cohomology with appropriate coefficients) such that the map x ↦ x ⌣ u_ξ gives an isomorphism H^*(B) → H*+n(Th(ξ)). In cobordism, the Thom spectrum Mξ is defined as the colimit of the Thom spaces of the pullbacks of ξ over the classifying space Bξ. The Pontryagin‑Thom construction identifies the cobordism group Ωₙ^ξ(X) with the stable homotopy group πₙ(Mξ ∧ X₊). The Thom isomorphism in cobordism takes the form Ω_*^ξ(B) ≅ Ω*+n^ξ(Th(ξ)), and it is induced by the map of spectra Mξ → Σⁿ Mξ given by the Thom class. This isomorphism is natural with respect to bundle maps and respects the ring structure: the product of Thom classes corresponds to the external product of bundles. Computationally, the Thom isomorphism reduces the calculation of Ω_*^ξ for a general space to the calculation of the coefficient ring Ω_*^ξ(point) together with the action of the Steenrod algebra on the Thom class. For the universal bundles over BO, BSO, and BSpin, the Thom spectra are MO, MSO, and MSpin. Their homotopy rings are the unoriented, oriented, and spin cobordism rings. The Thom isomorphism for the universal bundle γ over BO yields Ω_*^O(BO) ≅ Ω*+∞^O(MO) which, after passing to the colimit, gives the identification of the coefficient ring with the polynomial algebra on the Stiefel‑Whitney numbers. In the oriented case, the Thom isomorphism for the universal oriented bundle over BSO expresses the oriented cobordism ring as a polynomial algebra on the Pontryagin numbers modulo the relations coming from the Hirzebruch signature theorem. For spin bundles, the Thom isomorphism for the universal spin bundle over BSpin identifies MSpin with a module over KO via the Atiyah‑Bott‑Shapiro orientation, and the Anderson‑Brown‑Peterson spectral sequence computes π_*MSpin by filtering through the KO‑homology of MSpin. The Thom isomorphism also underlies the proof that the cobordism ring of a product splits as a tensor product: Ω_*^ξ(X × Y) ≅ Ω_*^ξ(X) ⊗Ω_*^ξ Ω_*^ξ(Y) after inverting the appropriate primes, a fact used heavily in the calculation of cobordism rings of classifying spaces of finite groups.

Worked Example: Relative Cobordism of a Disk Pair
Compute the relative unoriented cobordism group Ω₃^O(D⁴, S³) and identify the connecting homomorphism to Ω₂^O(S³).
The pair (D⁴, S³) is a 4‑disk with its boundary 3‑sphere. By the Thom isomorphism for the trivial bundle of rank 1 over S³, Ω₃^O(D⁴, S³) ≅ Ω₂^O(S³). Since S³ is a closed 3‑manifold, its unoriented cobordism group Ω₂^O(S³) fits into the long exact sequence of the pair (D⁴, S³): … → Ω₃^O(S³) → Ω₃^O(D⁴) → Ω₃^O(D⁴, S³) → Ω₂^O(S³) → Ω₂^O(D⁴) → … . The absolute groups of a disk are trivial in positive dimensions because D⁴ is contractible, so Ω₃^O(D⁴) = Ω₂^O(D⁴) = 0. The sequence therefore gives an isomorphism Ω₃^O(D⁴, S³) ≅ Ω₂^O(S³). The group Ω₂^O(S³) is the unoriented cobordism group of maps from closed surfaces to S³. Since any map from a surface to S³ is null‑homotopic (π₂(S³)=0), the only invariant is the Stiefel‑Whitney number w₁² of the surface, which vanishes for all closed surfaces. Hence Ω₂^O(S³) = 0, and consequently Ω₃^O(D⁴, S³) = 0. The connecting homomorphism ∂: Ω₃^O(D⁴, S³) → Ω₂^O(S³) is the zero map between zero groups.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for a relative cobordism group of a disk‑sphere pair, immediately apply the Thom isomorphism to shift the problem to an absolute group of the sphere. Then use the long exact sequence of the pair; the contractibility of the disk kills the absolute terms, leaving an isomorphism. Remember that the connecting homomorphism is induced by taking the boundary of a relative cobordism, so it vanishes whenever the relative group is zero.

Cobordism of Manifolds with G‑Structure and the Madsen–Tillmann Spectrum

A G‑structure on a manifold M is a lift of the stable normal bundle ν_M: M → BO to a map M → BG, where G → O is a topological group homomorphism. The cobordism groups Ω_*^G classify closed manifolds equipped with such a lift up to cobordism respecting the G‑structure. The Pontryagin‑Thom construction identifies Ω_*^G with the stable homotopy groups of the Thom spectrum MG = Th(γ_G), where γ_G is the universal G‑bundle over BG. When G = O, SO, Spin, U, SU, etc., one recovers the classical theories. The Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTG is defined as the Thom spectrum of the virtual bundle −γ_G over BG; it is the Spanier‑Whitehead dual of MG in the stable homotopy category. The key theorem of Madsen and Tillmann (later extended by Galatius–Madsen–Tillmann–Weiss) states that the stable moduli space of G‑manifolds, after group completion, is weakly equivalent to the infinite loop space Ω^∞ MTG. This identification translates geometric problems about the homology of mapping class groups and diffeomorphism groups into stable homotopy calculations. For G = SO(2), the spectrum MTSO(2) is equivalent to the complex cobordism spectrum MU, and the theorem recovers the classical result that the stable mapping class group of surfaces is the homology of Ω^∞ MU. For higher G, the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum provides a systematic way to compute the rational homology of the moduli spaces of manifolds with G‑structure. The homotopy groups of MTG are the cobordism groups of manifolds with a G‑structure on the stable tangent bundle (rather than the normal bundle), and the duality between MG and MTG exchanges the roles of normal and tangent structures. In practice, one computes π_*MTG by using the Adams spectral sequence with coefficients in a suitable homology theory (often Morava K‑theory) and the known action of the Steenrod algebra on the Thom class of −γ_G. The Madsen–Tillmann framework also clarifies the relationship between cobordism and the cobordism hypothesis: a fully extended topological quantum field theory with tangential structure G corresponds to a fully dualizable object in the symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category of bordisms with G‑structure, and the classification of such theories is given by the mapping space MapE_∞(MTG, C) for a target (∞,n)‑category C.

Exotic Spheres and the Kervaire Invariant in Cobordism

Exotic spheres are smooth manifolds homeomorphic but not diffeomorphic to the standard sphere Sⁿ. Their classification is a central problem in high‑dimensional topology and is deeply intertwined with cobordism. The group Θₙ of h‑cobordism classes of oriented homotopy n‑spheres fits into the exact sequence 0 → bPn+1 → Θₙ → Coker(J) → 0, where bPn+1 is the subgroup of spheres bounding parallelizable manifolds and Coker(J) is the cokernel of the stable J‑homomorphism π_n(SO) → π_n^S. The Kervaire invariant problem asks for which dimensions n there exists a framed manifold of dimension 2n with Kervaire invariant 1. The Kervaire invariant is a ℤ/2‑valued cobordism invariant of framed manifolds defined via the quadratic refinement of the intersection form on the middle homology. In the language of cobordism, the Kervaire invariant one elements correspond to non‑zero classes in the framed cobordism group Ω2nfr that are not in the image of the J‑homomorphism. The solution by Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel (2016) showed that such elements exist only in dimensions 2, 6, 14, 30, 62, and possibly 126. The proof uses the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for the sphere spectrum and the fact that the Kervaire invariant one element would give a non‑trivial element in the homotopy of the Thom spectrum MSpin(2n) after a suitable localization. The connection to cobordism is made precise by the Pontryagin‑Thom isomorphism Ω2nfr ≅ π2n^S. The Kervaire invariant one element, if it exists, would be detected by the mod 2 Moore spectrum and would survive to the E_∞‑page of the Adams spectral sequence. The non‑existence in dimensions ≥ 254 follows from the construction of a spectrum Ω with a map S⁰ → Ω that detects the Kervaire invariant and the proof that π−1Ω = 0. This result has immediate consequences for the groups Θₙ: for n ≠ 1, 3, 7, 15, 31, 63, 127, the subgroup bPn+1 is cyclic of order given by the Bernoulli numbers, and the exotic spheres are completely classified by the cokernel of J. In dimensions where the Kervaire invariant is zero, the framed cobordism group is generated by the image of J, and the exotic spheres are detected by the α‑invariant (the index of the Dirac operator) in spin cobordism. The interplay between framed cobordism, the J‑homomorphism, and the Kervaire invariant remains a guiding example of how cobordism invariants control the differential topology of spheres.

Chromatic Homotopy Theory and the Filtration of Cobordism Spectra

Chromatic homotopy theory organizes stable homotopy types by their complexity as measured by Morava K‑theories K(n). The complex cobordism spectrum MU is the universal complex oriented cohomology theory; its coefficient ring MU_* ≅ ℤ[x₁, x₂, …] carries the universal formal group law. The Landweber exact functor theorem shows that any formal group law over a ring R that is Landweber exact gives a homology theory MU_* ⊗L R. The height of a formal group law at a prime p stratifies the moduli stack of formal groups into locally closed substacks of height n. The corresponding localization of MU yields the Johnson–Wilson spectra E(n) and the Morava E‑theories E_n. The chromatic filtration of the stable homotopy category is the tower of Bousfield localizations L_n with respect to the wedge of Morava K‑theories K(0) ∨ … ∨ K(n). Each cobordism spectrum admits a chromatic resolution: for example, MSpin and MString are built from MU by killing certain generators corresponding to the vanishing of Stiefel‑Whitney and Pontryagin classes. The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for a cobordism spectrum Mξ has E₂‑page ExtMU_*MUs,t(MU_*, MU_*Mξ) and converges to the p‑local homotopy groups πt−s(Mξ)(p). The chromatic filtration of this spectral sequence reflects the height of the formal group law associated to Mξ. For MU itself, the formal group law has infinite height, and the spectral sequence collapses at height 0 after rationalization. For MSpin, the formal group law has height 1 at p=2 after inverting the Bott element, and the Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence computes π_*MSpin by filtering through KO, which is the height‑1 part of the chromatic tower. For MString, the formal group law has height 2 at p=2, and the homotopy groups are related to topological modular forms tmf, the height‑2 piece of the chromatic tower. The string orientation MString → tmf is a map of ring spectra that induces an isomorphism on homotopy groups after completing at 2 and inverting the discriminant. Higher string structures (Fivebrane, Ninebrane) correspond to further connected covers of BO and their Thom spectra are expected to be related to higher chromatic theories such as tmf₁(3) and the elliptic cohomology of higher level. The chromatic perspective explains why the computation of cobordism rings becomes progressively more difficult: each increase in height introduces new periodic families (Greek letter elements) and requires more sophisticated algebraic geometry of the moduli of formal groups. In practice, the chromatic filtration provides a roadmap for calculating cobordism groups: one first computes the rational part (height 0), then the image of J and KO‑theory (height 1), then the tmf‑part (height 2), and so on. This strategy underlies the modern computations of Ω_*Spin, Ω_*String, and the stable stems via the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence.

  • Confusing the relative cobordism group Ωₙ(X, A) with the reduced cobordism of the quotient X/A; they agree only when the inclusion A X is a cofibration and the normal bundle of A in X is trivial.
  • Assuming the Thom isomorphism holds for any vector bundle without checking orientability with respect to the chosen cobordism theory; for unoriented cobordism the Thom class always exists, but for oriented or spin cobordism the bundle must admit the corresponding structure.
  • Treating the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTG as the Thom spectrum of the universal G‑bundle; it is the Thom spectrum of the virtual bundle −γ_G, and its homotopy groups classify manifolds with G‑structure on the tangent bundle, not the normal bundle.
  • Believing that the Kervaire invariant one problem is solved for all dimensions; the Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel theorem leaves the case n = 126 open, and the existence of a Kervaire invariant one element in dimension 254 is still unknown.
  • Overlooking that the chromatic height of a cobordism spectrum depends on the prime; MSpin has height 1 at p = 2 but height 0 at odd primes, so the Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence is only needed at the prime 2.
  • Assuming the Adams–Novikov E₂‑page for a cobordism spectrum is simply Ext over the ring MU_*; it is Ext over the Hopf algebroid (MU_*, MU_*MU), and the comodule structure on MU_*Mξ is essential for the differentials.
  • Neglecting that the string orientation MString → tmf is not an equivalence; it induces an isomorphism on homotopy groups only after 2‑completion and inverting the discriminant, and the kernel and cokernel contain important torsion information.

Baas–Sullivan Cobordism and Manifolds with Corners

The classical cobordism theories of Thom, Pontryagin, and Milnor treat smooth manifolds without boundary or with a single boundary component. Baas and Sullivan independently extended this framework to manifolds with corners and more general singularities by introducing a notion of manifold with singularity type modeled on a fixed category of local models. In the Baas–Sullivan construction one starts with a symmetric monoidal category C whose objects are smooth manifolds with corners (for example, the category of manifolds with faces) and whose morphisms are embeddings preserving the corner structure. A C-manifold is a manifold equipped with a transverse map to a universal C-manifold; the resulting cobordism groups form a graded ring ΩC*. The Thom spectrum MC representing this theory is built as the colimit of Thom spaces of the universal bundles over the classifying spaces of the structure group of C-manifolds. When C is the category of manifolds with faces, MC is equivalent to the iterated loop space Ω∞Σ∞MO and its homotopy groups recover the unoriented cobordism groups of manifolds with corners. A key computational tool is the Baas–Sullivan spectral sequence, which filters a C-manifold by the depth of its corners and has E1-page given by the ordinary cobordism groups of the strata. This spectral sequence converges to ΩC* and shows that the cobordism ring of manifolds with corners is a polynomial algebra over the ordinary cobordism ring on generators corresponding to the corner strata. In particular, the unoriented cobordism ring of manifolds with corners is ΩO*[x1, x2, …] where each xi has degree i and represents the cobordism class of the i‑dimensional corner. The theory also provides a natural setting for the study of stratified spaces and for the construction of topological quantum field theories with defects, because the corners encode the junctions of defect lines and surfaces.

Thom Spectra and the Stable Homotopy Category

Every cobordism theory defined by a tangential structure ξ (such as orientation, spin, string, or a general G-structure) is represented by a Thom spectrum Mξ. The Pontryagin–Thom construction gives a natural isomorphism Ωξn(X) ≅ πn(Mξ ∧ X+) for any space X, where ∧ denotes the smash product and X+ is X with a disjoint basepoint. This isomorphism makes the category of Thom spectra a full subcategory of the stable homotopy category SH. In SH, Thom spectra are ring spectra when the structure ξ is multiplicative (e.g., orientation, spin, complex structure). The multiplication Mξ ∧ Mξ → Mξ corresponds to the Cartesian product of manifolds with ξ‑structure. The unit map S → Mξ (where S is the sphere spectrum) classifies the trivial ξ‑manifold. The stable homotopy category provides powerful tools for computing cobordism groups: Bousfield localization with respect to a homology theory (such as Morava K‑theory) isolates chromatic layers of Mξ; the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence computes π*(Mξ) from the Hopf algebroid (MU*, MU*MU); and the chromatic convergence theorem guarantees that the tower of localizations recovers the p‑local homotopy groups. Moreover, the Thom isomorphism in SH states that for a vector bundle V → X with ξ‑structure, the Thom space Th(V) is equivalent to Mξ ∧ X+ in SH. This equivalence underlies the identification of cobordism groups with homotopy groups and explains why the Thom spectrum is the universal recipient for characteristic numbers of ξ‑manifolds.

Worked Example: Computing Ω2Spin via the Adams Spectral Sequence
Use the classical Adams spectral sequence based on the Steenrod algebra at the prime 2 to determine the second spin cobordism group Ω2Spin.
The spin Thom spectrum MSpin is 2‑locally equivalent to the connective cover of KO, denoted ko. The classical Adams spectral sequence for ko has E2-page ExtA(H*ko, F2) where A is the mod‑2 Steenrod algebra. In low degrees the cohomology of ko is known: H0ko = F2, H1ko = 0, H2ko = F2 generated by the Stiefel‑Whitney class w2. The Ext groups in stem 2 are ExtA0,2 = 0 and ExtA1,3 = F2 generated by h1 (the class detecting η). There are no possible differentials for degree reasons, so the spectral sequence collapses at E2. The resulting homotopy group π2(ko) ≅ Z/2, which by the Pontryagin–Thom isomorphism equals Ω2Spin. Hence Ω2Spin ≅ Z/2, generated by the spin structure on the 2‑torus T2 with its unique non‑bounding spin structure.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a low‑dimensional cobordism group, first identify the relevant Thom spectrum (MO, MSO, MSpin, MU, etc.) and recall its connective cover (ko, ku, tmf, …). Then decide whether the classical Adams spectral sequence (Steenrod algebra) or the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (MU*MU) is more efficient. For unoriented and spin cobordism at the prime 2, the classical Adams sequence is usually simpler because the cohomology of MO and MSpin is a free module over the Steenrod algebra on known generators. Write down the E2-page in the required stem, check for possible differentials (often none for degree reasons), and read off the answer. This systematic approach avoids memorising long tables and works for any prime after localising.

Cobordism and Topological K‑Theory

The relationship between cobordism and topological K‑theory is mediated by a series of canonical orientations. The complex cobordism spectrum MU admits a universal complex orientation, which yields a ring map MU → KU (periodic complex K‑theory) sending the universal formal group law to the multiplicative formal group law. On homotopy groups this map is the Todd genus: for a stably complex manifold M, the image of [M] in π*KU ≅ Z[u, u−1] is the Todd genus Td(M) times the Bott element udim M/2. The oriented Thom spectrum MSO maps to KO (real K‑theory) via the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro orientation MSpin → KO, which factors through MSO after inverting 2. This orientation realises the Â‑genus on spin manifolds: the composition Ω*Spin → π*KO ≅ Z[η, α, β]/(2η, η3, …) sends a spin manifold to its Â‑genus times the appropriate power of the Bott element β. The Witten genus provides a further orientation MString → tmf (topological modular forms), which at the level of homotopy groups recovers the elliptic genus. These orientations are not merely algebraic; they are maps of E∞ ring spectra and therefore respect power operations and the full multiplicative structure. Consequently, the kernels of these maps (e.g., the kernel of MSpin → KO) are precisely the cobordism classes of manifolds with vanishing characteristic numbers (Â‑genus, Pontryagin numbers, etc.). The image of the orientation often lands in a computable subring of the target K‑theory, giving effective calculational tools: for instance, the Â‑genus of a spin 8k‑manifold is an integer, and the map Ω8kSpin → Z is surjective, while the kernel is detected by higher KO‑theory invariants such as the α‑invariant.

Low‑Dimensional Cobordism Groups and Their Generators

Explicit knowledge of cobordism groups in low dimensions is essential for concrete computations and for testing general theorems. For unoriented cobordism (Thom spectrum MO) the groups ΩnO are Z/2‑vector spaces with bases given by products of real projective spaces. The first few groups are: Ω0O = Z/2 (point), Ω1O = 0, Ω2O = Z/2 generated by RP2, Ω3O = 0, Ω4O = (Z/2)2 generated by RP4 and RP2 × RP2, Ω5O = (Z/2)2 generated by RP2 × RP3 and RP5. The ring structure is polynomial: Ω*O ≅ Z/2[x2, x4, x5, x6, …] where xi can be taken as the class of RPi for i not a power of 2, together with certain Dold manifolds. For oriented cobordism (MSO) the groups are torsion‑free in low degrees: Ω0SO = Z (point), Ω1SO = Ω2SO = Ω3SO = 0, Ω4SO = Z generated by CP2 (signature 1), Ω5SO = 0, Ω6SO = 0, Ω7SO = 0, Ω8SO = Z2 generated by CP4 and CP2 × CP2. The oriented cobordism ring is polynomial on generators in degrees 4, 8, 12, … (the Milnor hypersurfaces). For spin cobordism (MSpin) the 2‑primary part is more interesting: Ω0Spin = Z, Ω1Spin = Z/2 (the circle with non‑bounding spin structure), Ω2Spin = Z/2 (the 2‑torus with its unique non‑bounding spin structure), Ω3Spin = 0, Ω4Spin = Z generated by the K3 surface (Â‑genus 2), Ω5Spin = 0, Ω6Spin = 0, Ω7Spin = 0, Ω8Spin = Z2 generated by the K3 surface squared and a certain Bott manifold. These low‑dimensional groups are computed via the Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence (for spin) or directly from the Adams spectral sequence for MO and MSO.

The Classical Adams Spectral Sequence for Unoriented and Oriented Cobordism

The classical Adams spectral sequence (based on the mod‑p Steenrod algebra) is a primary tool for computing the homotopy groups of Thom spectra such as MO, MSO, and MSpin at a fixed prime p. For a spectrum E, the spectral sequence has E2-page ExtA(p)s,t(H*E; Fp, Fp) and converges to the p‑local homotopy groups πt−s(E)(p). For MO, the cohomology H*MO is a free module over the Steenrod algebra A on the Thom class u and its Steenrod squares SqIu. Consequently ExtA(H*MO, F2) ≅ ExtA(F2, F2) ⊗ F2[w2, w3, …] where the polynomial generators correspond to the Stiefel‑Whitney classes. This Ext group is exactly the classical Adams E2-page for the sphere spectrum, tensored with a polynomial algebra. The differentials are all zero for degree reasons, so the spectral sequence collapses at E2. The result is that π*MO ≅ π*S ⊗ Ω*O, where π*S are the stable stems. In particular, the unoriented cobordism groups are recovered as the 0‑line of the Adams spectral sequence for MO. For MSO, the cohomology H*MSO is not free over A; it is a quotient of H*MO by the ideal generated by the odd Stiefel‑Whitney classes. The resulting Ext groups are more complicated and the spectral sequence does not collapse. However, in low degrees (up to dimension 7) the Adams spectral sequence for MSO can be computed by hand using the known cohomology of BSO and the Thom isomorphism. The outcome matches the known oriented cobordism groups: π0MSO = Z, π1MSO = π2MSO = π3MSO = 0, π4MSO = Z, etc. For MSpin, the classical Adams spectral sequence at p = 2 is equivalent to the Adams spectral sequence for the connective real K‑theory spectrum ko, because MSpin is 2‑locally equivalent to ko. The E2-page is ExtA(H*ko, F2) and the differentials are known to be generated by the action of the Steenrod algebra on the cohomology of ko. This spectral sequence computes the spin cobordism groups and reveals the 2‑primary torsion (e.g., the Z/2 in dimensions 1 and 2) as well as the free part detected by the Â‑genus.

  • Confusing the Baas–Sullivan cobordism of manifolds with corners with the ordinary cobordism of the underlying manifold; the corner data contributes additional polynomial generators that are not present in the classical theory.
  • Assuming that every Thom spectrum is a ring spectrum in the stable homotopy category; only multiplicative tangential structures (orientation, spin, complex, etc.) yield E∞ ring structures.
  • Treating the map MU → KU as an isomorphism on homotopy groups; it is surjective onto the image of the Todd genus but has a large kernel consisting of manifolds with vanishing Todd genus.
  • Using the classical Adams spectral sequence for MSO without accounting for the non‑free A‑module structure of H*MSO; this leads to incorrect E2-pages and missing differentials.
  • Believing that the low‑dimensional spin cobordism groups are entirely determined by the Â‑genus; the Z/2 summands in dimensions 1 and 2 are not detected by the Â‑genus and require the Adams spectral sequence or the Anderson–Brown–Peterson sequence to see.
  • Overlooking that the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for a cobordism spectrum uses the Hopf algebroid (MU*, MU*MU) and not merely the ring MU*; the comodule structure on MU*Mξ is essential for differentials.
  • Assuming the Witten genus MString → tmf is an equivalence after 2‑completion; it induces an isomorphism on homotopy groups only after inverting the discriminant, and the kernel and cokernel contain important torsion information.

The Landweber Exact Functor Theorem and Complex Cobordism

The Landweber Exact Functor Theorem (LEFT) provides a systematic method for constructing homology theories from the complex cobordism spectrum MU. Given a graded ring homomorphism φ: MU* → R that makes R a Landweber exact MU*‑module, the functor X ↦ MU*(X) ⊗MU* R defines a homology theory represented by a spectrum E with coefficient ring R. The exactness condition requires that for each prime p the sequence (p, v₁, v₂, …) be regular on R, where v_i are the Hazewinkel generators of MU* after p‑typicalization. When the condition holds, the resulting spectrum E inherits a complex orientation and its formal group law is the pushforward of the universal formal group law over MU* along φ. Classical examples include the complex K‑theory spectrum KU (φ sends the universal formal group law to the multiplicative one), the Brown–Peterson spectrum BP (φ kills all v_i for i > n), and the Morava K‑theories K(n) (φ kills p, v₁,…,vn‑1 and inverts v_n). The theorem also guarantees that the Adams–Novikov E₂‑term for E is ExtMU*MU(MU*, R), which is computable via the cobar complex. In practice, LEFT is the bridge that turns algebraic data — a formal group law over a ring — into a genuine stable homotopy type, and it underlies the construction of elliptic cohomology theories such as tmf.

Morava K‑Theories and the Chromatic Filtration of MU

Morava K‑theories K(n) (for a fixed prime p and height n ≥ 1) are the fields of the stable homotopy category in the sense of the thick subcategory theorem. Their coefficient rings are K(n)* ≅ 𝔽_p[v_n±1] with |v_n| = 2(pⁿ−1). Each K(n) is a complex oriented ring spectrum whose formal group law has height exactly n. The Bousfield localization of MU with respect to K(n) yields the n‑th chromatic layer LK(n)MU, and the chromatic tower MU → LK(1)MU → LK(2)MU → … converges to the p‑completion of MU. The filtration reflects the stratification of the moduli stack of formal groups Mfg by height: the generic point corresponds to height 0 (rational cobordism), the closed points of height 1 give the p‑typical part detected by K(1) (essentially p‑adic K‑theory), height 2 points are detected by K(2) (related to elliptic curves), and so on. The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for MU filtered by the K(n)‑localizations has E₂‑term ExtMU*MU(MU*, MU*) which decomposes as a direct sum of the cohomology of the Morava stabilizer groups S_n with coefficients in the Lubin–Tate ring. This decomposition is the algebraic incarnation of the chromatic convergence theorem and explains why complex cobordism sees all heights simultaneously. Computationally, the K(n)‑local sphere is built from the Morava E‑theory spectrum E_n (with coefficients W(𝔽pⁿ)[[u₁,…,un‑1]][u±1]) by taking homotopy fixed points under the extended Morava stabilizer group. The resulting homotopy groups π_*LK(n)S are the target of the chromatic approach to the stable stems.

Worked Example: Verifying Landweber Exactness for KU
Show that the map φ: MU* → ℤ sending the universal formal group law to the multiplicative formal group law makes ℤ a Landweber exact MU*‑module, and identify the resulting spectrum.
The multiplicative formal group law over ℤ is F(x,y)=x+y−xy. Its p‑typical logarithm is ℓ(x)=∑k≥1 xp^k/p, so the Hazewinkel generators satisfy v₁ = p, v_i = 0 for i>1. The sequence (p, v₁, v₂, …) becomes (p, p, 0, 0, …). Regularity of this sequence on ℤ means multiplication by p is injective (true) and multiplication by p on ℤ/p is injective (vacuously true because the module is zero). Hence ℤ is Landweber exact. By LEFT the associated homology theory is X ↦ MU*(X) ⊗MU* ℤ, which is represented by the complex K‑theory spectrum KU. The formal group law of KU is exactly the multiplicative one, confirming the construction.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks to identify a spectrum from a formal group law, first write down the p‑typical logarithm, read off the Hazewinkel generators, and test the regularity condition. If the sequence is regular, invoke LEFT; the resulting spectrum is the unique (up to equivalence) complex oriented theory with that formal group law. Memorize the standard examples: multiplicative → KU, additive → Hℚ, height n Honda → K(n), universal → MU.

The Nilpotence and Periodicity Theorems in Cobordism

The Nilpotence Theorem of Devinatz, Hopkins, and Smith states that a map f: Σ^k X → X of finite spectra is nilpotent (some iterate is null) if and only if its image under the Morava K‑theory functor K(n) is zero for all n and all primes p. Applied to the stable homotopy groups of spheres, this implies that any element of positive degree in π_*S that is detected by complex cobordism MU is nilpotent unless it survives to some K(n). The Periodicity Theorem complements this by asserting that for each n there exists a v_n‑self map on a finite type n spectrum, giving rise to v_n‑periodic families in the stable stems. In the context of cobordism, the nilpotence theorem shows that the kernel of the Hurewicz map MU* → π_*S consists entirely of nilpotent elements, while the periodicity theorem guarantees that the v_n‑periodic part of π_*S is captured by the K(n)‑localizations of MU. Consequently, the chromatic filtration of MU reflects the decomposition of the stable homotopy category into nilpotent and periodic layers. This perspective is essential for understanding why the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence based on MU*MU converges to the p‑completion of the stable stems and why the E₂‑term can be analyzed height by height.

Cobordism and the Hurewicz Image in Stable Homotopy

The Hurewicz homomorphism h: π_*S → MU* sends a stable homotopy class represented by a map S^k → S0 to the cobordism class of the corresponding framed manifold. Its image consists precisely of those cobordism classes that admit a stable framing, i.e. manifolds with trivialized stable normal bundle. By the Pontryagin–Thom construction, π_*S ≅ Ω_*fr, the framed cobordism ring. The map h factors as π_*S → Ω_*fr → Ω_*^U = MU*, where the second arrow forgets the framing. The kernel of h is the ideal of framed manifolds that bound a framed manifold; the cokernel measures the obstruction to framing a complex manifold. Classical results of Kervaire and Milnor show that in low dimensions the Hurewicz image is generated by the Hopf invariant one elements (η in degree 1, ν in degree 3, σ in degree 7) and the Kervaire invariant elements (θ_j in degree 2j+1−2). The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for MU computes the Hurewicz image via the 0‑line ExtMU*MU0(MU*, MU*) = MU*, and the differentials encode the framing obstructions. In particular, the image of the J‑homomorphism appears as the 1‑line of the ANSS, and the detection of exotic spheres by the Kervaire invariant is reflected in the non‑triviality of certain v_n‑periodic families.

The Quillen Equivalence Between MU and Formal Group Laws

Quillen's theorem identifies the coefficient ring MU* with the Lazard ring L, the universal ring classifying one‑dimensional commutative formal group laws. The isomorphism L ≅ MU* is constructed by sending the universal formal group law over L to the formal group law arising from the complex orientation of MU. This equivalence is not merely algebraic: it lifts to an equivalence of ∞‑categories between the ∞‑category of MU‑modules and the ∞‑category of comodules over the Hopf algebroid (MU*, MU*MU). Under this equivalence, a complex oriented cohomology theory E corresponds to a formal group law over E* together with a compatible comodule structure. The Landweber Exact Functor Theorem is the statement that the forgetful functor from MU‑modules to formal group laws admits a left adjoint on the subcategory of Landweber exact objects. Moreover, the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for an MU‑module M is the derived functor spectral sequence for the homotopy groups of the corresponding comodule. This categorical viewpoint explains why the chromatic filtration of MU corresponds to the stratification of the moduli stack of formal groups by height, and why Morava K‑theories appear as the residue fields of that stack. The Quillen equivalence thus provides the conceptual backbone for the entire modern approach to stable homotopy theory via formal geometry.

  • Confusing the Landweber exactness condition with flatness; exactness requires regularity of the sequence (p, v₁, v₂, …) on the target ring, not merely flatness over MU*.
  • Assuming every formal group law yields a spectrum; only Landweber exact ones do, and non‑exact laws (e.g. the additive law over ℤ) fail to produce a homology theory via LEFT.
  • Treating the chromatic tower as a tower of spectra mapping to MU rather than a tower of Bousfield localizations of MU; the maps go MU → LK(1)MU → LK(2)MU → …, not the reverse.
  • Believing the nilpotence theorem implies all positive‑degree elements of π_*S are nilpotent; it only says elements invisible to all K(n) are nilpotent, while v_n‑periodic families survive.
  • Identifying the Hurewicz image with the whole of MU*; the image is the framed cobordism ring, a proper subring detected by the 0‑line of the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence.
  • Overlooking that the Quillen isomorphism L ≅ MU* is graded; the Lazard ring is concentrated in even degrees, matching the even periodicity of MU*.
  • Assuming the Adams–Novikov E₂‑term for MU is simply ExtMU*(MU*, MU*); it is Ext over the Hopf algebroid (MU*, MU*MU), which encodes the comodule structure essential for differentials.

Cobordism and Elliptic Cohomology: The Witten Genus and the String Orientation of TMF

The Witten genus is a genus on string manifolds — manifolds equipped with a lift of the structure group to the 3‑connected cover String(n) of Spin(n). It takes values in the ring of integral modular forms and is defined as the index of the Dirac operator on the free loop space of the manifold. The genus vanishes on boundaries of string manifolds, so it defines a ring homomorphism Ω_*String → MF^*(SL2(ℤ)). The topological refinement of this genus is the sigma orientation, a map of ring spectra MString → tmf (the connective spectrum of topological modular forms) constructed by Ando, Hopkins, and Strickland. The sigma orientation lifts the Witten genus to a map of cohomology theories; on homotopy groups it recovers the q‑expansion of the Witten genus. The non‑connective version TMF is obtained from tmf by inverting the discriminant Δ, and the string orientation extends to MString → TMF. This orientation is central to the chromatic picture at height 2: the Morava E‑theory E2 at a supersingular elliptic curve receives a map from TMF, and the Witten genus detects the v2‑periodic families in the stable stems. The image of the Witten genus in modular forms is precisely the subring of modular forms with integral q‑expansion that satisfy the congruences of the Ochanine genus. The construction uses the compactified moduli stack of elliptic curves M̄_ell and the sheaf of E_∞ ring spectra O^top; the global sections give tmf. The string orientation is then a map of E_∞ ring spectra MString → O^top(M̄_ell). This geometric model explains why the Witten genus is a cobordism invariant and why it factors through the string cobordism ring.

Cobordism of Manifolds with Pin and Pin^c Structures

Pin and Pin^c structures are lifts of the orthogonal structure group O(n) to the groups Pin+(n), Pin-(n), and Pin^c(n) = (Pin+(n) × U(1))/ℤ₂. They are classified by the vanishing of w₂ + w₁² (Pin+), w₂ (Pin-), and w₂ + w₁² + c₁ mod 2 (Pin^c). The corresponding Thom spectra are MPin+, MPin-, and MPin^c. Their cobordism rings Ω_*Pin+, Ω_*Pin-, Ω_*Pin^c have been computed in low degrees using the Adams spectral sequence and the Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence. For example, Ω1Pin+ ≅ ℤ₂ (generated by the circle with the non‑bounding Pin+ structure), Ω2Pin+ ≅ ℤ₂ (the Arf invariant surface), and Ω3Pin+ ≅ ℤ₈ (detected by the η invariant). Pin- cobordism differs in degree 2: Ω2Pin- ≅ ℤ₂ ⊕ ℤ₂, reflecting the two distinct Pin- structures on the torus. Pin^c cobordism is closely related to spin^c cobordism; there is a long exact sequence relating Ω_*Pin^c, Ω_*Spin^c, and Ω_*SO via the forgetful and determinant maps. These groups appear in the classification of symmetry‑protected topological phases of fermionic systems, where the Pin+ structure corresponds to time‑reversal symmetry with T² = +1 and Pin- to T² = -1. The Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro orientation gives maps MPin^± → KO and MPin^c → KU, linking these cobordism theories to real and complex K‑theory.

Technique: Using the sigma orientation to compute the Witten genus
Why does the Witten genus vanish on a string manifold that bounds a string manifold?
The Witten genus is defined as the index of the Dirac operator on the free loop space L M. If M = ∂W with W a string manifold, the loop space L M bounds the loop space L W. The Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index theorem for manifolds with boundary implies that the index of the Dirac operator on L M equals the spectral flow of the family of Dirac operators on the fibres of L W → L M, which vanishes because the family is defined on a compact manifold without boundary. Equivalently, the sigma orientation MString → tmf is a map of ring spectra; on homotopy groups it sends the cobordism class of a bounding string manifold to zero in tmf_*, and the q‑expansion of tmf_* recovers the Witten genus. Hence the Witten genus of any string boundary is zero.
Exam‑day strategy
Memorise the three key Pin groups by their characteristic class obstructions: Pin+ (w₂ + w₁² = 0), Pin- (w₂ = 0), Pin^c (w₂ + w₁² + c₁ ≡ 0 mod 2). For the Witten genus, remember the chain: string manifold → sigma orientation → tmf → q‑expansion = Witten genus. The vanishing on boundaries follows from the fact that the sigma orientation is a ring map and tmf is a homology theory.

Cobordism and the Telescope Conjecture in Chromatic Homotopy Theory

The telescope conjecture, formulated by Ravenel, asserts that for a finite spectrum X of type n (i.e. K(n-1)_*X = 0 but K(n)_*X ≠ 0), the Bousfield localization LT(v_n)X with respect to the telescope of a v_n self‑map v_n: Σ^d X → X agrees with the K(n)-localization LK(n)X. In the context of cobordism, the conjecture governs how the v_n‑periodic part of the stable homotopy groups of spheres — detected by the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence based on MU — is captured by the telescope of a v_n self‑map on a finite type‑n complex. The nilpotence theorem of Devinatz–Hopkins–Smith shows that any map between finite spectra which is zero on all Morava K‑theories is nilpotent; the telescope conjecture would imply that the v_n‑periodic families are exactly the homotopy classes detected by the telescope. For n = 1 the conjecture is a theorem of Miller (using the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for BP⟨1⟩). For n ≥ 2 it remains open; a counterexample would produce a new kind of periodicity not seen by Morava K‑theories. The conjecture is equivalent to the smashing conjecture for the localization functor LK(n) on the category of finite spectra. Its resolution would clarify the structure of the chromatic tower MU → LK(1)MU → LK(2)MU → … and the relationship between the telescope of a v_n self‑map and the K(n)-local sphere.

Cobordism of Manifolds with Spin^c Structures and the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro Orientation

A Spin^c structure on an n‑manifold is a lift of the structure group to Spin^c(n) = (Spin(n) × U(1))/ℤ₂. The obstruction is the integral class W₃ = β(w₂) ∈ H³(M; ℤ), where β is the Bockstein. The Thom spectrum MSpin^c carries a canonical orientation into complex K‑theory: the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro map MSpin^c → KU is a ring spectrum map that on homotopy groups gives the Spin^c cobordism ring Ω_*Spin^c ≅ ℤ[ℂP², ℂP⁴, …] (polynomial on even‑dimensional complex projective spaces). The forgetful map Ω_*Spin^c → Ω_*SO is surjective in even degrees and its kernel is generated by the classes of ℂP2k with the canonical Spin^c structure. The determinant line bundle of a Spin^c structure provides a map Ω_*Spin^c → Ω_*U that is injective after tensoring with ℚ. The index theorem for the Dirac operator coupled to a line bundle L shows that the Spin^c Dirac index equals the evaluation of the Â‑genus twisted by ec₁(L)/2. This formula underlies the Seiberg–Witten invariants of 4‑manifolds, where the Spin^c structures are the basic data. The Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence computes Ω_*Spin^c from the known Ω_*SO and the action of the Steenrod algebra on the Thom class.

Cobordism and the Surgery Exact Sequence in High‑Dimensional Manifold Classification

The surgery exact sequence of Browder, Novikov, Sullivan, and Wall relates the structure set S(M) of a closed manifold M (homotopy equivalences to M modulo cobordism) to the normal invariants [M, G/O] and the algebraic L‑groups L_*(ℤ[π₁M]). The sequence runs … → Ln+1(ℤ[π]) → S(M) → [M, G/O] → L_n(ℤ[π]) → … . The normal invariant map sends a degree‑one normal map (N, f) → M to the stable normal bundle data in [M, G/O]; its image is the set of normal invariants that can be realized by a manifold. The surgery obstruction map θ: [M, G/O] → L_n(ℤ[π]) measures whether a normal invariant can be killed by surgery. Cobordism enters because the L‑groups are defined as cobordism groups of quadratic Poincaré complexes, and the assembly map A: H_*(Bπ; L(ℤ)) → L_*(ℤ[π]) is a map from the homology of the classifying space with coefficients in the L‑theory spectrum. The Farrell–Jones conjecture (proved for many groups) identifies this assembly map with an isomorphism, linking the topological surgery exact sequence to the algebraic K‑ and L‑theory of the group ring. In dimension ≥ 5, the exact sequence classifies manifolds up to h‑cobordism; combined with the s‑cobordism theorem it yields a classification up to homeomorphism or diffeomorphism. The role of cobordism is thus twofold: the normal invariants live in a cobordism group of maps to G/O, and the obstructions live in the L‑groups which are themselves cobordism groups of algebraic objects.

  • Confusing the string orientation of tmf with the Witten genus itself; the orientation is a lift to a spectrum, the genus is its q‑expansion.
  • Assuming Pin+ and Pin- cobordism agree in all degrees; they differ already in degree 2 because w₁² distinguishes them.
  • Believing the telescope conjecture is proved for all heights; it is only known for n = 1 and remains open for n ≥ 2.
  • Thinking Spin^c cobordism is the same as complex cobordism; the forgetful map to Ω_*U is not an isomorphism, though it becomes one after rationalisation.
  • Treating the surgery exact sequence as a short exact sequence; it is a long exact sequence of pointed sets, and the maps are not all group homomorphisms.
  • Overlooking that the L‑groups in the surgery sequence are defined using quadratic forms over ℤ[π], not just ordinary cobordism groups of manifolds.
  • Neglecting the role of the assembly map in identifying the algebraic L‑groups with the topological surgery obstructions; this identification is the content of the Farrell–Jones conjecture.

Cobordism and the Brown–Peterson Spectrum BP

The Brown–Peterson spectrum BP is a ring spectrum that captures the p‑local part of complex cobordism for a fixed prime p. Its coefficient ring is BP_* ≅ ℤ_(p)[v₁, v₂, …] where |v_i| = 2(p^i−1). The map MU_(p) → BP is a ring spectrum map that splits the p‑local complex cobordism spectrum into a wedge of suspensions of BP. This splitting is the content of the Landweber filtration theorem and underlies the chromatic approach to stable homotopy theory. The generators v_i correspond to the Hazewinkel or Araki generators of the universal p‑typical formal group law. The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for BP has E₂‑term ExtBP_*BP(BP_*, BP_*) which computes the p‑local stable homotopy groups of spheres. The cobordism groups Ω_*^U ⊗ ℤ_(p) are isomorphic to BP_* ⊗BP_* MU_* via the Landweber exact functor theorem. In practice, BP is used to isolate v_n‑periodic phenomena: the telescope of a v_n self‑map on a finite spectrum yields the K(n)‑local sphere, and the chromatic tower MU → LK(1)MU → LK(2)MU → … is built from the Bousfield localizations of BP. The spectrum BP also carries a canonical orientation for p‑typical formal group laws, making it the universal recipient for p‑typical genera. Computations of BP_*BP and the associated Hopf algebroid are central to the algebraic topology of the 1970s and 1980s; they provide the algebraic input for the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence and for the construction of Morava K‑theories K(n) and Morava E‑theories E_n.

Cobordism and the Adams–Novikov E₂ Term and the Beta Family

The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (ANSS) is a first‑quadrant spectral sequence with E₂s,t = Exts,tBP_*BP(BP_*, BP_*) converging to the p‑local stable homotopy groups πt−s^S ⊗ ℤ_(p). The cobordism ring MU_* maps to BP_* via the Quillen idempotent, and the ANSS for MU is isomorphic to the ANSS for BP after p‑localization. The E₂ term is computed using the cobar complex of the Hopf algebroid (BP_*, BP_*BP). The resulting Ext groups are organized by chromatic height: the 1‑line corresponds to the image of J and the α family, the 2‑line contains the β family (generated by β_i ∈ Ext2,2p^i(p−1)), and higher lines contain the Greek letter elements γ, δ, … . The β family elements β_i are permanent cycles for p ≥ 3 and detect the v1‑periodic homotopy classes; they map to the image of the J‑homomorphism after applying the Hurewicz map from MU to the sphere.
