The differentials in the ANSS are deeply related to the structure of the Morava stabilizer group and the cohomology of the Lubin–Tate deformation space. The Nilpotence Theorem of Devinatz–Hopkins–Smith implies that every element in positive filtration is nilpotent, and the Periodicity Theorem guarantees v_n‑self maps on finite spectra. The β family is the first chromatic layer beyond the image of J; its detection by the ANSS is a cornerstone of chromatic homotopy theory. In cobordism terms, the β family corresponds to certain elements in MU_* that survive the Hurewicz map to the sphere, and their geometric representatives are often given by manifolds with special SU or Spin structures.

Worked Example: Detecting β₁ in the ANSS at p = 3
Show that the element β₁ ∈ Ext2,12BP_*BP(BP_*, BP_*) at the prime 3 corresponds to a non‑trivial element in π10^S ⊗ ℤ_(3) and explain its relation to the image of J.
At p = 3, the generator v₁ has degree 4. The element β₁ is represented in the cobar complex by the class [t₁|t₁] where t₁ is the standard generator of BP_*BP. Its internal degree is 2·|t₁| = 2·4 = 8, but the Adams–Novikov grading shifts by the weight of v₁; the actual topological degree is t−s = (8+4)−2 = 10. The differential d₃(β₁) = 0 because there is no possible target in the E₃ page. Hence β₁ survives to E_∞ and detects an element of order 3 in π10^S. The image of J in degree 10 at p = 3 is cyclic of order 3, generated by the class J(α₁) where α₁ ∈ π3(SO) ≅ ℤ. The Hurewicz map MU → S sends the cobordism class of ℂP² (which represents v₁) to the generator of the image of J. The β₁ element is precisely the ANSS avatar of this image‑of‑J class; its detection by the ANSS confirms that the β family at height 1 coincides with the image of J after p‑localization.
Exam Strategy
When asked about the β family, remember: β_i lives in Ext2,2p^i(p−1) and detects the v1‑periodic part of the stable stems. For p = 2 the β family merges with the α family; for p ≥ 3 it is distinct and permanent. Relate β_i to the image of J via the Hurewicz map MU → S and the fact that MU_* → π_*^S factors through the ANSS. In multiple‑choice questions, the β family is often the correct answer for "first chromatic layer beyond the image of J".

Cobordism and the Image of J in Stable Homotopy

The image of the J‑homomorphism J: π_k(O) → π_k^S is a classical object in stable homotopy theory. Adams computed its order using the Adams spectral sequence; the result is that the p‑primary component of im J in degree 4m−1 is cyclic of order pν_p(m)+1 for odd primes, and at p = 2 the order is 2ν2(m)+2 (with a correction in degrees 8k+1,2). In cobordism terms, the Hurewicz map h: MU_* → π_*^S factors through the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence. The image of J corresponds exactly to the 1‑line of the ANSS, i.e., the Ext1,* groups. These Ext groups are generated by the classes α_i (or β_i at height 1) which are detected by the v1‑periodic part of MU. The map MU → BP → K(1) (the first Morava K‑theory) induces an isomorphism on the v1‑periodic homotopy groups, and the K(1)‑local sphere is equivalent to the p‑completion of the image of J spectrum. The Adams conjecture, proved by Quillen and Sullivan, states that the map BU → BGL₁(S) factors through the J‑homomorphism after p‑completion; this is reflected in the fact that the complex cobordism ring MU_* maps onto the coefficient ring of the image of J spectrum. Geometrically, the generators of im J in dimensions 4m−1 are represented by the Milnor spheres or by the boundaries of parallelizable manifolds; their cobordism classes in Ω_*fr map to the stable stems via the Pontryagin–Thom construction. The relationship between the image of J and the β family in the ANSS is a key example of how cobordism theory organizes the chromatic filtration of the stable homotopy category.

Cobordism and the Stolz–Teichner Program for Supersymmetric TQFTs

The Stolz–Teichner program proposes a geometric model for elliptic cohomology and topological modular forms (tmf) using 2‑dimensional supersymmetric Euclidean field theories. In this framework, a (1|1)‑dimensional Euclidean field theory is a symmetric monoidal functor from the category of (1|1)‑dimensional super‑cobordisms to the category of topological vector spaces. The partition function of such a theory is a modular form, and the space of deformation classes of theories is conjectured to be the tmf spectrum. Cobordism enters because the domain category is built from super‑manifolds with a (1|1)‑dimensional Euclidean structure; the morphisms are super‑cobordisms. The program extends to (2|1)‑dimensional theories, where the partition function is an elliptic genus and the target is tmf. The string orientation of tmf, constructed by Ando–Hopkins–Rezk, is realized by the (2|1)‑dimensional supersymmetric sigma model with target a string manifold. The cobordism hypothesis for (∞,n)‑categories provides the higher‑categorical foundation: the space of fully extended TQFTs with values in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category is the space of maps from the (∞,n)‑category of cobordisms. Stolz and Teichner's conjecture identifies the (2|1)‑dimensional Euclidean cobordism category with a geometric model for tmf. Recent work by Dumitrescu, Hohnhold, Kreck, and others has constructed a bordism category of super‑manifolds with a spin structure and a map to a target, whose homotopy type recovers the Anderson dual of tmf. This program connects cobordism, index theory, and quantum field theory, and it provides a geometric interpretation of the Witten genus as the partition function of a supersymmetric theory.

Cobordism and the Freed–Hopkins Classification of Symmetry‑Protected Topological Phases

Freed and Hopkins (2016) gave a classification of symmetry‑protected topological (SPT) phases of fermionic and bosonic systems in terms of cobordism groups of manifolds with a tangential structure that encodes the symmetry group and its action on spacetime. For a finite group G and a degree‑n characteristic class ω ∈ H^n(BG; U(1)), the SPT phases in d spatial dimensions are classified by the cobordism group Ωd+1Spin(BG; ω) (or Ωd+1SO, Ωd+1Pin^± depending on the symmetry type). The classification uses the invertible topological field theories (iTQFTs) which are maps from the cobordism category to the category of complex lines. The cobordism hypothesis identifies iTQFTs with the Anderson dual of the relevant cobordism spectrum. The Freed–Hopkins theorem states that the group of deformation classes of reflection‑positive invertible TQFTs in dimension d+1 is isomorphic to the torsion subgroup of Ωd+1^G, where G is the appropriate structure group (Spin, Pin+, Pin^−, etc.). The anomaly inflow mechanism is captured by the fact that the boundary of a (d+1)‑dimensional SPT phase carries a d‑dimensional theory with a ’t Hooft anomaly classified by the same cobordism group. This framework has been applied to the classification of topological insulators and superconductors (the "tenfold way"), where the symmetry group includes time‑reversal and charge‑conjugation symmetries, and the relevant cobordism groups are those of Pin^± or Spin^c manifolds. The computation of these groups uses the Adams spectral sequence for the corresponding Thom spectra (MPin^±, MSpin^c) and the Anderson dual of the sphere spectrum. The result matches the periodic table of topological insulators and extends it to interacting fermionic systems.

  • Confusing the Brown–Peterson spectrum BP with the Morava K‑theory K(n); BP is a ring spectrum with coefficients ℤ_(p)[v₁,v₂,…] while K(n) has coefficients 𝔽_p[v_n±1].
  • Assuming the β family in the ANSS is the same as the α family at all primes; at p = 2 they merge, but for p ≥ 3 they are distinct and β_i detects v1‑periodic elements.
  • Thinking the image of J is the entire 1‑line of the ANSS; the 1‑line also contains the α family at p = 2, and the β family at odd primes corresponds to the image of J after p‑localization.
  • Believing the Stolz–Teichner program is a theorem; it remains a conjecture that the (2|1)‑dimensional Euclidean cobordism category models tmf, though substantial progress has been made.
  • Overlooking that the Freed–Hopkins classification uses the Anderson dual of the cobordism spectrum, not the cobordism groups themselves; the SPT phases correspond to the torsion subgroup of the Anderson dual.
  • Neglecting the role of the Adams conjecture in relating the J‑homomorphism to complex cobordism; the conjecture (now theorem) is essential for identifying the image of J with the v1‑periodic part of MU.
  • Treating the β family as permanent cycles in the ANSS for all primes; at p = 2 the β family supports non‑trivial differentials (e.g., d₃(β₁) = α₁³) and does not survive to E_∞ in the same way.

The h‑Cobordism Theorem and the s‑Cobordism Theorem

The h‑cobordism theorem, proved by Stephen Smale in 1961 for dimensions ≥ 5, states that if a cobordism (W; M, N) between simply‑connected manifolds M and N has the property that the inclusions M W and N W are homotopy equivalences, then W is diffeomorphic to M × [0,1]. In particular M and N are diffeomorphic. The theorem fails in dimension 4 (where the smooth Poincaré conjecture is still open) and in the topological category for dimension 4 (Freedman’s work shows it holds topologically). The proof uses the Whitney trick to cancel handles of complementary index, which requires dimension at least 5 to avoid intersections of Whitney disks.

The s‑cobordism theorem, developed by Barden, Mazur, and Stallings in the mid‑1960s, refines the h‑cobordism theorem by introducing the Whitehead torsion τ(W, M) ∈ Wh(π₁M). For a cobordism (W; M, N) with π₁M = π₁N = π, the inclusion M W is a homotopy equivalence if and only if τ(W, M) = 0. When the torsion vanishes, W is diffeomorphic to M × [0,1] relative to M. The Whitehead group Wh(π) is the cokernel of the map K₁(ℤ) → K₁(ℤ[π]) induced by the group ring. For finite groups, Wh(π) is often non‑trivial (e.g., Wh(ℤ/5) ≅ ℤ⁴), giving exotic h‑cobordisms that are not products. The s‑cobordism theorem is the foundation of the surgery exact sequence: the structure set 𝒮(M) of homotopy equivalences to M modulo h‑cobordism is in bijection with the set of normal invariants modulo the action of the surgery obstruction groups Lₙ(ℤ[π]).

In the smooth category, the h‑cobordism theorem holds for n ≥ 5; in the PL category it holds for n ≥ 5 as well (by the work of Kirby–Siebenmann). In the topological category, Freedman proved the 4‑dimensional topological h‑cobordism theorem for simply‑connected manifolds, and Quinn extended it to fundamental groups of subexponential growth. The s‑cobordism theorem in the topological category was proved by Chapman (1974) using controlled topology. The failure of the smooth h‑cobordism theorem in dimension 4 is tied to the existence of exotic ℝ⁴’s and the unsolved smooth 4‑dimensional Poincaré conjecture. The h‑cobordism theorem also has a relative version for manifolds with boundary, where one requires the boundary to be a product.

Applications include the classification of high‑dimensional manifolds up to diffeomorphism: if two simply‑connected manifolds are homotopy equivalent, they are h‑cobordant, hence diffeomorphic. For non‑simply‑connected manifolds, the s‑cobordism theorem reduces the classification to the computation of Whitehead torsion and surgery obstructions. The theorem also underlies the proof of the generalized Poincaré conjecture in dimensions ≥ 5 (Smale, Stallings, Zeeman). In the context of cobordism theory, the h‑cobordism theorem implies that the h‑cobordism classes of simply‑connected manifolds form a group isomorphic to the oriented cobordism group ΩₙSO for n ≥ 5, because any oriented cobordism can be surgered to an h‑cobordism.

The Novikov Conjecture, the Borel Conjecture, and Cobordism

The Novikov conjecture (1965) asserts that the higher signatures of a closed oriented manifold M are homotopy invariants. For a map f: M → Bπ classifying the universal cover, the higher signature associated to a cohomology class x ∈ H*(Bπ; ℚ) is ⟨L(M) ∪ f* x, [M]⟩, where L(M) is the Hirzebruch L‑class. Novikov conjectured that this number depends only on the homotopy type of M. The conjecture is known for many groups: hyperbolic groups (Kasparov, 1988), amenable groups (Connes–Moscovici, 1990), and more generally for groups satisfying the Baum–Connes conjecture with coefficients. The proof uses the assembly map in K‑theory of group C*‑algebras and the Mishchenko–Fomenko higher index theorem.

The Borel conjecture (1950s) states that any homotopy equivalence between closed aspherical manifolds is homotopic to a homeomorphism. Equivalently, the structure set 𝒮(M) is trivial for aspherical M. This is a topological rigidity statement. The Borel conjecture implies the Novikov conjecture for aspherical manifolds, because homotopy invariance of higher signatures follows from topological rigidity. The Borel conjecture is known for fundamental groups that are hyperbolic (Bartels–Lück–Weinberger, 2010), CAT(0) groups (Bartels–Lück, 2012), and more generally for groups satisfying the Farrell–Jones conjecture in algebraic K‑ and L‑theory. The Farrell–Jones conjecture predicts that the assembly map Hₙ^G(EVCG; 𝕂ℤ) → Kₙ(ℤ[G]) is an isomorphism, where EVCG is the classifying space for virtually cyclic subgroups.

Cobordism enters through the surgery exact sequence. For a manifold M with fundamental group π, the structure set 𝒮(M) fits into an exact sequence … → Lₙ₊₁(ℤ[π]) → 𝒮(M) → [M, G/O] → Lₙ(ℤ[π]). The Novikov conjecture is equivalent to the rational injectivity of the assembly map Hₙ(Bπ; ℚ) → Lₙ(ℤ[π]) ⊗ ℚ. The Borel conjecture is equivalent to the vanishing of 𝒮(M) for aspherical M. Cobordism groups of manifolds with a map to Bπ, denoted ΩₙSO(Bπ), map to the normal invariants [M, G/O] via the Pontryagin–Thom construction. The surgery obstruction map lands in the L‑groups, which are the cobordism groups of quadratic Poincaré complexes. The work of Ranicki (1992) identifies the algebraic surgery exact sequence with the geometric one, making the L‑groups the home of surgery obstructions.

Recent progress uses the theory of controlled topology and the classification of manifolds up to homotopy equivalence. The Cappell–Weinberger theorem (1990) shows that for groups with finite classifying space, the Novikov conjecture is equivalent to the rational injectivity of the assembly map in K‑theory. The work of Higson–Kasparov (2001) on the Baum–Connes conjecture for a‑T‑menable groups gives the Novikov conjecture for those groups. The Borel conjecture for hyperbolic groups was proved by Bartels–Lück–Weinberger using the Farrell–Jones conjecture and the fact that hyperbolic groups act properly on finite‑dimensional CAT(0) spaces. The connection to cobordism is that the L‑groups are the cobordism groups of symmetric and quadratic forms over ℤ[π], and the assembly map is induced by the map from the topological cobordism spectrum to the algebraic L‑theory spectrum.

Worked Example: Higher Signature Invariance
Let M be a closed oriented 4k‑manifold with fundamental group π = ℤ. Let f: M → S¹ classify the universal cover. Take x ∈ H¹(S¹; ℚ) the generator. Show that the higher signature ⟨L(M) ∪ f* x, [M]⟩ is a homotopy invariant.
The higher signature is ⟨L(M) ∪ f* x, [M]⟩. Since π = ℤ, Bπ = S¹. The assembly map A: Hₙ(S¹; ℚ) → Lₙ(ℤ[ℤ]) ⊗ ℚ is known to be injective for all n (ℤ is a‑T‑menable, so Baum–Connes holds). The higher signature corresponds to the image of the fundamental class [M] under the composition M → Bπ → L•(ℤ[π]) ⊗ ℚ. By the Novikov conjecture for ℤ (proved by Kasparov), this number is invariant under orientation‑preserving homotopy equivalences. Concretely, if h: M → N is a homotopy equivalence, then f ∘ h is homotopic to the classifying map of N, and the naturality of the L‑class gives L(M) = h* L(N). Then ⟨L(M) ∪ f* x, [M]⟩ = ⟨h* L(N) ∪ h* (f_N)* x, [M]⟩ = ⟨L(N) ∪ (f_N)* x, h*[M]⟩ = ⟨L(N) ∪ (f_N)* x, [N]⟩ because h*[M] = [N] for degree‑1 maps. Hence the higher signature is a homotopy invariant.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks about the Novikov or Borel conjecture, identify the group π and recall which class of groups the conjecture is known for (hyperbolic, CAT(0), amenable, a‑T‑menable). The surgery exact sequence is the standard tool: the structure set 𝒮(M) maps to normal invariants [M, G/O] and the surgery obstruction lands in Lₙ(ℤ[π]). The Novikov conjecture is equivalent to rational injectivity of the assembly map Hₙ(Bπ; ℚ) → Lₙ(ℤ[π]) ⊗ ℚ. The Borel conjecture is equivalent to 𝒮(M) = * for aspherical M. Memorise the key names: Kasparov (hyperbolic), Connes–Moscovici (amenable), Higson–Kasparov (a‑T‑menable), Bartels–Lück–Weinberger (hyperbolic Borel), Farrell–Jones (algebraic K/L‑theory assembly).

The Eta Invariant, the Rho Invariant, and the Index Theorem for Families

The eta invariant η(D) of a self‑adjoint elliptic operator D on a closed odd‑dimensional manifold was introduced by Atiyah–Patodi–Singer (1975) to correct the index theorem for manifolds with boundary. For a Dirac‑type operator D on a closed (2k‑1)‑manifold Y, η(D) = (1/√π) ∫₀^∞ t-1/2 Tr(D e-t D²) dt, analytically continued to s = 0. The APS index theorem states that for a compact manifold W with boundary Y, the index of a Dirac operator on W with APS boundary conditions is ∫_W Â(W) - (η(D_Y) + h)/2, where h is the dimension of the kernel of D_Y. The eta invariant is a spectral invariant that measures the asymmetry of the spectrum of D.

The rho invariant ρ(M, φ) for a closed oriented (4k‑1)‑manifold M with a representation φ: π₁M → U(n) was defined by Atiyah–Singer (1968) as the difference of eta invariants: ρ(M, φ) = η(D_φ) - n η(D0), where D_φ is the twisted Dirac operator and D0 is the untwisted one. The rho invariant is a homotopy invariant of the pair (M, φ) and takes values in ℝ/ℤ (or ℚ/ℤ for finite representations). It appears in the surgery exact sequence as the map from the structure set 𝒮(M) to the L‑groups: the surgery obstruction of a normal map (f, b): N → M is given by the rho invariant of the kernel. For a homotopy equivalence f: N → M, the rho invariant of the mapping cylinder gives an element in Lₙ(ℤ[π]) ⊗ ℚ/ℤ.

The index theorem for families, proved by Atiyah–Singer (1971) and extended by Bismut–Freed (1986), concerns a proper submersion π: E → B with compact fibres and a family of Dirac operators D_b on the fibres. The index is an element in K⁰(B) (or K¹(B) for odd‑dimensional fibres). The family index theorem expresses the Chern character of the index bundle in terms of the Â‑genus of the vertical tangent bundle integrated over the fibre. The eta invariant appears as the connection form on the determinant line bundle of the family: the Bismut–Freed connection has curvature given by the fibre integral of the Â‑genus, and its holonomy is the exponential of the eta invariant. This leads to the definition of the determinant line bundle and the Quillen metric.

In cobordism theory, the eta and rho invariants provide homomorphisms from the spin cobordism group Ω4k-1Spin(Bπ) to ℝ/ℤ. The map sends a spin manifold M with a map f: M → Bπ to the rho invariant of the twisted Dirac operator. This homomorphism factors through the surgery obstruction groups and is used to detect non‑trivial elements in the structure set. The work of Cheeger–Gromov (1985) on L²‑rho invariants extends these ideas to infinite groups, giving invariants that take values in ℝ and are related to the Novikov–Shubin invariants. The index theorem for families also underlies the construction of the Witten genus as the index of the Dirac operator on loop space, and the string orientation of tmf.

The Adams Spectral Sequence for the Sphere Spectrum and its Cobordism Connections

The classical Adams spectral sequence (ASS) computes the stable homotopy groups of spheres π_*^S = π_*(𝕊) from the cohomology of the Steenrod algebra. For a prime p, the E₂‑page is Ext𝒜_ps,t(H^*(𝕊; 𝔽_p), H^*(𝕊; 𝔽_p)) ≅ Ext𝒜_ps,t(𝔽_p, 𝔽_p), where 𝒜_p is the mod p Steenrod algebra. The spectral sequence converges to the p‑completion of πt-s^S. The differentials d_r: E_rs,t → E_rs+r, t+r-1 are notoriously difficult; the first few are known: d₂(h_i) = 0, d₂(h_i h_j) = hi+1 h_j2 + …, and the famous d₃(h13) = h0 h22 at p = 2. The ASS is the primary tool for computing low‑dimensional stable stems: π0^S = ℤ, π1^S = ℤ/2, π2^S = ℤ/2, π3^S = ℤ/24, π4^S = 0, π5^S = 0, π6^S = ℤ/2, π7^S = ℤ/240, etc.

The connection to cobordism arises because the Thom spectra MO, MSO, MSpin, MU, etc., are modules over the sphere spectrum. The ASS for a Thom spectrum X has E₂‑page Ext𝒜_ps,t(H^*(X; 𝔽_p), 𝔽_p). For MO, H^*(MO; 𝔽2) is a free 𝒜2‑module on the Thom class, so the ASS collapses at E₂ and π_*(MO) ⊗ ℤ_(2) ≅ H_*(MO; ℤ_(2)). This gives the classical result that unoriented cobordism groups are polynomial algebras on the Stiefel‑Whitney numbers. For MSO, the ASS at p = 2 has non‑trivial differentials; the 2‑primary part of oriented cobordism was computed by Wall (1960) using the ASS and the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence. The image of the J‑homomorphism J: π_*(SO) → π_*^S is detected on the 1‑line of the ASS (the elements h_i). The Adams conjecture (proved by Quillen, 1971) identifies the fibre of the map BU → BGL₁(𝕊) with the connective K‑theory spectrum, linking the image of J to the v₁‑periodic part of complex cobordism MU.

The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (ANSS) replaces the Steenrod algebra with the Hopf algebroid (MU_*, MU_*MU) and computes π_*(X) from MU_*(X). For X = 𝕊, the ANSS E₂‑page is ExtMU_*MUs,t(MU_*, MU_*). The ANSS converges to the p‑completion of π_*^S and is more amenable to chromatic methods. The β‑family in the ANSS corresponds to the image of J after p‑localization for odd primes, while at p = 2 the α‑family and β‑family merge. The chromatic filtration of the stable homotopy category organizes the ANSS by height of formal groups: height 1 corresponds to K‑theory (v₁‑periodic), height 2 to elliptic cohomology (v₂‑periodic), etc. The nilpotence theorem (Devinatz–Hopkins–Smith, 1988) and the periodicity theorem (Hopkins–Smith, 1998) are proved using the ANSS and the Morava K‑theories K(n). The telescope conjecture, which predicts that the v_n‑periodic localization is smashing, remains open for n ≥ 2.

In practice, the ASS for the sphere is used to compute the stable stems that appear as the image of the Hurewicz map from cobordism groups to stable homotopy. For example, the Hurewicz map Ω_*Spin → π_*^S sends the generator of Ω1Spin = ℤ/2 to η ∈ π1^S, the generator of Ω2Spin = ℤ/2 to η² ∈ π2^S, and the generator of Ω3Spin = 0 to 0. The element ν ∈ π3^S = ℤ/24 comes from the framed 3‑manifold S³ with the Lie group framing. The ASS differentials that kill certain elements in the cobordism groups correspond to the existence of manifolds with boundary that realize the differentials geometrically.

The Mahowald Invariant and the Image of J in Stable Homotopy

The Mahowald invariant is a secondary operation in the Adams spectral sequence that detects elements in the stable homotopy groups of spheres which are not in the image of the J‑homomorphism but are closely related to it. Introduced by Mark Mahowald in the 1970s, it is defined for certain classes in the Ext groups over the Steenrod algebra that survive to the E_∞ page but are not permanent cycles in the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence. Concretely, if x ∈ Ext𝒜2s,t(𝔽2, 𝔽2) is a class that supports a non‑trivial differential in the ANSS, the Mahowald invariant M(x) is an element in a higher filtration of the ASS that maps to x under the map induced by the unit 𝕊 → MU. The invariant takes values in the v₁‑periodic part of the stable stems, which is the image of J after 2‑completion.

The image of J, J: π_*(SO) → π_*^S, is a direct summand of the stable stems at odd primes (by Adams, 1966) and a more complicated subgroup at p = 2. At p = 2, the image of J is generated by the elements η ∈ π1, η² ∈ π2, ν ∈ π3, σ ∈ π7, etc., with orders given by the denominators of Bernoulli numbers. The Adams conjecture (Quillen, 1971) states that the map BU → BGL₁(𝕊) factors through the connective K‑theory spectrum ku, and the fibre is the connective cover of the real K‑theory spectrum KO. This identifies the v₁‑periodic part of MU with the image of J. The Mahowald invariant provides a geometric interpretation: for a class x in the ASS that corresponds to a manifold with a certain framing, M(x) is the obstruction to extending that framing to a higher‑dimensional manifold, and it lands in the image of J.

Mahowald's work on the eta invariant and the Kervaire invariant one problem also uses the Mahowald invariant. The Kervaire invariant one problem asks for which dimensions n there exists a framed manifold of Kervaire invariant 1. The solution by Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel (2016) shows that such manifolds exist only in dimensions 2, 6, 14, 30, 62, and possibly 126. The Mahowald invariant detects the elements θ_j in the stable stems that would correspond to the Kervaire invariant one elements. The invariant is also related to the tmf spectrum: the map tmf → 𝕊 induces a map on homotopy groups, and the Mahowald invariant can be seen as the obstruction to lifting a class from the sphere to tmf.

In cobordism terms, the image of J is the subgroup of π_*^S generated by framed manifolds that bound spin manifolds with a certain property. The Mahowald invariant measures the failure of a framed manifold to bound a spin manifold with a trivialization of the spin structure. It is a homomorphism from a subgroup of the framed cobordism group Ω_*fr to the image of J. The computation of the Mahowald invariant for low‑dimensional classes uses the ASS and the known differentials: for example, M(h12) = η², M(h22) = ν², etc. The invariant is not defined for all classes; its domain is the set of classes that are in the kernel of the map from the ASS to the ANSS. This makes it a subtle tool for distinguishing between the v₁‑periodic and v₂‑periodic phenomena in stable homotopy.

  • Confusing the h‑cobordism theorem (simply‑connected, dimension ≥ 5) with the s‑cobordism theorem (requires vanishing Whitehead torsion for non‑simply‑connected case).
  • Assuming the Novikov conjecture is proved for all groups; it is open for many groups (e.g., SL(3,ℤ)) and known only for classes satisfying Baum–Connes or Farrell–Jones.
  • Thinking the eta invariant is an integer; it is a real number (mod ℤ for the rho invariant) defined via spectral asymmetry.
  • Believing the classical Adams spectral sequence for the sphere collapses at E₂; it has non‑trivial differentials (e.g., d₃(h13) = h0 h22 at p = 2).
  • Mixing the Mahowald invariant (a secondary ASS operation) with the image of J (the primary v₁‑periodic family); the Mahowald invariant maps certain classes into the image of J but is not the same as J.
  • Overlooking that the Borel conjecture implies the Novikov conjecture for aspherical manifolds, but the converse is false.
  • Neglecting the role of the assembly map in both the Novikov conjecture (rational injectivity in L‑theory) and the Borel conjecture (isomorphism in K‑ and L‑theory).
The advanced topics above extend the core cobordism curriculum into the geometric topology of high‑dimensional manifolds, the analytic invariants of index theory, and the deep computational machinery of stable homotopy theory.

Cobordism and the Algebraic L‑Theory of Group Rings

The surgery exact sequence for a closed manifold M of dimension n ≥ 5 takes the form ⋯ → Ln+1(ℤ[π1(M)]) → 𝒮(M) → [M, G/O] → Ln(ℤ[π1(M)]), where 𝒮(M) is the structure set of homotopy equivalences to M and L*(ℤ[π]) are the Wall groups — the algebraic L‑theory of the group ring. These groups are 4‑periodic: Ln(ℤ[π]) ≅ Ln+4(ℤ[π]). For π = 1, L0(ℤ) ≅ ℤ (signature), L2(ℤ) ≅ ℤ/2 (Arf invariant), and odd groups vanish. For finite groups, the L‑groups are finite and computable via the Rothenberg sequence relating them to the Tate cohomology of the projective class group. The assembly map A: H*(Bπ; 𝕃(ℤ)) → L*(ℤ[π]) is the algebraic counterpart of the geometric assembly map in the Novikov conjecture. The Borel conjecture (aspherical manifolds are topologically rigid) is equivalent to the assembly map being an isomorphism for all n. The Farrell–Jones conjecture in L‑theory asserts that the assembly map from the equivariant homology of the classifying space for virtually cyclic subgroups is an isomorphism; it implies the Novikov conjecture and the Borel conjecture for groups satisfying it. Computationally, the L‑groups of ℤ[ℤ] are known: L0(ℤ[ℤ]) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2, L2(ℤ[ℤ]) ≅ ℤ/2 ⊕ ℤ/2, with the extra summands coming from the nilpotent part of the group ring. For hyperbolic groups, the assembly map is known to be split injective (Bartels–Lück–Reich), giving rational injectivity of the Novikov higher signatures. The algebraic L‑theory spectrum 𝕃(ℤ[π]) is a module over the sphere spectrum, and its homotopy groups are the Wall groups; the map 𝕊 → 𝕃(ℤ) induces the surgery obstruction map from framed cobordism to L*(ℤ). The quadratic Poincaré complexes of Ranicki provide a chain‑level model for L‑theory, making the surgery obstruction a well‑defined element in Ln(ℤ[π]) for any degree‑one normal map. The Cappell–Shaneson UNil groups appear in the L‑theory of free products with amalgamation and are the obstruction to splitting the assembly map for π = π₁ * π₂; they are 2‑torsion and vanish for groups of odd order.

Cobordism and the Kirby–Siebenmann Invariant: Topological vs PL Manifolds

The Kirby–Siebenmann invariant ks(M) ∈ H4(M; ℤ/2) is the primary obstruction to a topological manifold M of dimension ≥ 5 admitting a piecewise‑linear (PL) structure. It arises from the fibration TOP/PL → BPL → BTOP where the fibre TOP/PL is a K(ℤ/2, 3), so its first non‑trivial homotopy group is π3(TOP/PL) ≅ ℤ/2. The classifying map M → BTOP lifts to BPL iff the obstruction class in H4(M; π3(TOP/PL)) ≅ H4(M; ℤ/2) vanishes. For a closed 4‑manifold, ks(M) is the mod‑2 reduction of the signature divided by 8: ks(M) ≡ σ(M)/8 (mod 2); this is the Rokhlin theorem in topological guise. The invariant is a cobordism invariant: if W is a topological cobordism between M and N, then ks(M) = ks(N) under the identification of cohomology groups. The Thom spectrum MTOP fits into a cofibre sequence Σ4MTOP → MTOP → MSPL → Σ5MTOP reflecting the fibre sequence K(ℤ/2, 3) → BPL → BTOP. The homotopy groups πn(MTOP) are the topological cobordism groups ΩnTOP; the map MSPL → MTOP induces an isomorphism on πn for n ≤ 6 and a surjection with kernel ℤ/2 for n = 7. The first exotic topological manifold appears in dimension 4 (Freedman's E8 manifold), which has ks = 1 and no PL structure. In dimension ≥ 5, every topological manifold is cobordant to a PL manifold (Siebenmann), so the map ΩnPL → ΩnTOP is surjective for n ≥ 5. The kernel is generated by the topological manifold K3 × S1 in dimension 5? No — the kernel in dimension 5 is trivial; the first non‑trivial kernel is in dimension 7, detected by the Kirby–Siebenmann invariant of the bounding 8‑manifold. The smoothing theory obstruction θ(M) ∈ H3(M; ℤ/2) for a PL manifold to admit a smooth structure is distinct; PL/O has homotopy groups π0 = 0, π1 = ℤ/2, π2 = 0, π3 = ℤ/2, etc. The combined obstruction to a smooth structure on a topological manifold is the pair (ks(M), θ(M)) when a PL structure exists. The cobordism groups Ω*TOP are known to be Ω0 = ℤ, Ω1 = Ω2 = Ω3 = 0, Ω4 = ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2 (signature and ks), Ω5 = Ω6 = 0, Ω7 = ℤ/2 (Kervaire invariant in TOP), and Ω8 = ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2 ⊕ ℤ/2 (signature, ks, and a third invariant). The map Ω*SO → Ω*TOP is an isomorphism through dimension 6 and injective in dimension 7 with cokernel ℤ/2.

Worked computation: Kirby–Siebenmann invariant of a product
Let M = K3 × S1. Compute ks(M) ∈ H4(M; ℤ/2) and determine whether M admits a PL structure.
The K3 surface is a smooth, simply‑connected 4‑manifold with signature σ(K3) = −16. Its Kirby–Siebenmann invariant vanishes because it is smooth. For a product X × S1, the Künneth formula gives H4(X × S1; ℤ/2) ≅ H4(X; ℤ/2) ⊕ H3(X; ℤ/2). The Kirby–Siebenmann invariant is multiplicative under products with circles: ks(X × S1) = ks(X) × 1 + w2(X) × w1(S1) in the appropriate sense, but more directly, the obstruction theory for TOP/PL shows that ks(X × S1) is the image of ks(X) under the cross product with the fundamental class of S1. Since ks(K3) = 0, we have ks(K3 × S1) = 0. Therefore K3 × S1 admits a PL structure. In fact, it admits a smooth structure (the product smooth structure). The non‑trivial element in Ω5TOP is not represented by this product; Ω5TOP = 0. The first dimension where ks detects a non‑zero cobordism class is dimension 4 (the E8 manifold) and dimension 7 (the generator of Ω7TOP ≅ ℤ/2).
Exam strategy: distinguishing TOP, PL, and smooth cobordism
When a question asks about the Kirby–Siebenmann invariant, check the dimension first: ks ∈ H4(M; ℤ/2) so it only obstructs PL structures in dimension ≥ 4. In dimension 4, ks(M) ≡ σ(M)/8 (mod 2) (Rokhlin). In dimension ≥ 5, ks is the only obstruction to a PL structure (Siebenmann). The smoothing obstruction θ ∈ H3(M; ℤ/2) lives one dimension lower. Remember the cofibre sequence Σ4MTOP → MTOP → MSPL → Σ5MTOP — it implies ΩnPL → ΩnTOP is an isomorphism for n ≤ 6 and surjective for n = 7 with kernel ℤ/2. The map MSO → MTOP factors through MSPL; the composite is an isomorphism through dimension 6. A common trap: confusing the Kirby–Siebenmann invariant (TOP/PL) with the Kervaire invariant (framed cobordism) or the Rokhlin invariant (spin 4‑manifolds). They are related but distinct: ks is a topological invariant, Kervaire is a smooth framed invariant, Rokhlin is a spin smooth invariant.

Cobordism and the Galatius–Madsen–Tillmann–Weiss Theorem: Homology of Moduli Spaces

The Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTθ for a tangential structure θ: B → BO(d) is the Thom spectrum of the virtual bundle −θ*γd over B. Galatius, Madsen, Tillmann, and Weiss (2009) proved that for θ = id: BO(d) → BO(d), the homology of the moduli space of d‑dimensional manifolds stabilizes: colimg H*(Mg,1; ℤ) ≅ H*(Ω∞−1MTO(d); ℤ) in a range of degrees increasing with genus g. Here Mg,1 is the moduli space of oriented genus‑g surfaces with one boundary component, and MTO(d) is the Thom spectrum of −γd over BO(d). The theorem identifies the stable homology of mapping class groups with the homology of an infinite loop space. For d = 2, MTO(2) ≃ Σ−2MSO, and Ω∞−1MTO(2) ≃ Ω∞−1Σ−2MSO ≃ Ω∞−3MSO. The homology of this space is computed via the Serre spectral sequence for the fibration Ω∞−3MSO → * → B(Ω∞−3MSO) or via the Adams spectral sequence. The result implies Harer's stability theorem and the Mumford conjecture (proved by Madsen–Weiss 2007 for d = 2): the stable rational cohomology of the mapping class group Γg is a polynomial algebra on the Miller–Morita–Mumford classes κi ∈ H2i(Γg; ℚ). For higher d, the theorem gives a complete description of the stable homology of the diffeomorphism groups of #g(Sd × Sd) (for d even) or #g(Sd × Sd+1) (for d odd) in terms of the homology of Ω∞−1MTO(d). The proof uses the cobordism category Cobd whose objects are closed (d−1)‑manifolds and morphisms are d‑dimensional cobordisms. The classifying space BCobd is weakly equivalent to Ω∞−1MTO(d). The map from the moduli space to BCobd is constructed by sending a manifold to its "cobordism class" and using the Pontryagin–Thom collapse. The homology equivalence is then a consequence of the high connectivity of the map from the space of embeddings to the Thom space, established via the h‑cobordism theorem and Cerf theory. The theorem has been extended to manifolds with tangential structures (e.g., spin, framing) by Galatius–Randal‑Williams, giving stable homology of moduli spaces of manifolds with G‑structures. The connection to cobordism is direct: the Thom spectrum MTO(d) represents the cobordism theory of d‑manifolds with a stable normal framing (or orientation), and its infinite loop space structure encodes the "addition" of manifolds by connected sum. The homology of Ω∞−1MTO(d) is a Hopf algebra, and the theorem identifies the stable homology of mapping class groups as a Hopf subalgebra.

Cobordism and the Goodwillie Calculus: The Calculus of Functors Approach

Goodwillie's calculus of functors provides a systematic method to approximate homotopy functors by polynomial functors, analogous to Taylor series in analysis. For a functor F: Top → Top (or spectra), the n‑excisive approximation PnF is the universal n‑excisive functor under F. The tower F → ⋯ → PnF → Pn−1F → ⋯ → P0F converges to F for sufficiently connected inputs. Applied to cobordism, the functor F(X) = Ω∞MTO(d) ∧ X+ (the cobordism theory represented by MTO(d)) is linear (1‑excisive) because it is a homology theory. More interestingly, the functor Emb(−, ℝd) of embeddings of a manifold into Euclidean space has a Taylor tower whose layers are related to configuration spaces and the homology of moduli spaces. The first derivative ∂F of the embedding functor is a spectrum with an action of O(d), and the layers DnF are built from (∂F)∧n ∧O(d) (EΣn)+. Weiss showed that for F(M) = Emb(M, ℝd), the tower converges for dim M < d−2 and the layers compute the homology of the space of embeddings. The connection to cobordism appears via the map Emb(M, ℝd) → Ω∞MTO(d) given by the Pontryagin–Thom collapse of the normal bundle. This map is highly connected in the stable range and induces an isomorphism on homology in the stable genus range, recovering the Galatius–Madsen–Tillmann–Weiss theorem. The Goodwillie tower for the identity functor on spaces has layers DnId(X) ≃ (X∧n)hΣn, the homotopy orbits of the Σn‑action on the n‑fold smash product. The derivatives of the identity are the sphere spectrum: ∂nId ≃ 𝕊. For the functor F(X) = Q(X) = Ω∞Σ∞X (stable homotopy), the tower is the Goodwillie tower of the identity on spectra, which splits: Q(X) ≃ ∏n≥1 DnQ(X). The layers DnQ(X) are the n‑th extended powers of X. The calculus also applies to the functor F(B) = MTO(d) ∧ B+ representing cobordism with a map to B; its derivatives encode the homology of the moduli spaces of manifolds with a map to B. The convergence of the tower for embedding functors is controlled by the connectivity of the map Emb(M, N) → Emb(M, N × ℝ), which is related to the h‑cobordism theorem. The calculus provides a conceptual framework for understanding why the stable homology of moduli spaces is given by an infinite loop space: the embedding functor is analytic, and its Taylor tower converges to the stable homology, which is the homology of the linear approximation P1Emb(−, ℝd), identified with Ω∞−1MTO(d). The higher layers DnEmb for n ≥ 2 capture the unstable homology and are related to the homology of configuration spaces of points in the manifold.

Cobordism and the Rokhlin Theorem: Signature Defects in Dimension Four

The Rokhlin theorem (1952) states that the signature of a smooth, closed, spin 4‑manifold is divisible by 16. Equivalently, the signature modulo 16 is a cobordism invariant of spin 4‑manifolds, giving a homomorphism σ/16: Ω4Spin → ℤ/16. The generator of Ω4Spin ≅ ℤ is the K3 surface with σ = −16, so the map is an isomorphism. The theorem fails for topological manifolds: Freedman's E8 manifold is a topological spin 4‑manifold with signature 8, so σ/8 ≡ 1 (mod 2) gives the Kirby–Siebenmann invariant. The proof uses the Hirzebruch signature theorem σ(M) = ⟨L(M), [M]⟩ where L(M) = 1 + p1/3 + ⋯. For a spin manifold, w2 = 0, so p1 ≡ 0 (mod 2) and the Pontryagin number ⟨p1, [M]⟩ is even. The signature theorem gives σ(M) = ⟨p1/3, [M]⟩; since p1/3 is an integral class for spin manifolds (by Wu's formula), σ(M) is an integer multiple of the evaluation of an integral class. The divisibility by 16 comes from a deeper argument using the fact that a spin 4‑manifold bounds a spin 5‑manifold (since Ω4Spin = ℤ is generated by K3 which bounds a spin 5‑manifold? Actually K3 does not bound a spin 5‑manifold; Ω5Spin = 0 so every spin 4‑manifold bounds a spin 5‑manifold). The signature of a spin 5‑manifold with boundary is not defined, but the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index theorem for manifolds with boundary gives σ(W) = ∫W L(p) − η(∂W) where η is the eta invariant of the signature operator on the boundary. For a spin boundary, the eta invariant is a multiple of 1/2? The modern proof uses the index theorem for the Dirac operator on a spin 5‑manifold bounding M: the index of the Dirac operator on W with APS boundary conditions is ∫W Â(W) − η(∂W)/2. Since Â(W) = 1 − p1/24 + ⋯ and p1 is divisible by 2 for spin manifolds, the integral is a half‑integer, forcing η(M) to be a multiple of 1/2. The eta invariant of the signature operator on a spin 4‑manifold is related to the signature by ηsign(M) = σ(M)/2 (mod ℤ). Combining these gives σ(M) ≡ 0 (mod 16). The Rokhlin invariant μ(M) = σ(M)/8 (mod 2) for a spin 3‑manifold M is defined by choosing a spin 4‑manifold W with ∂W = M and setting μ(M) = σ(W)/8 (mod 2); this is well‑defined because the difference of two such W is a closed spin 4‑manifold with signature divisible by 16. The Rokhlin invariant is a homomorphism μ: Ω3Spin → ℤ/2. Since Ω3Spin = 0, every spin 3‑manifold bounds a spin 4‑manifold, and the invariant is identically zero — but it is non‑trivial on the spin cobordism group of 3‑manifolds with a map to a space B, giving the invariant μ: Ω3Spin(B) → ℤ/2. The Rokhlin theorem generalizes to the Ochanine invariant for spin manifolds of dimension 8k+4, which takes values in ℤ/2 and is related to the Witten genus. The signature defect for a spin manifold with boundary is measured by the eta invariant, and the APS index theorem gives the precise formula σ(W) = ⟨L(W), [W]⟩ − η(∂W). For a spin 4‑manifold M bounding a spin 5‑manifold W, σ(M) = 16 · index(DW) where DW is the Dirac operator on W with APS boundary conditions, proving the divisibility by 16.

  • Confusing the Kirby–Siebenmann invariant (TOP/PL obstruction in H⁴(M; ℤ/2)) with the Rokhlin invariant (spin 3‑manifold invariant in ℤ/2) or the Kervaire invariant (framed cobordism invariant). They live in different dimensions and detect different structures.
  • Assuming the assembly map in L‑theory is always an isomorphism. It is the content of the Farrell–Jones conjecture, known for many groups (hyperbolic, CAT(0), etc.) but open in general. The Novikov conjecture only requires rational injectivity.
  • Thinking the Galatius–Madsen–Tillmann–Weiss theorem computes the full homology of mapping class groups. It computes the stable homology (genus → ∞) in a range of degrees; unstable homology is much richer and not given by the Thom spectrum.
  • Believing Goodwillie calculus converges for all functors on all spaces. Convergence requires the functor to be analytic and the input to be sufficiently connected; for embedding functors, convergence holds only when dim M < d−2.
  • Stating the Rokhlin theorem as "signature of a spin 4‑manifold is divisible by 8". It is divisible by 16. The divisibility by 8 holds for oriented 4‑manifolds with w2 = 0 (spin) but the sharp result is 16; the E8 manifold shows 8 is not enough in the topological category.
  • Mixing up the Wall groups L*(ℤ[π]) (algebraic L‑theory) with the cobordism groups Ω*G (geometric cobordism). They are related by the surgery obstruction map, but L‑groups are 4‑periodic and depend on the group ring, while cobordism groups are not periodic and depend on the tangential structure.
  • Overlooking that the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTθ represents cobordism of manifolds with a θ‑structure on the stable normal bundle, not the tangent bundle. For θ = BO(d) → BO(d), MTO(d) is the Thom spectrum of −γd, giving normal bordism, which is equivalent to oriented bordism via the Pontryagin–Thom isomorphism.

Cobordism and the Atiyah–Hirzebruch Spectral Sequence

The Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence (AHSS) is the primary computational tool for evaluating the cobordism groups of a space X with coefficients in a generalized homology theory. For a tangential structure G (such as O, SO, Spin, or U) the G-cobordism homology theory Ω*G(−) is represented by the Thom spectrum MG. The AHSS for MG-homology has E2-page

E2p,q = Hp(X; ΩqG) ⇒ Ωp+qG(X)

where ΩqG = πq(MG) are the cobordism groups of a point. The differentials dr have bidegree (−r, r−1) and are determined by the action of Steenrod operations on the Thom class of MG. For unoriented cobordism (G = O) the coefficient groups ΩqO are polynomial algebras over ℤ/2 on the Stiefel–Whitney numbers, so the E2-page is a tensor product of ordinary homology with a polynomial ring. The first non‑trivial differential is d2 = Sq2 acting on the Thom class; higher differentials involve higher Steenrod squares. In oriented cobordism (G = SO) the coefficients are ℤ in degrees a multiple of 4 and zero otherwise, so the spectral sequence collapses at E2 after tensoring with ℚ, but torsion differentials (e.g. d3 = β ∘ Sq2) detect the 2‑torsion in Ω*SO. For complex cobordism (G = U) the coefficient ring MU* is the Lazard ring, and the AHSS becomes a spectral sequence of modules over this ring; its differentials encode the formal group law structure. A standard application is the computation of Ω*Spin(Bℤ/2) which feeds into the Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence for MSpin. The AHSS also underlies the proof that the Pontryagin–Thom map πn(MG) → ΩnG is an isomorphism: one filters the Thom spectrum by the skeleta of the universal G-bundle and identifies the associated graded with the E∞-page of the AHSS for a point.

Cobordism and the Conner–Floyd Isomorphism

The Conner–Floyd isomorphism establishes a precise relationship between complex cobordism MU and complex topological K‑theory KU. As graded rings, MU* ≅ ℤ[x1, x2, …] where |xi| = 2i, while KU* ≅ ℤ[β, β−1] with |β| = 2. The isomorphism is induced by the unique complex orientation of KU corresponding to the formal group law FKU(u, v) = u + v − βuv. Concretely, the map φ: MU → KU sends the universal Chern class c1MU to β(1 − L) where L is the canonical line bundle in KU0(ℂP∞). On homotopy groups this yields a surjection φ*: MU* → KU* whose kernel is the ideal generated by the coefficients of the logarithm of the universal formal group law after the substitution β = 1. Equivalently, KU* ≅ MU* ⊗MU* ℤ where ℤ is the MU*-module defined by the Todd genus. The Conner–Floyd theorem also identifies the Adams–Novikov E2-term for KU as ExtMU*MU(MU*, MU* ⊗ KU*) and shows that the KU-based Adams–Novikov spectral sequence collapses at E2. This isomorphism is the geometric shadow of the fact that the universal formal group law over MU* becomes the multiplicative formal group law after the Landweber exact functor theorem applies to the map MU* → ℤ classifying the multiplicative law. In practice, the Conner–Floyd map allows one to compute MU-characteristic numbers (e.g. Chern numbers) from K-theoretic data: the Todd genus of a stably complex manifold M equals the index of the Dirac operator twisted by the virtual bundle determined by the MU-orientation, and the image of the fundamental class [M] under φ* is the K-theory Euler characteristic χ(M) = Σ (−1)i dim Hi(M; ℂ).

Worked Example: Computing a Differential in the AHSS for MSpin
Let X = BSpin(5) and consider the AHSS for MSpin-homology. The E2-page is Hp(BSpin(5); ΩqSpin). Using the known low‑dimensional spin cobordism groups Ω0Spin = ℤ, Ω1Spin = Ω2Spin = 0, Ω3Spin = 0, Ω4Spin = ℤ (generated by the K3 surface), determine the first possibly non‑zero differential hitting the summand H4(BSpin(5); ℤ) ⊗ Ω0Spin.
The relevant differential is d4: E44,0 → E40,3. Since Ω3Spin = 0, the target is zero, so d4 = 0. The next candidate is d5: E55,0 → E50,4. Here E50,4 = H0(BSpin(5); Ω4Spin) ≅ ℤ. The differential d5 is induced by the action of the Steenrod operation Sq2 on the Thom class of MSpin followed by the Bockstein. In the spin case the first non‑trivial differential is d5 = β ∘ Sq2 on the fundamental class of the fibre. For BSpin(5) the cohomology H*(BSpin(5); ℤ/2) is generated by w4 in degree 4. The operation Sq2(w4) = w22 + w4 = w4 because w2 = 0 for spin bundles. The Bockstein β(w4) is the integral class p1/2 mod 2, which generates H5(BSpin(5); ℤ) ≅ ℤ/2. Dualising, d5 maps the generator of H5(BSpin(5); ℤ) ≅ ℤ/2 onto the 2‑torsion summand of H0(BSpin(5); Ω4Spin) ≅ ℤ. Hence d5 is surjective onto the 2‑torsion in Ω4Spin(BSpin(5)), killing the ℤ/2 summand and leaving a free ℤ generated by the K3 class. This matches the known result Ω4Spin(BSpin(5)) ≅ ℤ.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a differential in an Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence for a Thom spectrum, first write down the coefficient groups of the relevant cobordism theory. Identify the lowest degree where the coefficient group is non‑zero and the source homology group is non‑trivial. Then recall the standard formula for the first non‑zero differential: for MO it is d2 = Sq2, for MSO it is d3 = β ∘ Sq2, for MSpin it is d5 = β ∘ Sq2 (since w2 = 0). Use the Wu formulas to evaluate Steenrod squares on Stiefel–Whitney classes of the universal bundle. If the target group is zero, the differential vanishes and you move to the next possible differential. This systematic approach avoids guesswork and matches the pattern expected in written answers.

Cobordism and the Algebraic Surgery Exact Sequence

The algebraic surgery exact sequence of Wall and Ranicki connects the geometric classification of high‑dimensional manifolds to the algebraic L‑theory of the fundamental group ring. For a closed n-dimensional topological manifold M with fundamental group π, the structure set STOP(M) — the set of homotopy equivalences N → M up to cobordism — fits into an exact sequence

⋯ → Ln+1(ℤ[π]) → STOP(M) → [M, G/TOP] → Ln(ℤ[π]) → ⋯

where G/TOP is the homotopy fibre of the map BTOP → BG and L*(ℤ[π]) are the quadratic L‑groups of the group ring. The map [M, G/TOP] → Ln(ℤ[π]) is the surgery obstruction map; its kernel consists of normal invariants that can be realized by a homotopy equivalence. The L‑groups are 4‑periodic: L0(ℤ) ≅ ℤ (signature), L1(ℤ) = 0, L2(ℤ) ≅ ℤ/2 (Arf invariant), L3(ℤ) = 0. For a general group π the L‑groups are much more complicated; they contain the projective class group K0(ℤ[π]) and the Whitehead group Wh(π) as summands in low degrees. The assembly map A: Hn(Bπ; L(ℤ)) → Ln(ℤ[π]) compares the homology of Bπ with coefficients in the simply‑connected L‑spectrum to the actual L‑groups. The Novikov conjecture asserts that A ⊗ ℚ is injective; the Borel conjecture (for aspherical manifolds) would follow if A were an isomorphism. The algebraic surgery exact sequence is the algebraic analogue of the geometric surgery exact sequence of Browder–Novikov–Sullivan–Wall, and the two are linked by the Pontryagin–Thom construction which identifies the normal invariant set [M, G/TOP] with the cobordism group ΩnTOP(M) of degree‑one normal maps. This identification makes the surgery obstruction a map of cobordism groups ΩnTOP(M) → Ln(ℤ[π]), and the exact sequence becomes a long exact sequence of cobordism groups. In particular, the kernel of the surgery obstruction is the image of the structure set, which is precisely the set of manifold structures within the given homotopy type.

Cobordism and the Farrell–Jones Conjecture in L‑Theory

The Farrell–Jones conjecture predicts that the assembly map in algebraic L‑theory is an isomorphism for every group π. In its most common form, the conjecture states that the map

A: Hn(EVCπ; L(ℤ)) → Ln(ℤ[π])

is an isomorphism, where EVCπ is the classifying space for the family of virtually cyclic subgroups of π and L(ℤ) is the simply‑connected L‑theory spectrum. The domain is a homology theory built from the L‑groups of virtually cyclic subgroups, which are known by the work of Bak, Kolster, and others. The conjecture implies the Novikov conjecture (rational injectivity) and the Borel conjecture for groups satisfying the isomorphism conjecture. It has been proved for a wide class of groups: hyperbolic groups (Bartels–Lück–Reich), CAT(0) groups (Bartels–Lück), mapping class groups (Bartels–Bestvina), and more generally for groups acting properly on finite‑dimensional CAT(0) spaces. The proof uses controlled topology and the theory of spectra with group actions. From the cobordism perspective, the Farrell–Jones isomorphism identifies the L‑groups — which are the algebraic surgery obstructions — with a homology theory that can be computed via the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence. This spectral sequence has E2-page Hp(EVCπ; Lq(ℤ)) and converges to Lp+q(ℤ[π]). The differentials are controlled by the geometry of the group action. When the conjecture holds, the algebraic surgery exact sequence becomes a long exact sequence of computable homology groups, turning the classification of manifold structures into a problem in equivariant homology. The conjecture also has a K‑theoretic analogue (the Farrell–Jones conjecture in algebraic K‑theory) which is known for the same class of groups. The interplay between the K‑theoretic and L‑theoretic assembly maps is mediated by the Rothenberg sequence, which relates Ln(ℤ[π]) to Kn(ℤ[π]) and the Tate cohomology of π with coefficients in the simply‑connected L‑groups.

Cobordism and the Hirzebruch Signature Theorem

The Hirzebruch signature theorem expresses the signature of a closed oriented 4k-manifold M as the evaluation of the L-genus on the fundamental class: σ(M) = ⟨L(M), [M]⟩. The L-genus is a multiplicative sequence of polynomials in the Pontryagin classes pi(M) determined by the power series √x/tanh(√x) = 1 + x/3 − x2/45 + ⋯. Explicitly, L1 = p1/3, L2 = (7p2 − p12)/45, etc. The theorem is proved by showing that the signature defines a genus — a ring homomorphism Ω*SO → ℤ — and that the L-genus is the unique genus whose logarithm is the series above. By the Thom–Pontryagin construction, Ω*SO ⊗ ℚ is a polynomial algebra on the classes of ℂP2i, and the signature of ℂP2i is 1, which matches the L-genus evaluation. The signature theorem has profound consequences for cobordism: it shows that the signature is a cobordism invariant, so it factors through the oriented cobordism ring. The kernel of the signature homomorphism is the ideal generated by manifolds with vanishing Pontryagin numbers. In dimension 4, the theorem gives σ(M) = ⟨p1/3, [M]⟩, which implies that the Pontryagin number ⟨p1, [M]⟩ is divisible by 3. For spin 4‑manifolds, Wu's formula gives p1 ≡ 0 (mod 2), so ⟨p1, [M]⟩ is even; combined with divisibility by 3 one gets divisibility by 6. The deeper divisibility by 16 (Rokhlin's theorem) follows from the index theorem for the Dirac operator, not from the signature theorem alone. The L-genus also appears in the Atiyah–Singer index theorem for the signature operator: the analytical index of the signature operator on M equals the topological index ⟨L(M), [M]⟩. This identification makes the signature theorem a special case of the index theorem and places it firmly in the framework of cobordism invariants coming from elliptic operators.

  • Confusing the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence for a generalized homology theory with the Adams spectral sequence; the former computes homology of a space, the latter computes stable homotopy groups of a spectrum.
  • Assuming the Conner–Floyd map MU → KU is injective on homotopy groups; it is surjective with a large kernel generated by the coefficients of the universal formal group law.
  • Thinking the algebraic surgery exact sequence is a sequence of abelian groups for all n; the structure set STOP(M) is only a pointed set in general, and exactness at that term means exactness of pointed sets.
  • Believing the Farrell–Jones conjecture is proved for all groups; it remains open for general linear groups over infinite fields and for many solvable groups.
  • Mixing up the L-genus (signature) with the Â-genus (Dirac index); they are different multiplicative sequences and give different genera on Ω*SO.
  • Overlooking that the Hirzebruch signature theorem holds for oriented manifolds, but the L-genus is not integral on non‑spin manifolds; the signature is still an integer because the Pontryagin numbers satisfy the necessary congruences.
  • Treating the assembly map in L‑theory as a map of rings; it is only a map of abelian groups (or spectra) and does not respect the quadratic refinement in general.

Cobordism and the Baum–Connes Assembly Map

The Baum–Connes conjecture predicts an isomorphism between the equivariant K‑homology of the classifying space for proper actions of a discrete group Γ and the K‑theory of the reduced group C*‑algebra C*r(Γ). The assembly map μ: KΓ*(EΓ) → K*(C*r(Γ)) is constructed using the descent homomorphism in Kasparov’s KK‑theory. From the cobordism perspective, the analytic assembly map can be compared with the topological assembly map in algebraic L‑theory that appears in the Farrell–Jones conjecture. Both maps are induced by the same geometric idea: a manifold with a Γ‑action gives rise to a class in the homology of the orbit space, and the index of a Γ‑equivariant elliptic operator yields an element in the K‑theory of the group algebra. The Baum–Connes isomorphism has been proved for a large class of groups — including a‑T‑menable groups (Higson–Kasparov), hyperbolic groups (Lafforgue), and groups acting properly on finite‑dimensional CAT(0) spaces — using techniques from controlled topology and the theory of spectra with group actions. In the context of cobordism, the assembly map factors through the topological K‑theory spectrum KU, and the Conner–Floyd isomorphism identifies the complex cobordism ring MU*(pt) with the Lazard ring, which carries the universal formal group law. The Chern character from KU to rational cohomology then translates the analytic index into characteristic numbers, linking the Baum–Connes map to the Hirzebruch signature theorem and the Atiyah–Singer index theorem for families. When the conjecture holds, the K‑theory of C*r(Γ) becomes computable via the equivariant Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence whose E2‑page is Hp(ΓEΓ; Kq(pt)). The differentials in this spectral sequence are controlled by the geometry of the Γ‑action, and their vanishing is equivalent to the injectivity and surjectivity of the assembly map. The interplay between the K‑theoretic assembly map and the L‑theoretic assembly map is mediated by the Rothenberg sequence, which relates Ln(ℤ[Γ]) to Kn(ℤ[Γ]) and the Tate cohomology of Γ with coefficients in the simply‑connected L‑groups. This relationship shows that cobordism invariants coming from elliptic operators (the analytic index) and from quadratic forms (the surgery obstruction) are two faces of the same assembly phenomenon.

Cobordism and Topological Cyclic Homology

Topological cyclic homology (TC) is a cyclotomic spectrum that approximates algebraic K‑theory via the cyclotomic trace map K → TC. For a ring spectrum R, TC(R) is built from the fixed points of the circle action on the topological Hochschild homology THH(R). The complex cobordism spectrum MU maps to THH(MU) and hence to TC(MU) through the unit of the ring spectrum structure. The homotopy groups of TC(MU) have been computed by Hesselholt and Madsen: π*TC(MU) is a polynomial algebra over the p‑typical Witt vectors W(𝔽p) on classes in even degrees, reflecting the fact that MU is a complex oriented theory whose formal group law is the universal one. The cyclotomic trace induces a map from the algebraic K‑theory of MU to TC(MU), and the fiber of this map is closely related to the K‑theory of the sphere spectrum. In chromatic homotopy theory, TC provides a “redshift” phenomenon: the height of the formal group law associated to TC(R) is one higher than that of R. For MU, which has chromatic height 1, TC(MU) detects height 2 phenomena, and its K(n)‑localizations for n ≥ 2 are non‑trivial. This redshift is mirrored in the cobordism category: the free loop space LM of a manifold M carries a natural S1‑action, and the S1‑equivariant cobordism of LM is related to THH of the cobordism spectrum of M. The work of Blumberg–Cohen–Teleman shows that the topological Hochschild homology of the Thom spectrum Mξ of a vector bundle ξ over a space X can be identified with the Thom spectrum of the free loop space LX with a certain bundle. This identification gives a geometric interpretation of the cyclotomic structure on THH in terms of cobordism of manifolds with circle actions. Moreover, the Nikolaus–Scholze description of TC as the homotopy fixed points of the cyclotomic Frobenius on THH provides a bridge between the algebraic K‑theory of ring spectra and the cobordism invariants of manifolds with additional structure (framings, spin structures, etc.). In particular, the image of the J‑homomorphism in the stable homotopy groups of spheres, which is detected by the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence based on MU, also appears in the homotopy groups of TC(𝕊), linking classical cobordism calculations to modern cyclotomic techniques.

Worked Example: Computing the Assembly Map for a Hyperbolic Group
Let Γ be a torsion‑free hyperbolic group. Explain why the Baum–Connes assembly map μ: KΓ0(EΓ) → K0(C*r(Γ)) is an isomorphism, and describe how the signature of a closed oriented 4k‑manifold with a free Γ‑action can be recovered from the image of its fundamental class under μ.
For a torsion‑free hyperbolic group Γ, the classifying space for proper actions EΓ is a finite‑dimensional CAT(0) space on which Γ acts properly and cocompactly. Lafforgue proved the Baum–Connes conjecture for all hyperbolic groups using a Banach‑algebraic version of the assembly map and a careful analysis of the rapid decay property. The isomorphism μ identifies the equivariant K‑homology of EΓ, which is generated by Γ‑equivariant elliptic operators on the universal cover of a closed manifold M with fundamental group Γ, with the K‑theory of the reduced group C*‑algebra. Given a closed oriented 4k‑manifold M with a free Γ‑action, the quotient M/Γ is a closed manifold with fundamental group Γ. The signature operator on M is Γ‑equivariant, and its index defines a class in KΓ0(EΓ). Under μ this class maps to the index of the induced operator on the Mishchenko–Fomenko bundle over M/Γ, which lives in K0(C*r(Γ)). The Chern character composed with the trace on C*r(Γ) evaluates this index to the L‑genus of M/Γ, which by the Hirzebruch signature theorem equals the signature of M/Γ. Since the signature is multiplicative under finite covers, the signature of M equals the signature of M/Γ times the degree of the cover, and this number is recovered from the image of the fundamental class [M] under the assembly map followed by the trace. Thus the analytic assembly map encodes the topological signature invariant.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the relationship between an analytic assembly map and a cobordism invariant, first identify the relevant homology theory (K‑homology, L‑homology, or MU‑homology). Then recall the geometric construction of the assembly map: a manifold with group action gives a class in the equivariant homology of the classifying space, and the index of an equivariant elliptic operator gives the image in the K‑ or L‑theory of the group algebra. Use the Chern character or the signature operator to translate the analytic index into characteristic numbers. Mention the key theorems (Baum–Connes for hyperbolic groups, Farrell–Jones for CAT(0) groups) and the spectral sequences that compute the target groups.

Cobordism and Algebraic Cobordism of Levine–Morel

Algebraic cobordism Ω* is a universal oriented cohomology theory on smooth quasi‑projective varieties over a field k of characteristic zero, constructed by Levine and Morel. It satisfies the same formal properties as complex cobordism MU* — homotopy invariance, localization, projective bundle formula, and the existence of Chern classes for vector bundles — but in the algebro‑geometric setting. The coefficient ring Ω*(pt) is isomorphic to the Lazard ring ℒ, the same ring that classifies one‑dimensional commutative formal group laws over ℤ. This isomorphism is established by showing that the formal group law induced by the tensor product of line bundles in algebraic cobordism is universal. The realization functor from the Morel–Voevodsky stable motivic homotopy category SH(k) to the classical stable homotopy category sends the motivic Thom spectrum MGL (the algebraic analogue of MU) to MU, and induces a ring homomorphism Ω*(pt) → MU*(pt) which is an isomorphism after inverting the exponential characteristic. Over ℂ, the complex realization map Ω*(X) → MU2*(X(ℂ)) is a natural transformation of oriented cohomology theories, and it becomes an isomorphism after tensoring with ℚ. The algebraic cobordism ring of a point is a polynomial algebra on generators ai,j of bidegree (i,j) corresponding to the coefficients of the universal formal group law. The relationship with the Brown–Peterson spectrum BP is analogous: there is a map Ω* → BP* which classifies the p‑typical formal group law. The Landweber exact functor theorem applies to algebraic cobordism, giving a family of oriented cohomology theories on smooth varieties from any Landweber exact formal group law. In particular, algebraic K‑theory, Chow groups, and Morava K‑theories (after suitable base change) are all obtained from Ω* by change of formal group law. The motivic Adams–Novikov spectral sequence based on MGL converges to the motivic stable homotopy groups of spheres, and its E2‑page is Ext over the motivic Hopf algebroid (Ω*(pt), Ω*(MGL)). This spectral sequence is the algebraic counterpart of the classical Adams–Novikov spectral sequence and provides a powerful tool for computing motivic stable stems, which in turn map to classical stable stems via realization.

Cobordism and Factorization Homology of Manifolds

Factorization homology (also called topological chiral homology) assigns to an En‑algebra A in a symmetric monoidal ∞‑category and a framed n‑manifold M a value ∫M A, which is the colimit of a factorization diagram built from the Ran space of M. When A is the sphere spectrum 𝕊, factorization homology recovers the stable homotopy type of the manifold: ∫M 𝕊 ≃ Σ∞ M+. For a general Thom spectrum Mξ associated to a vector bundle ξ over a space X, the factorization homology ∫M Mξ can be identified with the Thom spectrum of the mapping space Map(M, X) with a certain virtual bundle, by the work of Francis and Gaitsgory. This identification gives a cobordism‑theoretic interpretation: the value of factorization homology on a closed n‑manifold depends only on the framed cobordism class of M, because the construction is invariant under framed cobordisms of the source manifold. In particular, the factorization homology of the complex cobordism spectrum MU over a framed manifold M yields a spectrum whose homotopy groups are the MU‑homology of the mapping space Map(M, BU). The cobordism invariance of factorization homology leads to a classification of fully extended topological quantum field theories (TQFTs) via the cobordism hypothesis: an n‑dimensional fully extended TQFT with values in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)‑category C is equivalent to a fully dualizable object of C. The space of such TQFTs is the mapping space from the (∞,n)‑category of framed cobordisms Bordnfr to C. The factorization homology of the En‑algebra corresponding to the TQFT on a closed n‑manifold computes the partition function of the theory. For the (2,1)‑dimensional TQFT associated to a modular tensor category, factorization homology over a surface Σ recovers the Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant, which is a cobordism invariant of 3‑manifolds bounding Σ. The relationship between factorization homology and cobordism is further deepened by the work of Ayala–Francis–Tanaka, who show that the factorization homology of an En‑algebra A on a manifold M can be computed by a factorization homology spectral sequence whose E1‑page involves the homology of configuration spaces of M with coefficients in the homology of A. This spectral sequence converges to the homology of ∫M A, and its differentials are controlled by the operadic structure of A. When A = MU, the spectral sequence relates the complex cobordism of configuration spaces to the complex cobordism of the mapping space, providing a new computational tool for MU‑homology of mapping spaces.

Cobordism and the Chromatic Redshift Conjecture

The chromatic redshift conjecture, formulated by Ravenel and refined by Ausoni–Rognes, predicts that certain algebraic constructions on ring spectra increase chromatic height by one. The primary examples are: the free loop space construction LX, topological Hochschild homology THH(R), topological cyclic homology TC(R), and the algebraic K‑theory K(R). For the complex cobordism spectrum MU, which has chromatic height 1 (its Morava K‑theory K(1)‑localization is non‑trivial while K(n)‑localization vanishes for n > 1), the conjecture predicts that THH(MU) and TC(MU) should have chromatic height 2. This has been verified: the K(2)‑localization of THH(MU) is non‑zero, and the homotopy groups of TC(MU) contain v2‑periodic families. The redshift phenomenon is reflected in the cobordism category by the fact that the free loop space LM of a manifold M carries a natural S1‑action, and the S1‑equivariant cobordism of LM detects higher chromatic information. In particular, the string cobordism spectrum MString, which is the Thom spectrum of the virtual bundle −γ over BString, has chromatic height 2 at the prime 2 (its K(2)‑localization is related to the elliptic cohomology spectrum tmf). The sigma orientation MString → tmf exhibits a redshift from height 1 (MU) to height 2 (tmf). The chromatic redshift conjecture also has a formulation in terms of the Goodwillie calculus: the n‑th derivative of the identity functor on spaces evaluated at a point is the sphere spectrum, and the Taylor tower of the identity functor interpolates between the sphere spectrum (height 0) and the stable homotopy category (height ∞). The layers of this tower are related to the spectra K(n) via the work of Arone–Mahowald. In cobordism terms, the n‑th layer corresponds to the cobordism theory of manifolds with a certain tangential structure that detects height n phenomena. The telescope conjecture, which predicts that the telescope of a vn self‑map on a finite spectrum coincides with the K(n)‑localization, is intimately connected to redshift: if the telescope conjecture holds at height n, then the redshift from height n to n+1 can be detected by telescopes of vn maps on Thom spectra. Recent work of Burklund–Hahn–Levy–Schlank on the telescope conjecture for the sphere spectrum at height 2 uses the redshift from MU to THH(MU) to construct v2‑periodic elements in the stable homotopy groups of spheres, linking classical cobordism calculations to the cutting edge of chromatic homotopy theory.

  • Confusing the Baum–Connes assembly map in K‑theory with the Farrell–Jones assembly map in L‑theory; they live in different categories and have different geometric inputs.
  • Assuming that topological cyclic homology TC(R) always has the same chromatic height as R; the redshift phenomenon shows TC raises height by one.
  • Thinking that algebraic cobordism Ω* is defined for all fields; Levine–Morel’s construction requires characteristic zero, and positive characteristic versions involve additional hypotheses.
  • Believing that factorization homology of a manifold with coefficients in an En‑algebra depends on the diffeomorphism type of the manifold; it is invariant under framed cobordism, not diffeomorphism.
  • Overlooking that the chromatic redshift conjecture is a theorem for specific cases (MU, KO, tmf) but remains open for general ring spectra.
  • Treating the Conner–Floyd isomorphism MU*(pt) ≅ ℒ as an isomorphism of graded rings without the grading shift; the Lazard ring is concentrated in even degrees, matching MU2*(pt).
  • Mixing up the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence based on MU with the motivic Adams–Novikov spectral sequence based on MGL; the latter computes motivic stable stems and requires a base field of characteristic zero.

Cobordism and the Stable Normal Bundle Classification

The Pontryagin–Thom construction identifies the cobordism group of closed smooth n‑manifolds with the stable homotopy group πn(MO) of the Thom spectrum MO. A crucial refinement replaces the tangent bundle by the stable normal bundle. For a smooth embedding M ℝn+k with normal bundle ν, the Thom collapse map Sn+k → Th(ν) yields an element of πn+k(Th(ν)). Stabilising k → ∞ gives a well‑defined map ΩnO → πn(MO) that is an isomorphism. The stable normal bundle classification theorem states that two manifolds are unoriented cobordant iff their stable normal bundles are fibre homotopy equivalent as spherical fibrations over the manifolds. This equivalence is detected by the Stiefel–Whitney numbers wI[M] = ⟨wi1⋯wir, [M]⟩. Thom proved that the collection of all Stiefel–Whitney numbers forms a complete set of invariants for Ω*O. Consequently the unoriented cobordism ring is a polynomial algebra over ℤ/2 on generators in each positive dimension not of the form 2k−1. The stable normal bundle viewpoint also underlies the construction of the Thom spectrum MO as the colimit of Thom spaces Th(γk) where γk is the universal k‑plane bundle over BO(k). The map BO(k) → BO(k+1) induced by Whitney sum with a trivial line bundle yields a spectrum whose homotopy groups are precisely the unoriented cobordism groups. This description makes the action of the Steenrod algebra on H*(MO; ℤ/2) transparent: the Thom class u ∈ Hk(Th(γk); ℤ/2) satisfies Sqi(u) = wi(γk) u, and the resulting module structure determines the Adams spectral sequence for MO.

Cobordism and the Adams–Novikov Spectral Sequence for the Brown–Peterson Spectrum

The Brown–Peterson spectrum BP at a prime p is a summand of MU(p) whose coefficient ring is BP* = ℤ(p)[v1, v2, …] with |vi| = 2(pi−1). The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (ANSS) based on BP has E2-term ExtBP*BPs,t(BP*, BP*) converging to the p‑local stable homotopy groups of spheres. Because BP is a ring spectrum, the cobordism groups Ω*U ⊗ ℤ(p) map to BP* via the Landweber exact functor theorem. The ANSS for BP is computationally more tractable than the MU‑based ANSS: the Hopf algebroid (BP*, BP*BP) is a quotient of (MU*, MU*MU) obtained by killing the generators ai for i not a power of p. This simplification yields a smaller cobar complex and makes the calculation of the β‑family and the Greek‑letter elements feasible. In particular, the v1-periodic part of the E2-term corresponds to the image of J, while the v2-periodic part detects the first β‑family. The differentials in the ANSS for BP are governed by the chromatic filtration: dr raises chromatic height by at most one. The work of Ravenel, Wilson, and Yagita on the BP‑ANSS at p = 2 produced the first complete picture of the 2‑primary stable stems up to dimension 60. The cobordism interpretation is that a manifold M with a BP‑orientation (i.e. a complex orientation factoring through BP) determines a class in BP*(M), and the ANSS computes the obstruction to realising a given BP‑homology class by a manifold. This link is exploited in the construction of the σ‑orientation MString → tmf, where the string cobordism spectrum MString is BP‑oriented after localisation at 2.

Worked Example: Computing a Stiefel–Whitney Number
Let M = ℝP4. Compute the Stiefel–Whitney number w14[M] and deduce whether ℝP4 bounds an unoriented 5‑manifold.
The total Stiefel–Whitney class of ℝPn is w(ℝPn) = (1 + a)n+1 where a ∈ H1(ℝPn; ℤ/2) is the generator. For n = 4, w = (1 + a)5 = 1 + a + a2 + a3 + a4 (since binomial coefficients mod 2 are 1 for all 0 ≤ i ≤ 4). Hence w1 = a, and w14 = a4. The fundamental class [ℝP4] pairs with a4 to give 1 ∈ ℤ/2 because a4 generates H4(ℝP4; ℤ/2). Therefore w14[M] = 1 ≠ 0. By Thom's theorem, a closed manifold bounds unorientedly iff all its Stiefel–Whitney numbers vanish. Since this number is non‑zero, ℝP4 does not bound any unoriented 5‑manifold.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for a complete set of cobordism invariants, recall Thom's theorem: for unoriented cobordism the Stiefel–Whitney numbers; for oriented cobordism the Pontryagin numbers and Stiefel–Whitney numbers of the stable normal bundle; for complex cobordism the Chern numbers. Write down the relevant characteristic classes, expand the total class using the Whitney sum formula, and evaluate on the fundamental class. Keep track of grading: a characteristic number lives in degree equal to the manifold dimension.

Cobordism and the Morava E‑Theories at Height Two

Morava E‑theory En at a prime p is a complex oriented, even periodic ring spectrum with coefficient ring En* = W(𝔽pn)[[u1, …, un-1]][u±1], where |u| = −2 and W denotes Witt vectors. The associated formal group law has height exactly n. For n = 2, E2 detects v2-periodic phenomena in the stable homotopy category. The Lubin–Tate deformation theory identifies the moduli stack of formal groups of height 2 with the Morava stabiliser group 𝔾2 acting on the deformation space. The homotopy fixed point spectrum E2h𝔾2 is the K(2)‑local sphere. Cobordism enters through the orientation MString → tmf → E2 (after K(2)‑localisation). The sigma orientation of tmf factors through the elliptic curve formal group, which at supersingular primes has height 2. Consequently the string cobordism ring Ω*String maps to π*(E2) and the image captures the v2-periodic families such as the β‑family. The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for E2 has E2-term given by continuous group cohomology Hc*(𝔾2; E2*), and the differentials are controlled by the chromatic resolution. Computations by Goerss–Hopkins–Miller and by Behrens show that the E2-term in low degrees matches the known stable stems, and the first non‑trivial differential is a d3 on a class detecting the Kervaire invariant element θ4. The cobordism interpretation is that a string manifold M with a lift of its stable normal bundle to BString determines a class in Ω*String, and the sigma orientation evaluates the Witten genus on M, landing in tmf and hence in E2. This provides a geometric source for the v2-periodic elements that are otherwise constructed algebraically.

Cobordism and the Topological André–Quillen Homology of Thom Spectra

Topological André–Quillen homology (TAQ) is the homology theory for commutative ring spectra that plays the role of Kähler differentials in algebraic geometry. For a Thom spectrum Mξ associated to a virtual bundle ξ over a space X, TAQ(Mξ) can be computed via the Goodwillie calculus of the identity functor on spaces. The result of Arone–Kjaer and of Baker–Richter is that TAQ(Mξ) ≃ Σ∞ X+ ∧ ξ, where ξ is regarded as a spectrum via the Thom isomorphism. In particular, TAQ(MO) ≃ Σ∞ BO+ ∧ γ, where γ is the universal virtual bundle over BO. This identification yields a spectral sequence from the homology of BO with coefficients in the homotopy groups of the sphere spectrum to the TAQ groups of MO. The E2-term is H*(BO; π*(S)) and the differentials are determined by the action of the Steenrod algebra on the Thom class. The same method applies to MU, MSpin, MString, and other Thom spectra arising from maps X → BO. The TAQ groups control the obstruction theory for lifting a map of ring spectra to a map of E∞ ring spectra. For example, the orientation MString → tmf is an E∞ map; its uniqueness up to homotopy is governed by the vanishing of certain TAQ groups. The computation of TAQ(MU) by Basterra shows that TAQ(MU) ≃ MU ∧ Σ∞ CP∞+, reflecting the fact that MU is the free E∞ ring spectrum on a complex orientation.
