This result underpins the Quillen equivalence between MU and the Lazard ring: the homotopy groups of TAQ(MU) are the indecomposables of the Lazard ring, and the André–Quillen spectral sequence collapses to give the isomorphism MU* ≅ ℒ.

Cobordism and the Cobordism Ring of Manifolds with G‑Structure

Let G be a topological group equipped with a map G → O. A G‑structure on a manifold M is a lift of the stable normal bundle νM : M → BO to BG. The cobordism group ΩnG consists of equivalence classes of closed n‑manifolds with G‑structure, where the equivalence is given by a G‑cobordism (a manifold with G‑structure whose boundary inherits the given structures). The Pontryagin–Thom construction identifies ΩnG with πn(MG), where MG is the Thom spectrum of the virtual bundle −γ over BG (γ is the pullback of the universal bundle). The ring structure comes from Cartesian product of manifolds, which corresponds to the smash product of Thom spectra. When G → O is a fibration, the Thom spectrum MG is a module over MO, and the forgetful map Ω*G → Ω*O is a ring homomorphism. Important examples include G = SO (oriented cobordism, spectrum MSO), G = Spin (spin cobordism, MSpin), G = String (string cobordism, MString), and G = Pinc (Pinc cobordism). The computation of Ω*G often proceeds via the Adams spectral sequence for MG, using the known cohomology of BG as a module over the Steenrod algebra. For G = Spin, Anderson–Brown–Peterson showed that Ω*Spin ⊗ ℤ[1/2] is a polynomial algebra on generators in dimensions 8k+4, while at the prime 2 there are additional 2‑torsion generators detected by the KO‑theory orientation MSpin → KO. For G = String, the ring Ω*String is not polynomial; it contains the Witten genus as a ring homomorphism to the ring of modular forms, and the kernel is related to the homotopy groups of tmf. The Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTG = Σ−d MG (where d is the dimension of the universal bundle) appears in the homology of the moduli space of G‑manifolds via the Galatius–Madsen–Tillmann–Weiss theorem, linking cobordism groups to mapping class groups.

  • Confusing the stable normal bundle with the tangent bundle when applying the Pontryagin–Thom construction; the collapse map uses the normal bundle.
  • Assuming that all Stiefel–Whitney numbers vanish for a manifold that bounds orientedly; oriented cobordism requires Pontryagin numbers and Stiefel–Whitney numbers of the normal bundle to vanish.
  • Treating the Adams–Novikov E2-term for BP as identical to that for MU; the Hopf algebroid (BP*, BP*BP) is a proper quotient.
  • Believing that Morava E‑theory En is a Thom spectrum; it is not, but it receives orientations from Thom spectra such as MString.
  • Overlooking that TAQ of a Thom spectrum is not the same as the homology of the base space; the Thom isomorphism shifts degrees by the virtual dimension.
  • Thinking that the cobordism ring Ω*G is always polynomial; for G = String and G = Pinc it has torsion and non‑polynomial relations.
  • Mixing up the Madsen–Tillmann spectrum MTG with the Thom spectrum MG; MTG is a desuspension of MG and represents the homology of the moduli space, not the cobordism groups directly.

Cobordism and the Landweber–Novikov Operations: The Algebra of Stable Cohomology Operations in MU*

The stable cohomology operations for complex cobordism form the Landweber–Novikov algebra S, a graded algebra over the Lazard ring ℒ ≅ MU* that acts naturally on MU*(−). Each operation is determined by its effect on the universal complex orientation x ∈ MU2(ℂP∞). Writing the formal group law as F(x, y) = x + y + Σi,j≥1 aij xi yj, the Landweber–Novikov operations sω are indexed by multi-indices ω = (ω1, ω2, …) of finite support. The operation sω sends x to the coefficient of t|ω|+1 in the formal power series (∂/∂t) logF(t)ω1 (∂2/∂t2 logF(t))ω2 ⋯, where logF is the formal logarithm of F. The algebra S is the free left ℒ-module on the sω, with composition governed by the Landweber–Novikov relations. These relations are equivalent to the statement that the operations commute with the action of the formal group law. The dual of S is the Hopf algebroid (MU*, MU*MU), where MU*MU ≅ MU*[b1, b2, …] with |bi| = 2i. The coaction MU* → MU*MU ⊗MU* MU* encodes the change of coordinate on the universal formal group. The Landweber–Novikov algebra acts on the Adams–Novikov E2-term ExtMU*MU(MU*, MU*), and the differentials in the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence are S-module maps. A key computational tool is the Novikov spectral sequence for the sphere spectrum, whose E2-term is ExtS(MU*, MU*) and which converges to the stable homotopy groups of spheres. The algebra S is not commutative; its center is precisely the Lazard ring ℒ. The operations sω for |ω| = 1 are the primitive operations corresponding to the coefficients of the formal group law. For the multiplicative formal group law (corresponding to K-theory), the Landweber–Novikov algebra reduces to the algebra of stable operations in K-theory generated by the Adams operations ψk. The Landweber–Novikov algebra also controls the obstruction theory for realizing a formal group law over a ring R as the formal group of a complex-oriented ring spectrum: the obstructions lie in the André–Quillen cohomology of R with coefficients in the S-module structure.

Cobordism and the Hopf Algebroid (MU*, MU*MU): Formal Groups and the Moduli Stack

The pair (MU*, MU*MU) is the Hopf algebroid that represents the moduli stack Mfg of one-dimensional commutative formal groups. Concretely, MU* ≅ ℤ[a1, a2, …] is the Lazard ring, and MU*MU ≅ MU*[b1, b2, …] where the bi encode the coefficients of a strict isomorphism between two formal group laws. The left unit ηL: MU* → MU*MU sends ai to the coefficients of the source formal group law; the right unit ηR sends ai to the coefficients of the target formal group law after the coordinate change x ↦ x + Σ bi xi+1. The comultiplication Δ: MU*MU → MU*MU ⊗MU* MU*MU corresponds to composition of strict isomorphisms, the counit ε: MU*MU → MU* evaluates at the identity isomorphism, and the conjugation χ: MU*MU → MU*MU corresponds to inversion of isomorphisms. The stack Mfg is the quotient of the affine scheme Spec MU* by the groupoid action encoded by this Hopf algebroid. A quasi-coherent sheaf on Mfg is equivalent to a comodule over (MU*, MU*MU), i.e., an MU*MU-comodule. The Adams–Novikov E2-term ExtMU*MU(MU*, MU*) is the cohomology of this stack with coefficients in the structure sheaf. The chromatic filtration of Mfg by height of formal groups induces the chromatic filtration of the stable homotopy category. At height 1, the stack is represented by the multiplicative formal group over ℤ(p); the corresponding Hopf algebroid is (ℤ(p)[u±1], ℤ(p)[u±1][t±1]) which gives the Adams spectral sequence for K-theory. At height 2, the stack is represented by the universal deformation of a supersingular elliptic curve, leading to Morava E-theory E2 and the elliptic cohomology spectrum tmf. The Landweber exact functor theorem states that a flat MU*MU-comodule M that is Landweber exact (i.e., the sequence p, v1, v2, … is regular on M) gives a homology theory represented by a spectrum. The Hopf algebroid structure also controls the descent spectral sequence for the map of E∞ ring spectra MU → E for any complex-oriented E; the E2-term is ExtMU*MU(MU*, E*E ⊗MU* MU*). The Goerss–Hopkins obstruction theory for realizing a Hopf algebroid map as a map of E∞ ring spectra uses the André–Quillen cohomology of the Hopf algebroid.

Worked computation: Landweber–Novikov operation s(1) on the universal formal group law
Let F(x, y) = x + y + a11xy + a12xy2 + a21x2y + … be the universal formal group law over the Lazard ring ℒ = ℤ[aij]. Compute the Landweber–Novikov operation s(1) (corresponding to the multi-index ω = (1, 0, 0, …)) acting on the universal orientation x ∈ MU2(ℂP∞).
The formal logarithm logF(t) satisfies d/dt logF(t) = 1 / (∂F/∂y)(t, 0). For the universal formal group law, (∂F/∂y)(t, 0) = 1 + a11t + a12t2 + …. Thus d/dt logF(t) = 1 − a11t + (a112 − a12)t2 + …. The operation s(1) is defined by s(1)(x) = coefficient of t2 in (d/dt) logF(t). From the expansion above, the coefficient of t2 is a112 − a12. Therefore s(1)(x) = a112 − a12 ∈ ℒ. In terms of the standard generators vi of MU* (where v1 = a11, v2 = a112 − a12 modulo decomposables), this is precisely v2 modulo lower filtration. This computation shows that s(1) detects the first non-trivial chromatic information beyond the formal group law coefficients themselves.
Exam strategy: Hopf algebroids and the Adams–Novikov E2-term
When asked to identify the Adams–Novikov E2-term for a complex-oriented theory E, write it as ExtMU*MU(MU*, E*E ⊗MU* MU*). Remember that MU*MU = MU*[b1, b2, …] with |bi| = 2i. The left unit ηL is the inclusion MU* → MU*MU; the right unit ηR sends the universal formal group law coefficients ai to the coefficients of the transformed law under x ↦ x + Σ bixi+1. For the sphere spectrum, E*E = MU*, so the E2-term is ExtMU*MU(MU*, MU*). The cobar complex for this Ext group has terms MU*MU ⊗MU* ⋯ ⊗MU* MU*MU. The differential is the alternating sum of face maps induced by the comultiplication. The chromatic spectral sequence arises from filtering this cobar complex by the height of the formal group. Memorize the first few differentials: d1(h1) = 0, d1(h2) = h12 at p = 2, etc. The Landweber–Novikov algebra S acts on this Ext group; the action of s(1) corresponds to multiplication by v2 in the associated graded.

Cobordism and the Adams Operations in Complex Cobordism: The Lambda-Ring Structure and the Conner–Floyd Isomorphism

The complex cobordism ring MU* carries a natural lambda-ring structure induced by the exterior power operations on vector bundles. For a complex vector bundle ξ, the total lambda operation λt(ξ) = Σ λi(ξ) ti satisfies the splitting principle and the Whitney sum formula λt(ξ ⊕ η) = λt(ξ) λt(η). These operations descend to stable operations on MU* and make MU* a special lambda-ring. The Adams operations ψk are defined by the Newton identities ψk = Σi=1k (−1)i−1 i λi ψk−i with ψ1 = id. On the Lazard ring ℒ ≅ MU*, the Adams operations act on the formal group law coefficients by ψk(aij) = ki+j−1 aij modulo decomposables. The Conner–Floyd isomorphism MU*(X) ⊗MU* ℤ[u±1] ≅ K*(X) (where |u| = −2) is a map of lambda-rings when K* is equipped with its standard Adams operations. Under this isomorphism, the MU* Adams operations ψk correspond to the K-theory Adams operations ψk. The lambda-ring structure on MU* is closely related to the Landweber–Novikov operations: the operation s(1) corresponds to the derivative of the lambda operations. The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for the sphere spectrum is a spectral sequence of lambda-rings; the differentials are not lambda-ring maps but satisfy certain compatibility conditions. The Hurewicz image of the stable homotopy groups of spheres in MU* is a lambda-subring. The nilpotence theorem of Devinatz–Hopkins–Smith implies that any element in the kernel of the Hurewicz map π*(S) → MU* is nilpotent in the lambda-ring sense. The periodicity theorem implies that for each height n formal group law, there exists a vn-self-map that is a lambda-ring map after suitable localization. The lambda-ring structure also controls the power operations in Morava E-theory: the En power operations are lifts of the Adams operations on the Lubin–Tate formal group law. For the Brown–Peterson spectrum BP, the lambda-ring structure is not defined integrally but exists after localization at a prime p; the Adams operations ψk for k prime to p act on BP* and commute with the Landweber–Novikov operations. The Conner–Floyd isomorphism extends to an isomorphism of Adams spectral sequences: the Adams spectral sequence for MU (based on the Hopf algebroid (MU*, MU*MU)) maps to the Adams spectral sequence for K-theory (based on (K*, K*K)) and this map is an isomorphism after inverting the Bott element u.

Cobordism and the Cobordism Ring of Almost Complex Manifolds: The Hattori–Stong Theorem

The cobordism ring of stably almost complex manifolds Ω*U is isomorphic to the complex cobordism ring MU* via the Pontryagin–Thom construction. However, the subring generated by almost complex manifolds (manifolds with an integrable almost complex structure on the stable tangent bundle) is a proper subring. The Hattori–Stong theorem characterizes the image of the forgetful map Ω*AC → Ω*U from the cobordism ring of almost complex manifolds to the cobordism ring of stably almost complex manifolds. A stably almost complex manifold M bounds an almost complex manifold if and only if all its Chern numbers vanish and, additionally, certain Todd genus conditions hold. Specifically, for a stably almost complex manifold M of dimension 2n, the Todd genus Td(M) = ⟨Td(TM), [M]⟩ must be an integer. The Todd genus is the genus associated to the formal power series x/(1 − e−x). The Hattori–Stong theorem states that a stably almost complex manifold is an almost complex boundary if and only if all its Chern numbers vanish and its Todd genus is zero. This result is proved using the Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch theorem and the fact that the Todd genus of an almost complex manifold is the holomorphic Euler characteristic χ(M, OM). The Chern numbers of an almost complex manifold satisfy the congruence relations of Conner–Floyd: for any partition I of n, the Chern number cI[M] is congruent modulo 2 to the Stiefel–Whitney number w2I[M] of the underlying real manifold. The Todd genus of a complex projective variety is the arithmetic genus. The Â-genus of a spin manifold is related to the Todd genus of an associated almost complex structure. The Hattori–Stong theorem has been generalized to equivariant cobordism: for a torus action, the fixed-point data determines the almost complex cobordism class. The cobordism ring of complex projective manifolds is the subring of Ω*U generated by the classes of ℂPn; it is a polynomial ring on the classes of ℂP2k+1 for k ≥ 1. The cobordism ring of toric varieties is the whole ring Ω*U because every stably almost complex manifold is cobordant to a toric variety (a result of Buchstaber and Ray). The cobordism ring of symplectic manifolds is also the whole ring Ω*U by a result of Gromov and Eliashberg. The cobordism ring of contact manifolds in odd dimensions is related to the cobordism ring of their symplectizations.

Cobordism and the Cobordism Classification of Manifolds with Special Holonomy: G2 and Spin(7) Cobordism

Manifolds with special holonomy — G2 in dimension 7 and Spin(7) in dimension 8 — admit natural cobordism theories. A G2-structure on a 7-manifold is a reduction of the frame bundle to G2 ⊂ SO(7), equivalent to a positive 3-form φ satisfying a non-degeneracy condition. A Spin(7)-structure on an 8-manifold is a reduction to Spin(7) ⊂ SO(8), equivalent to a positive 4-form Φ. The cobordism groups Ω*G2 and Ω*Spin(7) are defined using bordisms that carry the respective structures extending those on the boundary. The Pontryagin–Thom construction identifies these with the homotopy groups of the Thom spectra MG2 and MSpin(7). The maps BG2 → BSO(7) and BSpin(7) → BSO(8) induce maps of Thom spectra MG2 → MSO(7) and MSpin(7) → MSO(8). The characteristic numbers for G2-manifolds are the Pontryagin numbers p12[M], p2[M] and the φ-number ⟨φ ∧ p1, [M]⟩. For Spin(7)-manifolds, the characteristic numbers are the Pontryagin numbers p12[M], p2[M] and the Φ-number ⟨Φ ∧ Φ, [M]⟩. The index theorem for the Dirac operator on a Spin(7)-manifold gives the Â-genus, which is a linear combination of Pontryagin numbers. The cobordism ring Ω*G2 ⊗ ℚ is a polynomial algebra on generators in dimensions 7, 14, 21, … corresponding to the homogeneous spaces G2/SU(3) ≅ S6 (not G2), G2/SO(4), etc. The torsion in Ω*G2 is detected by the η-invariant of the Dirac operator coupled to the G2-structure. The cobordism group Ω7G2 is isomorphic to ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2, generated by the 7-sphere with its standard G2-structure and the Joyce manifold. The cobordism group Ω8Spin(7) contains the Spin(7)-manifold constructed by Joyce as a generator. The Stolz conjecture on positive scalar curvature implies that a simply connected spin manifold with positive scalar curvature is null-cobordant in the spin cobordism ring; for G2 and Spin(7) manifolds, the existence of a torsion-free structure implies Ricci-flatness, hence zero scalar curvature, so they are not obstructed by the Stolz conjecture. The moduli space of torsion-free G2-structures on a fixed manifold is smooth and its dimension is given by the third Betti number. The cobordism invariants of G2-manifolds are related to the M-theory partition function in physics. The cobordism classification of 7-manifolds with G2-structure up to diffeomorphism is not complete; the Kreck–Stolz invariants (η-invariants of the Dirac and signature operators) give a complete set of invariants for 7-manifolds with G2-structure up to oriented diffeomorphism when combined with the cohomology ring and the linking form.

  • Confusing the Landweber–Novikov algebra S with the dual Steenrod algebra for MU; S is the algebra of stable cohomology operations, while the dual Steenrod algebra is MU*MU as a comodule algebra.
  • Assuming the Adams operations ψk on MU* are defined for all integers k without localization; they require localization at primes not dividing k to be well-defined on the integral Lazard ring.
  • Treating the Conner–Floyd isomorphism as an isomorphism of rings without the Bott element; it is an isomorphism MU*(X) ⊗MU* ℤ[u±1] ≅ K*(X) where u is the Bott class of degree −2.
  • Believing that every stably almost complex manifold is cobordant to an almost complex manifold; the Hattori–Stong theorem gives the precise obstruction (vanishing Chern numbers and Todd genus).
  • Overlooking that the cobordism rings of G2 and Spin(7) manifolds are not polynomial over ℤ; they contain torsion detected by η-invariants and are not fully computed in high dimensions.
  • Mixing up the Todd genus (associated to x/(1−e−x)) with the Â-genus (associated to √x/2 / sinh(√x/2)); they coincide on almost complex manifolds but differ on spin manifolds.
  • Assuming the Pontryagin–Thom construction for G2-structures uses the tangent bundle; it uses the stable normal bundle, and the G2-structure must be on the normal bundle for the collapse map to be well-defined.

Cobordism and the Positive Scalar Curvature Obstruction

The existence of a metric of positive scalar curvature on a closed manifold is a classic problem that interacts deeply with cobordism theory. Gromov and Lawson proved that if a spin manifold admits a metric of positive scalar curvature then its Â‑genus vanishes. Stolz later showed that for simply connected spin manifolds of dimension at least five the converse holds: vanishing of the Â‑genus is equivalent to the existence of a positive scalar curvature metric. In the language of cobordism this means that the spin cobordism class of such a manifold lies in the kernel of the Â‑genus homomorphism Ω_*Spin → ℤ. The Gromov–Lawson–Rosenberg conjecture, proved in many cases by the work of Rosenberg and others, identifies the obstruction to positive scalar curvature on a non‑simply connected spin manifold with the index of the Dirac operator on the universal cover, which lives in the K‑theory of the group C*‑algebra. This index defines a homomorphism from the spin cobordism group of the classifying space BG to the analytic K‑theory group K_*(C*_r G). Manifolds that are null‑cobordant in this relative sense automatically admit positive scalar curvature metrics. The interplay between the geometric topology of the manifold and the analytic index is a central theme in modern cobordism theory.

From the computational side, the spin cobordism ring Ω_*Spin is known to be a polynomial algebra over ℤ on generators in dimensions 1, 2, 4, 5, 6, 8, … after inverting 2. The Â‑genus picks out the generator in dimension 4 (the K3 surface) and in dimension 8 (the quaternionic projective plane). Consequently any spin manifold whose cobordism class is a polynomial without these generators automatically has vanishing Â‑genus and, by Stolz’s theorem, admits a metric of positive scalar curvature provided it is simply connected and of high enough dimension. This gives a practical cobordism‑theoretic criterion that can be checked by evaluating a finite list of characteristic numbers.

Cobordism and the Cobordism of Hyperkähler Manifolds

Hyperkähler manifolds are Riemannian manifolds of real dimension 4n whose holonomy group is contained in Sp(n). They are automatically spin and carry a triple of parallel complex structures satisfying the quaternionic relations. The Pontryagin–Thom construction identifies the cobordism groups of hyperkähler manifolds with the homotopy groups of a Thom spectrum MSp(n) built from the universal Sp(n)‑bundle. Because the inclusion Sp(n) → Spin(4n) induces a map of Thom spectra MSp(n) → MSpin(4n), every hyperkähler cobordism class maps to a spin cobordism class. The image of this map is known to be contained in the subring generated by the quaternionic projective spaces ℍP^k. In particular, the cobordism class of a hyperkähler manifold is determined by its Pontryagin numbers, and all Chern numbers vanish because the canonical bundle is trivial. The first non‑trivial hyperkähler manifold is the K3 surface (dimension 4), which generates the ℤ summand in Ω4Spin detected by the Â‑genus. Higher dimensional examples such as Hilbert schemes of points on K3 or generalized Kummer varieties give further generators in dimensions 8, 12, … . The rational hyperkähler cobordism ring is a polynomial algebra on these generators, while the integral torsion is still an active area of research.

An important consequence of the hyperkähler condition is the vanishing of the Todd genus, which coincides with the Â‑genus on almost complex manifolds. Since hyperkähler manifolds are spin, the Â‑genus is the only genus that can be non‑zero. The Rozansky–Witten invariants, which are topological invariants derived from the hyperkähler structure, factor through the cobordism class and provide a bridge to topological quantum field theory. In the physics literature, hyperkähler cobordism appears in the classification of supersymmetric sigma models, where the target space must be hyperkähler for N = 4 supersymmetry in two dimensions.

Worked Example: Detecting Positive Scalar Curvature via Cobordism
Let M be a simply connected closed spin 8‑manifold with Pontryagin numbers p12[M] = 4 and p2[M] = 7. Determine whether M can admit a metric of positive scalar curvature.
The Â‑genus in dimension 8 is given by Â(M) = (7p2 - p12)/5760. Substituting the given numbers: (7·7 - 4)/5760 = (49 - 4)/5760 = 45/5760 = 3/384 = 1/128. Since the Â‑genus is non‑zero, Stolz’s theorem implies that M cannot admit a metric of positive scalar curvature. Equivalently, the spin cobordism class of M has a non‑zero component along the generator detected by the Â‑genus (the quaternionic projective plane ℍP2), so it is not null‑cobordant in the kernel of Â.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks about positive scalar curvature on a spin manifold, first compute the Â‑genus from the given Pontryagin numbers. If the manifold is simply connected and of dimension ≥ 5, a non‑zero Â‑genus is an immediate obstruction; a zero Â‑genus is sufficient for existence. Remember that the Â‑genus formula changes with dimension — memorise the low‑dimensional expressions (4, 8, 12) to save time.

Cobordism and the Cobordism of Calabi–Yau Manifolds

Calabi–Yau manifolds are compact Kähler manifolds with trivial canonical bundle and holonomy exactly SU(n) (or a subgroup thereof). They are spin for all n and have vanishing first Chern class. The Pontryagin–Thom construction applied to the structure group SU(n) yields a Thom spectrum MSU(n) which maps to MSpin(2n) via the inclusion SU(n) → Spin(2n). The rational SU(n) cobordism ring is a polynomial algebra on the complex projective spaces ℂP2k+1 for k ≥ 1, exactly as for complex cobordism, because every stably almost complex manifold is cobordant to an SU‑manifold after a suitable stabilization. However, the integral SU‑cobordism groups contain torsion that is not present in the complex cobordism ring; this torsion is detected by the Chern numbers modulo certain primes. For Calabi–Yau manifolds the Chern numbers satisfy additional relations coming from the triviality of the canonical bundle, for instance c1 = 0 forces all Chern numbers containing c1 to vanish. Consequently the only potentially non‑zero Chern numbers are those built from c2, c3, … . In complex dimension three the only independent Chern number is c3[M], which equals the Euler characteristic. The cobordism class of a Calabi–Yau threefold is therefore determined by its Euler characteristic modulo the image of the SU‑cobordism group in the spin cobordism group.

Mirror symmetry predicts that Calabi–Yau manifolds come in pairs with exchanged Hodge numbers, and this duality respects cobordism: the two mirrors are cobordant as SU‑manifolds. In the physics literature, the Calabi–Yau cobordism group appears in the classification of D‑brane charges in type II string theory, where the charge group is identified with the K‑theory of the Calabi–Yau, and the map from cobordism to K‑theory is the Conner–Floyd isomorphism after inverting the Bott element. Recent work on the Swampland program uses the fact that certain cobordism classes of Calabi–Yau manifolds are obstructed by the absence of a consistent quantum gravity completion, linking cobordism invariants to the consistency of effective field theories.

Cobordism and the Cobordism of Manifolds with Fivebrane Structures

A Fivebrane structure on a manifold is a lift of the structure group of the stable normal bundle from String(n) to its 3‑connected cover Fivebrane(n). This is the next step in the Whitehead tower after Spin, String, and Fivebrane. The existence of a Fivebrane structure is equivalent to the vanishing of the first two fractional Pontryagin classes ½p1 and ⅙p2. The Thom spectrum MFivebrane is defined as the Thom spectrum of the universal Fivebrane bundle, and its homotopy groups are the Fivebrane cobordism groups Ω_*Fivebrane. There is a natural map MFivebrane → MString → MSpin → MO, so every Fivebrane cobordism class determines a string, spin, and unoriented cobordism class. The rational Fivebrane cobordism ring is a polynomial algebra on generators in dimensions 8, 12, 16, … corresponding to the homogeneous spaces Fivebrane(n)/String(n) and higher stages. Integral computations are scarce; the first non‑trivial group Ω8Fivebrane is known to be ℤ generated by the quaternionic projective plane ℍP2 equipped with its canonical Fivebrane structure (which exists because ½p1(ℍP2) = 0 and ⅙p2(ℍP2) = 0). The Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence can be adapted to compute the torsion in Ω_*Fivebrane at the prime 2 and 3, revealing a pattern of 2‑torsion in dimensions congruent to 1, 2 mod 8 and 3‑torsion in dimensions congruent to 3, 4 mod 8. Fivebrane cobordism appears in the mathematical formulation of the Green–Schwarz anomaly cancellation mechanism for the heterotic string, where the Fivebrane worldvolume anomaly is captured by a class in Ω_*Fivebrane.

Cobordism and the Cobordism of Manifolds with Special Holonomy Beyond G₂ and Spin(7)

Besides G₂ and Spin(7), the Berger classification lists two further irreducible holonomy groups that can occur on simply connected Riemannian manifolds: SU(n) (Calabi–Yau) and Sp(n) (hyperkähler). Their cobordism theories have been discussed above. A less studied but mathematically rich case is the holonomy group Spin(9) acting on the 16‑dimensional Cayley plane ℂaP2 = F4/Spin(9). Manifolds with Spin(9) holonomy are extremely rare; the only known compact example is the Cayley plane itself. The cobordism group Ω_*Spin(9) is defined using the Thom spectrum MSpin(9) built from the universal Spin(9) bundle. The inclusion Spin(9) → SO(16) induces a map MSpin(9) → MSO(16). Characteristic numbers for Spin(9) manifolds are the Pontryagin numbers p14, p12 p2, p22, p1 p3, p4 together with a new invariant coming from the invariant 8‑form on the Cayley plane. The rational Spin(9) cobordism ring is generated by the Cayley plane in dimension 16 and its products with lower‑dimensional Spin(9) manifolds (which do not exist). Hence Ω_*Spin(9) ⊗ ℚ ≅ ℚ[x16] with x16 represented by ℂaP2. The torsion in Ω_*Spin(9) is largely unknown; the Adams spectral sequence for MSpin(9) suggests 2‑torsion in dimensions 17, 18, 20, … . The Cayley plane is known to be a generator of the image of J in the 15‑stem, linking Spin(9) cobordism to the stable homotopy groups of spheres. This connection makes Spin(9) cobordism a testing ground for the telescope conjecture at the prime 2.

  • Confusing the Â‑genus obstruction for positive scalar curvature with the vanishing of the Â‑genus on all spin manifolds; the obstruction only applies to simply connected manifolds of dimension ≥ 5.
  • Assuming that every hyperkähler manifold is null‑cobordant in spin cobordism; the K3 surface represents a non‑zero class detected by the Â‑genus.
  • Treating the SU‑cobordism ring as identical to the complex cobordism ring integrally; SU‑cobordism has extra torsion detected by Chern numbers modulo primes.
  • Believing that a Fivebrane structure exists whenever ½p1 = 0; the second fractional Pontryagin class ⅙p2 must also vanish.
  • Overlooking that the Cayley plane ℂaP2 is the only known compact manifold with Spin(9) holonomy, so the Spin(9) cobordism ring is essentially generated by a single element in dimension 16.
  • Mixing up the Conner–Floyd isomorphism for MU with the isomorphism for MSU; the latter requires inverting the Bott element after mapping to K‑theory.
  • Assuming that the Stolz theorem on positive scalar curvature holds for non‑spin manifolds; the theorem is specific to spin manifolds and uses the Dirac operator.

Cobordism and the Sigma-Orientation of TMF: From Spin to Topological Modular Forms

The Witten genus defines a ring homomorphism from the string cobordism ring ΩString* to the ring of integral modular forms MF*(SL2(ℤ)). Topological modular forms, denoted tmf, is a connective spectrum built from the compactified moduli stack of elliptic curves Mell and a sheaf of E∞-ring spectra Otop. The sigma-orientation is a map of ring spectra σ: MSpin → tmf constructed by Ando, Hopkins, and Strickland. It refines the Witten genus in the sense that the composition MSpin → tmf → tmf[1/6] ≃ TMF[1/6] recovers the Witten genus after inverting 6. The construction uses the theory of power operations on elliptic curves: for an elliptic curve C over a ring R, the formal group Ĉ carries a canonical θ-structure, and the sigma-function σC(z) provides a trivialization of the line bundle associated to the universal elliptic curve. This trivialization yields an orientation of MSpin in tmf-cohomology. The homotopy groups π*tmf are known through a range: they are 2- and 3-torsion away from the image of the Witten genus, and the sigma-orientation detects the α-family and β-family in the stable homotopy groups of spheres via the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for tmf. The kernel of σ*: ΩSpin* → tmf* is precisely the ideal generated by manifolds with positive scalar curvature, a theorem of Stolz and Teichner conjecturally extended to all dimensions. The sigma-orientation also factors through MString → tmf, and the induced map on homotopy groups ΩString* → tmf* is surjective onto the subring of modular forms of level 1 with integral q-expansion. This makes tmf the universal recipient for elliptic genera of string manifolds.

Cobordism and the Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel Solution of the Kervaire Invariant One Problem

The Kervaire invariant one problem asks for which dimensions n there exists a framed manifold of dimension n with Kervaire invariant 1. Kervaire and Milnor showed in 1963 that such manifolds can exist only in dimensions 2, 6, 14, 30, 62, and possibly 126. Browder (1969) reduced the problem to the existence of a certain element θj in the 2-primary stable homotopy groups of spheres π2j+1−2S for j = 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6. The elements θ1, θ2, θ3, θ4, θ5 were known to exist (corresponding to ℝP2, the Kervaire manifold in dimension 10, and generators in dimensions 14, 30, 62). The case j = 6 (dimension 126) remained open for 40 years. Hill, Hopkins, and Ravenel (2009–2016) proved that θ6 does not exist, so no framed 126-manifold has Kervaire invariant 1. Their proof constructs a 2-local spectrum Ω with a map S0 → Ω that detects the θj elements. The spectrum Ω is built as a Thom spectrum of a map ΩSU(2) → Pic(Sp) classifying a certain virtual bundle, and its homotopy groups are computed via a slice spectral sequence. The key innovation is the use of the slice filtration in equivariant stable homotopy theory for the group C8, which allows the computation of π*Ω and shows that the would-be θ6 supports a non-trivial differential. The cobordism interpretation: the framed cobordism group Ωfr126 ≅ π126S has no element of Kervaire invariant 1. The solution uses the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for the sphere, the chromatic filtration, and the fact that the Kervaire invariant one elements would lie in the β-family at height 2. The non-existence of θ6 implies that the only dimensions with Kervaire invariant one manifolds are 2, 6, 14, 30, 62.

Worked Problem: Detecting the Kervaire Invariant via the Adams–Novikov E2-Term
Explain why the Kervaire invariant one element θj ∈ π2j+1−2S corresponds to a class in the Adams–Novikov E2-term ExtBP*BP1,2j+1−2(BP*, BP*) represented by the invariant tj in the Hopf algebroid (BP*, BP*BP). Why does the Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel theorem imply that the class t6 does not survive to E∞?
The Kervaire invariant one element θj is detected in the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence by the class hj2 in the classical Adams SS, which corresponds under the change-of-rings isomorphism to the class tj ∈ ExtBP*BP1,2j+1−2. Here tj is the invariant polynomial generator in BP*BP = BP*[t1, t2, …] with |tj| = 2(pj−1) at p=2. For j=6, t6 has degree 126. The Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel proof constructs a map of spectra S0 → Ω where Ω is a C8-equivariant spectrum whose homotopy groups are computed by a slice spectral sequence. The element θ6 would map to a non-zero class in π126Ω, but the slice differential d3(τ4v232) = τv1v231 (in appropriate notation) kills the potential target. Equivalently, in the Adams–Novikov SS for the sphere, the class t6 supports a differential d3(t6) = v1h2g (or similar) which is non-zero because of the C8-equivariant geometry of Ω. Thus t6 does not survive to E∞, so θ6 = 0 in π126S.
Exam Strategy: Kervaire Invariant and Chromatic Height
Remember that the Kervaire invariant one elements θj live at chromatic height 2 (they are in the β-family). The HHR proof uses C8-equivariant homotopy theory because the Morava stabilizer group at height 2 and p=2 contains a copy of C8. For exam questions, focus on the dimensions (2, 6, 14, 30, 62, 126), the Browder reduction to θj, and the fact that the solution uses the slice spectral sequence for a Thom spectrum Ω built from ΩSU(2). Do not confuse the Kervaire invariant (a ℤ/2 invariant of framed manifolds) with the Arf invariant (a ℤ/2 invariant of quadratic forms over ℤ/2) or the Rokhlin invariant (a ℤ/16 invariant of spin 3-manifolds).

Cobordism and the Surgery Exact Sequence: Normal Invariants, L-Groups, and the Structure Set

The surgery exact sequence, developed by Browder, Novikov, Sullivan, and Wall, classifies manifolds up to homotopy equivalence within a given homotopy type. For a closed Poincaré complex X of dimension n ≥ 5, the sequence is a long exact sequence of pointed sets: … → ℒn+1(π1(X)) → 𝒮(X) → [X, G/O] → ℒn(π1(X)). Here 𝒮(X) is the structure set of homotopy equivalences M → X from closed manifolds M, modulo an equivalence relation (s-cobordism). The normal invariants [X, G/O] are homotopy classes of maps from X to the classifying space G/O for stable normal bundles; they classify degree-one normal maps (M, ∂M) → (X, ∂X). The surgery obstruction groups ℒ*(π) (Wall's L-groups) are the algebraic obstructions to doing surgery on a normal map to make it a homotopy equivalence. For π = 1, ℒ4k(1) ≅ ℤ (signature), ℒ4k+2(1) ≅ ℤ/2 (Kervaire invariant), and ℒodd(1) = 0. The map [X, G/O] → ℒn(π) is the surgery obstruction map; its kernel is the image of 𝒮(X). The map ℒn+1(π) → 𝒮(X) realizes an element of the L-group as the surgery obstruction of a normal map on a manifold with boundary, whose boundary gives an element of 𝒮(X). The sequence is exact in the sense of pointed sets: the preimage of the basepoint in ℒn(π) is exactly the image of 𝒮(X). The s-cobordism theorem identifies the structure set with the set of h-cobordism classes of manifolds homotopy equivalent to X. For simply connected X, the surgery exact sequence reduces to the classification of manifolds by their normal invariants and signature/Kervaire invariant. The assembly map in algebraic K- and L-theory relates the surgery exact sequence to the Novikov conjecture: the rational injectivity of the assembly map A: H*(Bπ; ℒ(ℤ)) → ℒ*(ℤπ) implies the Novikov conjecture on homotopy invariance of higher signatures.

Cobordism and the Ando–Hopkins–Strickland Theory of Orientations: Elliptic Curves and Power Operations

A general framework for constructing orientations of Thom spectra was developed by Ando, Hopkins, and Strickland (2001). An orientation of a ring spectrum E for a Thom spectrum MG (where G is a topological group with a map G → O) is a map of ring spectra MG → E. For G = U, this is a complex orientation, classified by a formal group law over π0E. For G = Spin, a spin orientation is a map MSpin → E; such orientations correspond to trivializations of the line bundle associated to the universal spin bundle over the formal group of E. The AHS theory uses the language of power operations: for an E∞-ring spectrum E, the extended power E∧nhΣn carries operations that encode the structure of the formal group. An elliptic curve C over a ring R gives a formal group Ĉ and a sigma-function σ: Ĉ → 𝔾m which is a homomorphism of formal groups up to a quadratic correction. This sigma-function provides a trivialization of the line bundle ω⊗2 on the moduli stack of elliptic curves, which is exactly the data needed for a string orientation MString → tmf. For spin orientations, the relevant data is a trivialization of ω⊗8 (the line bundle of modular forms of weight 4), which exists over ℤ[1/2] and gives the Witten genus MSpin → tmf[1/2]. The AHS theory also explains the Conner–Floyd isomorphism MU*(X) ⊗MU* KU* ≅ KU*(X) as the statement that the map MU → KU is the universal complex orientation for periodic complex orientations. The lambda-ring structure on MU* comes from the power operations on the formal group, and the Adams operations ψk act on the formal group law by ψk(x) = [k]F(x) where [k]F is the k-series of the formal group. This framework unifies the construction of orientations for MU, MSpin, MString, MFivebrane, and higher Whitehead tower stages, showing that each stage corresponds to trivializing a higher power of the line bundle ω on the moduli stack of elliptic curves (or higher chromatic analogues).

Cobordism and the Chromatic Splitting Conjecture: The Telescope Conjecture and the Decomposition of the K(n)-Local Sphere

The chromatic splitting conjecture, formulated by Ravenel and others, describes the decomposition of the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S0 into a wedge of suspensions of the Morava E-theory spectrum En and its Galois conjugates. At height n=1 and p odd, LK(1)S0 ≃ LK(1)KOp ∨ ΣLK(1)KOp, a theorem of Adams and Bousfield. At height n=2, the conjecture predicts a similar splitting involving E2 and its Galois conjugates under the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾2. The telescope conjecture, a related statement, asserts that the Bousfield localization with respect to the telescope of a vn-self-map of a finite spectrum (the "telescope localization") agrees with the K(n)-localization. For n=1, this is true (Mahowald). For n=2, it is known to be false at p=2 (Mahowald–Ravenel–Shick) but open at odd primes. The connection to cobordism is through the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence: the E2-term for the sphere is ExtBP*BP(BP*, BP*), and the chromatic filtration corresponds to the filtration by the ideals In = (p, v1, …, vn-1). The chromatic splitting conjecture implies that the K(n)-local sphere is built from the Lubin–Tate tower, whose layers are the Morava K-theories K(n). The cobordism spectrum MU maps to each En via the Landweber exact functor theorem, and the chromatic convergence theorem says that the sphere is the homotopy limit of the K(n)-localizations. The failure of the telescope conjecture at n=2, p=2 means that there are v2-periodic phenomena in the stable homotopy groups of spheres that are not detected by K(2)-localization; these are related to the exotic torsion in π*MU and the beta-family. The Stolz–Teichner program for supersymmetric TQFTs predicts that the chromatic splitting conjecture is related to the decomposition of the space of 2-dimensional supersymmetric field theories, which are classified by tmf (height 2) and its generalizations.

  • Confusing the sigma-orientation MSpin → tmf with the string orientation MString → tmf; the former exists integrally, the latter requires inverting 6 to match the Witten genus.
  • Assuming the Kervaire invariant one problem is solved for all dimensions; the HHR theorem only covers dimension 126, leaving the possibility of exotic smooth structures in higher dimensions unrelated to Kervaire invariant.
  • Treating the surgery exact sequence as an exact sequence of abelian groups; it is an exact sequence of pointed sets, and the maps are not homomorphisms in general.
  • Believing that every spin manifold admits a string structure; the obstruction is ½p1 ∈ H4(M; ℤ), which is non-zero for many spin manifolds (e.g., ℍP2).
  • Mixing up the telescope conjecture (localization with respect to a telescope) with the chromatic splitting conjecture (decomposition of the K(n)-local sphere); they are related but distinct statements.
  • Overlooking that the Ando–Hopkins–Strickland theory requires E to be an E∞-ring spectrum; for E∞-ring spectra that are not complex orientable, the formal group law framework does not apply directly.
  • Assuming the Novikov conjecture follows from the surgery exact sequence; the surgery exact sequence classifies manifolds up to homotopy equivalence, while the Novikov conjecture concerns homotopy invariance of higher signatures, which is implied by the rational injectivity of the assembly map in L-theory.

These advanced topics illustrate how cobordism theory serves as a unifying framework connecting differential topology, stable homotopy theory, algebraic geometry, and mathematical physics through the language of Thom spectra, formal groups, and chromatic homotopy theory.

Cobordism and the Cobordism of Symplectic Manifolds

A symplectic manifold is a pair (M, ω) where M is a smooth manifold of even dimension 2n and ω is a closed, non‑degenerate 2‑form. Two symplectic manifolds (M₀, ω₀) and (M₁, ω₁) are symplectically cobordant if there exists a compact symplectic manifold (W, Ω) with boundary ∂W = M₀ ⊔ M₁ such that Ω restricts to ω₀ on M₀ and to ω₁ on M₁ (with appropriate sign conventions). The set of cobordism classes forms the symplectic cobordism group Ωsymp2n.

Low‑dimensional calculations

In dimension 2 every oriented surface admits a symplectic form (an area form). The genus gives a complete invariant: Ωsymp2 ≅ ℤ, generated by the torus T² with its standard area form. The sphere S² bounds the disc D² with the standard symplectic form, so it represents zero.

Dimension 4 and Seiberg–Witten obstructions

For 4‑manifolds the situation is far richer. Gromov’s h‑principle shows that any almost complex structure on a 4‑manifold can be deformed to a symplectic form on the complement of a finite set of points, but global obstructions appear. The Seiberg–Witten invariant provides a powerful cobordism invariant: if a closed symplectic 4‑manifold has b₂⁺ ≥ 2, its Seiberg–Witten invariant is non‑zero for the canonical spinc structure. Consequently, two symplectic 4‑manifolds with different Seiberg–Witten basic classes cannot be symplectically cobordant. This yields infinitely many distinct cobordism classes in each even dimension ≥ 4.

Relation to contact cobordism

The symplectization of a contact manifold (M, ξ = ker α) is the symplectic manifold (ℝ × M, d(eᵗα)). A symplectic cobordism between contact manifolds induces a contact cobordism after passing to the boundaries. This functorial relationship allows one to transfer computations from contact topology (e.g., Heegaard Floer contact invariants) to symplectic cobordism groups.

Ring structure

The Cartesian product of symplectic manifolds makes the graded group Ωsymp* into a commutative ring. The complex projective spaces ℂPⁿ with their Fubini–Study forms generate a polynomial subring ℤ[ℂP¹, ℂP², …] after inverting 2, but torsion phenomena (e.g., 2‑torsion detected by the Arf invariant of the associated almost complex structure) remain an active area of research.

Cobordism and the Cobordism of Contact Manifolds

A contact manifold of dimension 2n+1 is a pair (M, ξ) where ξ = ker α is a hyperplane field defined by a 1‑form α satisfying α ∧ (dα)ⁿ ≠ 0. A contact cobordism from (M₋, ξ₋) to (M₊, ξ₊) is a compact symplectic manifold (W, ω) with ∂W = M₋ ⊔ M₊ such that ω is exact near the boundary and the Liouville vector field points outward along M₊ and inward along M₋. The induced contact structures on the boundaries agree with ξ₋ and ξ₊ up to isotopy.

Contact cobordism groups

The set of contact cobordism classes forms the contact cobordism group Ωcont2n+1. In dimension 3, Eliashberg’s classification of overtwisted contact structures implies that every closed 3‑manifold admits an overtwisted contact structure, and two overtwisted structures are contactomorphic if they are homotopic as plane fields. The tight contact structures, however, carry subtle invariants. The Heegaard Floer contact invariant c(ξ) ∈ HF⁺(−M) provides a homomorphism Ωcont3 → ℤ/2ℤ (after suitable grading). Manifolds with c(ξ) = 0 bound a contact cobordism to the standard contact S³, while those with c(ξ) = 1 do not.

Higher dimensions and the Bourgeois–Eliashberg construction

For n ≥ 2, the h‑principle for contact structures (Gromov, Eliashberg–Mishachev) shows that the inclusion of contact structures into almost contact structures is a weak homotopy equivalence. Consequently, the contact cobordism groups are isomorphic to the almost contact cobordism groups, which can be computed via the Thom–Pontryagin construction for the stable normal bundle with a reduction to U(n) × 1. This yields Ωcont2n+1 ⊗ ℚ ≅ 0 for n ≥ 2, while torsion subgroups are related to the image of the J‑homomorphism in stable homotopy.

Symplectic fillings and the contact category

A strong symplectic filling of (M, ξ) is a contact cobordism from the empty set to (M, ξ). The existence of a filling imposes constraints on the contact homology of (M, ξ). The contact category, whose objects are contact manifolds and morphisms are contact cobordisms up to equivalence, carries a monoidal structure given by disjoint union. Its Grothendieck ring is closely related to the symplectic cobordism ring via the symplectization functor.

Worked Example: Symplectic Cobordism in Dimension 2
Show that the symplectic cobordism group Ωsymp2 is isomorphic to ℤ and identify a generator.

Every closed oriented surface Σg of genus g admits a symplectic form (an area form). The Euler characteristic χ(Σg) = 2 − 2g is a cobordism invariant for oriented surfaces, and it is additive under disjoint union. For a symplectic cobordism (W, Ω) with ∂W = Σg₀ ⊔ Σg₁, the symplectic form Ω gives an orientation on W, so the usual oriented cobordism argument applies: χ(Σg₀) = χ(Σg₁). Hence genus is a complete invariant for symplectic cobordism in dimension 2. The map g ↦ [Σg] defines an isomorphism ℤ → Ωsymp2. The torus T² = Σ₁ represents 1 ∈ ℤ, and the sphere S² = Σ₀ represents 0 because it bounds the disc D² with the standard symplectic form. Thus Ωsymp2 ≅ ℤ generated by [T²].

Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a cobordism group computation in low dimensions, first recall the classical oriented/unoriented results, then check whether the extra geometric structure (symplectic, contact, foliation, CR, Spinh) imposes additional constraints or relaxes them. In dimension 2, symplectic and oriented cobordism coincide; in dimension 3, contact cobordism is detected by Heegaard Floer invariants; in higher dimensions, h‑principles often reduce the problem to algebraic topology of the structure group. Write down the relevant exact sequence or spectral sequence (e.g., the Adams spectral sequence for the Thom spectrum of the structure group) to organise the answer.

Cobordism and the Cobordism of Foliated Manifolds

A codimension‑q foliation on a manifold M is a decomposition of M into immersed submanifolds (leaves) of dimension dim M − q, locally modelled on the fibres of a submersion to ℝq. Haefliger showed that foliations are classified up to concordance by homotopy classes of maps M → BΓq, where BΓq is the classifying space of the Haefliger groupoid Γq. Two foliated manifolds (M₀, ℱ₀) and (M₁, ℱ₁) are foliated cobordant if there exists a foliated manifold (W, ℱ) with ∂W = M₀ ⊔ M₁ and ℱ transverse to the boundary, inducing ℱ₀ and ℱ₁.

The foliated cobordism groups

The set of foliated cobordism classes forms the group Ωfoln,q (dimension n, codimension q). By the Pontryagin–Thom construction for Haefliger structures, Ωfoln,q ≅ πn(MΓq), where MΓq is the Thom spectrum of the universal Haefliger bundle. Thurston’s work on the homology of BΓq (especially for q = 1, 2) gives concrete calculations: Ωfol3,1 ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2ℤ, generated by the Reeb foliation on S³ and a certain foliation on the mapping torus of a Dehn twist. The Godbillon–Vey invariant GV(ℱ) ∈ H³(M; ℝ) is a cobordism invariant for codimension‑1 foliations; it vanishes on boundaries of foliated 4‑manifolds, providing a homomorphism Ωfol3,1 → ℝ whose image is ℤ (after normalisation).

Relation to the Thurston norm and taut foliations

For a 3‑manifold M, a taut foliation (one with a closed transversal intersecting every leaf) gives a class in Ωfol3,1 that is non‑trivial. The Thurston norm on H₂(M; ℤ) measures the minimal complexity of a surface representing a homology class; the existence of a taut foliation implies that the unit ball of the Thurston norm is a polygon with vertices corresponding to the Euler classes of the foliation. This connects foliated cobordism to 3‑manifold topology and the geometry of the Thurston norm.

Higher codimensions and the Haefliger cohomology

For q ≥ 2, the rational cohomology of BΓq is a polynomial algebra on the universal Godbillon–Vey classes GV2i+1 of degree 2i+1 (i ≥ 1). The corresponding characteristic numbers give homomorphisms Ωfoln,q → ℝ. The kernel of these maps is torsion, and the torsion subgroups are related to the stable homotopy groups of spheres via the map MΓq → S⁰ induced by forgetting the foliation.

Cobordism and the Cobordism of CR Manifolds

A CR manifold of hypersurface type is a real (2n+1)‑manifold M equipped with a rank‑n complex subbundle H ⊂ TM and an integrable almost complex structure J on H. The Levi form Lp(X, Y) = −i dα(X, JY) (where α is a local contact form with ker α = H) measures the failure of H to be a complex tangent space. A CR cobordism is a complex manifold (W, J) with boundary ∂W = M such that the induced CR structure on M coincides with the given one. The CR cobordism group ΩCR2n+1 classifies CR manifolds up to this relation.

The Tanaka–Webster connection and pseudo‑hermitian invariants

On a strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold (Levi form positive definite), the Tanaka–Webster connection provides a canonical linear connection on H compatible with J and the Levi metric. Its curvature yields the Webster scalar curvature R and the torsion tensor A. The CR Yamabe problem asks for a contact form with constant Webster scalar curvature; its solution gives a conformal invariant, the CR Yamabe constant, which is a CR cobordism invariant.

Kohn–Rossi cohomology and the ∂b complex

The tangential Cauchy–Riemann operator ∂b defines a complex of differential forms on M. Its cohomology Hqb(M) (Kohn–Rossi cohomology) is a CR invariant. For a CR manifold bounding a complex manifold, the ∂b complex is the boundary analogue of the Dolbeault complex, and the Hodge theory of ∂b gives obstructions to fillability. In particular, if M = ∂W with W a Stein manifold, then Hqb(M) = 0 for q ≥ 1. This yields a homomorphism ΩCR2n+1 → ⊕q≥1 Hqb(M) whose kernel consists of fillable CR manifolds.

Low‑dimensional results and the CR signature theorem

In dimension 3 (n = 1), the CR cobordism group ΩCR3 is isomorphic to ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2ℤ. The ℤ factor is detected by the CR index of the ∂b operator (the Kohn–Rossi index), and the ℤ/2ℤ factor is the Arf invariant of the associated Spinc structure. The CR analogue of the Hirzebruch signature theorem, due to Boutet de Monvel and others, expresses the signature of a complex manifold with boundary in terms of the η‑invariant of the ∂b operator on the boundary, giving a powerful tool for computing ΩCR2n+1 in higher dimensions.

Cobordism and the Cobordism of Spinh Manifolds

A Spinh structure on an oriented manifold M is a lift of the structure group of the tangent bundle from SO(n) to Spinh(n) = (Spin(n) × Sp(1))/ℤ₂, where the central ℤ₂ identifies the non‑trivial elements of the centres of Spin(n) and Sp(1). This generalises both Spin structures (when the Sp(1) factor is trivial) and Spinc structures (when the Sp(1) factor is replaced by U(1)). The Spinh cobordism group ΩSpinhn classifies Spinh manifolds up to cobordism through Spinh manifolds.

Thom spectrum and the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro orientation

The Thom spectrum MSpinh is the Thom spectrum of the universal Spinh bundle. There is a canonical map MSpinh → KO ∧ HP∞ (the smash product of real K‑theory with the suspension spectrum of quaternionic projective space) which is an equivalence after inverting 2. This is the Spinh analogue of the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro orientation MSpin → KO and MSpinc → KU. Consequently, ΩSpinh* ⊗ ℤ[1/2] ≅ KO*(HP∞) ⊗ ℤ[1/2].

Generators and the role of quaternionic projective spaces

The quaternionic projective spaces ℍPk admit canonical Spinh structures (their tangent bundles are associated to the principal Sp(k+1)‑bundle over ℍPk). Their cobordism classes generate the polynomial part of ΩSpinh* ⊗ ℚ. Specifically, ΩSpinh* ⊗ ℚ ≅ ℚ[ℍP¹, ℍP², ℍP³, …] with |ℍPk| = 4k. The torsion subgroups are 2‑primary and have been computed by Anderson, Brown, and Peterson using the Adams spectral sequence for MSpinh. The first few groups are: ΩSpinh0 ≅ ℤ, ΩSpinh1 = ΩSpinh2 = ΩSpinh3 = 0, ΩSpinh4 ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2ℤ (generated by ℍP¹ and a certain 2‑torsion manifold), ΩSpinh5 = ΩSpinh6 = ΩSpinh7 = 0, ΩSpinh8 ≅ ℤ² ⊕ (ℤ/2ℤ)², etc.

Relation to positive scalar curvature and the Stolz exact sequence

Stolz proved that a simply connected Spinh manifold of dimension ≥ 5 admits a metric of positive scalar curvature if and only if its α‑invariant in KO*(HP∞) vanishes. This gives a homomorphism ΩSpinhn → KOn(HP∞) whose kernel is precisely the subgroup of manifolds bounding a Spinh manifold with positive scalar curvature. The resulting exact sequence (the Stolz exact sequence) connects Spinh cobordism, the KO‑theory of HP∞, and the classification of positive scalar curvature metrics.

  • Confusing symplectic cobordism with contact cobordism: a symplectic cobordism has a symplectic form on the interior, while a contact cobordism is a symplectic cobordism with a specific Liouville behaviour at the boundary.
  • Assuming the Godbillon–Vey invariant classifies foliated cobordism in all codimensions; it is only a complete invariant for codimension‑1 foliations of 3‑manifolds up to concordance.
  • Treating the CR cobordism group as a ring under Cartesian product; the product of two CR manifolds is not naturally a CR manifold of hypersurface type (the Levi form becomes degenerate).
  • Overlooking that Spinh structures exist only when the second Stiefel–Whitney class w₂ lifts to an integral class that is the reduction of a quaternionic line bundle; not every Spinc manifold admits a Spinh structure.
  • Believing that the symplectic cobordism ring is polynomial on ℂPⁿ after inverting 2; 2‑torsion generators (e.g., the K3 surface) survive and are detected by the Seiberg–Witten invariant.
  • Mixing up the Haefliger classifying space BΓq with the classifying space for foliations of a fixed manifold; BΓq classifies foliations up to concordance, not up to diffeomorphism.
  • Ignoring the role of the η‑invariant in the CR signature theorem; the boundary correction term is essential for the index formula on complex manifolds with boundary.
The advanced topics above illustrate how cobordism theory extends beyond classical smooth, oriented, and complex structures to encompass symplectic, contact, foliated, CR, and Spinh geometries, each with its own Thom spectrum, characteristic invariants, and computational tools.

Cobordism and the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer Index Theorem

The Atiyah–Patodi–Singer (APS) index theorem, published in a series of papers between 1973 and 1976, provides the definitive index formula for elliptic operators on compact manifolds with boundary. Unlike the classical Atiyah–Singer theorem, which applies to closed manifolds, the APS theorem introduces a boundary correction term expressed through the η‑invariant of a self‑adjoint elliptic operator on the boundary. For a Dirac‑type operator D on a compact spin manifold W with boundary M = ∂W, the theorem states

index(D+) = ∫W Â(TW) − (η(M) + h)/2,

where Â(TW) is the Â‑genus form, η(M) is the η‑invariant of the induced boundary operator, and h is the dimension of its kernel. This formula directly links the cobordism class of M (as a spin manifold) to the index of an operator on any spin filling W. Consequently, the η‑invariant defines a homomorphism η : ΩSpin4k−1 → ℝ/ℤ whose kernel is precisely the subgroup of spin boundaries that admit a positive scalar curvature metric (by the Gromov–Lawson relative index theorem). The APS theorem also underpins the proof of the Novikov conjecture for groups with finite classifying space via the Mishchenko–Fomenko higher index theory, and it provides the analytic foundation for the ρ‑invariant of Atiyah–Patodi–Singer, which detects exotic smooth structures on spheres in dimensions 4k−1. In the context of cobordism, the APS theorem shows that the spin cobordism group ΩSpin4k−1 maps injectively into ℝ/ℤ modulo the image of the index map, a fact used by Stolz to characterise the positive scalar curvature obstruction in spin cobordism.

Cobordism and the Kreck Modified Surgery Theory

Matthias Kreck’s modification of classical surgery theory, developed in the late 1970s and 1980s, replaces the normal map data of Browder–Novikov–Sullivan–Wall with a normal 1‑type — a fibration B → BO (or BSO, BSpin, etc.) whose first non‑trivial homotopy group occurs in degree 2. A B‑manifold is a manifold M equipped with a lift of its stable normal bundle to B. Two B‑manifolds are B‑cobordant if there exists a cobordism W with a B‑structure extending those on the boundary. Kreck proved that for a simply connected B‑manifold M of dimension ≥ 5, the B‑cobordism class is determined by a surgery obstruction in a certain L‑group Ln(ℤ[π1(B)]) together with a normal invariant in [M, G/B], where G/B is the homotopy fibre of B → BO. This framework unifies the classification of manifolds with various tangential structures (orientation, spin, string, etc.) and yields a modified surgery exact sequence

⋯ → Ln+1(ℤ[π]) → SB(X) → [X, G/B] → Ln(ℤ[π]) → ⋯

where SB(X) is the structure set of B‑manifolds homotopy equivalent to a given B‑manifold X. The power of Kreck’s approach lies in its flexibility: by choosing B appropriately, one can classify manifolds up to diffeomorphism, homeomorphism, or even block diffeomorphism. For example, taking B = BSpin × K(ℤ, 4) recovers the classification of string manifolds, while B = BTop yields the topological surgery exact sequence of Kirby–Siebenmann. Kreck’s theory also provides a systematic method for computing the inertia group of a manifold — the group of exotic smooth structures that become standard after crossing with ℝ2 — by analysing the map from the structure set to the cobordism group of B‑manifolds.

Worked Example: Computing the APS η‑Invariant for a Lens Space
Let L(p, q) be the 3‑dimensional lens space obtained by p/q surgery on the unknot, equipped with its unique spin structure (which exists because p is odd). Compute the reduced η‑invariant η(L(p, q)) of the signature operator and deduce its image in ΩSpin3 ≅ 0.
The signature operator on a 3‑manifold is the odd signature operator B = *d + d*. Its η‑invariant for L(p, q) is given by the Dedekind sum formula η(L(p, q)) = 4 s(q, p) where s(q, p) = ∑k=1p−1 ((k/p)) ((qk/p)) and ((x)) = x − ⌊x⌋ − 1/2 for x ∉ ℤ, 0 otherwise. For p = 3, q = 1, s(1, 3) = ((1/3))((1/3)) + ((2/3))((2/3)) = (−1/6)(−1/6) + (1/6)(1/6) = 1/18. Thus η = 4/18 = 2/9. The APS theorem for a spin 4‑manifold W bounding L(3,1) gives index(D+) = ∫W Â − η/2. Since ΩSpin3 = 0, L(3,1) bounds a spin 4‑manifold (e.g., the disc bundle of the tautological line bundle over ℂP1 with appropriate framing). The integrality of the index forces η/2 to be congruent to the Â‑genus modulo ℤ, which holds because 2/9 is the correct fractional part. The vanishing of ΩSpin3 implies every 3‑dimensional spin manifold bounds a spin 4‑manifold, so the η‑invariant modulo ℤ is a cobordism invariant that detects the trivial group.
Exam Strategy: APS and Kreck in Questions
When a question asks for the boundary correction term in an index theorem on a manifold with boundary, the answer is always the η‑invariant (or ρ‑invariant for the relative case). For Kreck modified surgery, remember that the normal 1‑type B encodes the tangential structure: B = BSO for oriented, B = BSpin for spin, B = BString for string. The surgery obstruction lives in the L‑group of the fundamental group of B, not of the manifold itself. A typical trap is to confuse the classical surgery exact sequence (which uses the manifold's fundamental group) with Kreck's version (which uses π1(B)). Always identify B first.

Cobordism and the Classification of Simply Connected Topological 4‑Manifolds

Michael Freedman’s 1982 proof of the topological Poincaré conjecture in dimension 4 and his classification of simply connected topological 4‑manifolds rests on a deep interplay between cobordism, surgery, and the intersection form. Freedman showed that for any unimodular symmetric bilinear form Q over ℤ, there exists a closed, simply connected topological 4‑manifold M with intersection form Q. Moreover, two such manifolds are homeomorphic if and only if their intersection forms are isometric, except for the exotic E8 manifold (the unique simply connected topological 4‑manifold with intersection form E8 and Kirby–Siebenmann invariant ks = 1). The proof uses the topological surgery exact sequence for the normal 1‑type BTop, where the surgery obstruction groups are the simply connected L‑groups L4(ℤ) ≅ ℤ (signature) and L5(ℤ) = 0. The key innovation is the construction of a Casson handle — a topological 2‑handle built from an infinite tower of immersed discs — which replaces the smooth 2‑handle in the surgery process. Casson handles are classified by their intersection form and are all homeomorphic to the standard 2‑handle × ℝ2 (Freedman’s disc embedding theorem). The Kirby–Siebenmann invariant ks ∈ ℤ/2ℤ, which obstructs the existence of a PL structure, appears as the obstruction to extending a topological normal map to a homeomorphism. In cobordism terms, the topological cobordism group ΩTop4 ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2ℤ is generated by ℂP2 (signature 1) and the E8 manifold (signature 8, ks = 1). The smooth cobordism group ΩSO4 ≅ ℤ (generated by ℂP2) maps onto the signature summand, while the E8 manifold is not smoothly cobordant to zero (its signature is 8, but it does not bound a smooth spin manifold because the Â‑genus would be 1, contradicting Rokhlin’s theorem). This dichotomy between topological and smooth cobordism in dimension 4 is the source of exotic ℝ4s and the failure of the smooth h‑cobordism theorem.

Cobordism and the Baum–Douglas Geometric Model for K‑Homology

In 1982, Paul Baum and Ronald Douglas introduced a geometric description of the K‑homology groups K*(X) of a compact space X using spinc cobordism. A geometric cycle is a triple (M, E, f) where M is a compact spinc manifold (possibly with boundary), E → M is a complex vector bundle, and f : M → X is a continuous map. Two cycles are equivalent if they are related by direct sum/disjoint union, vector bundle modification (replacing M by the total space of a complex vector bundle with a Thom class), and cobordism (there exists a spinc manifold W with boundary M0 ⊔ M1 and extensions of E and f). The resulting group of equivalence classes is naturally isomorphic to K*(X). The isomorphism sends a cycle to the K‑homology class represented by the Dirac operator on M twisted by E, pushed forward via f. This model makes the analytic index map K*(X) → K*(pt) ≅ ℤ transparent: it is the index of the twisted Dirac operator on a closed cycle. The Baum–Douglas model also provides a geometric interpretation of the K‑theory fundamental class of a spinc manifold M as the cycle (M, ℂ, id), and the Poincaré duality isomorphism K*(M) ≅ K*(M) as cap product with this class. In the context of cobordism, the Baum–Douglas equivalence relation is precisely spinc cobordism of cycles, so the K‑homology of a point is the spinc cobordism ring ΩSpinc* modulo the vector bundle modification relation. This ring is known to be ℤ in even degrees and 0 in odd degrees, with the even generator given by ℂP2k. The model extends to equivariant K‑homology for compact group actions (using G‑spinc manifolds) and to twisted K‑homology (using bundles of projective spaces), making it a versatile tool in index theory and the Baum–Connes conjecture.

Cobordism and the Eells–Kuiper Invariant in Dimension 4k+1

James Eells and Nicolaas Kuiper introduced in 1962 a diffeomorphism invariant μ for closed (4k+1)‑dimensional manifolds M that are k‑connected and have a fixed framing on the stable normal bundle (i.e., a trivialisation of TM ⊕ ℝN). The invariant takes values in ℚ/ℤ and is defined by choosing a parallelisable (4k+2)‑manifold W with ∂W = M and setting μ(M) = (signature(W) − pk(W)[W, M]) / ak mod ℤ, where pk is the k‑th Pontryagin class and ak is a normalising constant (e.g., a1 = 23·32·5·7 = 2520 for k = 1). The Eells–Kuiper invariant classifies such manifolds up to framed cobordism: two framed (4k+1)‑manifolds are framed cobordant if and only if their μ‑invariants agree. For k = 1 (dimension 5), this gives a complete classification of framed 5‑manifolds, which are all framed cobordant to a connected sum of copies of the Wu manifold SU(3)/SO(3) (which has μ = 1/28 mod ℤ). In higher dimensions, the invariant detects the image of J in the stable homotopy groups of spheres: the framed cobordism group Ωfr4k+1 ≅ π4k+1S contains a cyclic summand of order ak generated by the Milnor sphere, and μ maps this summand isomorphically onto (1/ak)ℤ/ℤ. The Eells–Kuiper invariant also appears in the Adams spectral sequence as the value of the d2 differential on certain classes, and it is closely related to the Kervaire invariant in dimension 4k+2 via the formula μ(∂W) ≡ Kervaire(W) mod 2 for a framed (4k+2)‑manifold W. Modern refinements use the Anderson–Brown–Peterson splitting of MSpin to express μ in terms of KO‑theory characteristic numbers, linking it to the α‑invariant of the Dirac operator.

  • Confusing the APS η‑invariant (a real number defined analytically) with the ρ‑invariant (an element of the representation ring of the fundamental group, defined as a difference of η‑invariants).
  • Assuming Kreck’s modified surgery sequence uses the fundamental group of the manifold; it uses π1(B) where B is the normal 1‑type.
  • Believing every simply connected topological 4‑manifold is smoothable; the E8 manifold and exotic ℝ4s are counterexamples.
  • Thinking the Baum–Douglas model uses spin manifolds; it uses spinc manifolds, and the vector bundle modification relation is essential to kill the odd‑degree groups.
  • Treating the Eells–Kuiper invariant as an integer; it takes values in ℚ/ℤ and its denominator is the order of the image of J in that dimension.
  • Overlooking that the Kirby–Siebenmann invariant is a cobordism invariant in ΩTop4 but not in ΩSO4 (the E8 manifold has ks = 1 but signature 8, so it is not smoothly null‑cobordant).
  • Assuming the APS theorem applies to any elliptic boundary condition; it requires the APS boundary condition (projection onto non‑negative eigenspaces of the boundary operator), which is a global pseudo‑differential condition.

Cobordism and the Classification of 't Hooft Anomalies via Cobordism Groups of Manifolds with G‑Structure

The modern classification of 't Hooft anomalies in quantum field theory rests on a precise mathematical theorem: the group of deformation classes of invertible topological quantum field theories in dimension d with symmetry group G is naturally isomorphic to the Anderson dual of the cobordism spectrum MG shifted by d+1. For a finite group G and a d-dimensional QFT with G symmetry, the anomaly is an element of Hom(ΩGd, U(1)) ⊕ Ext(ΩGd−1, U(1)), where ΩG* denotes the cobordism ring of manifolds equipped with a principal G-bundle. When G is a compact Lie group, one replaces principal bundles by G-structures on the stable normal bundle. The Freed–Hopkins theorem identifies the group of reflection-positive invertible TQFTs with the torsion subgroup of ΩGd together with a free part detected by characteristic numbers. In practice, one computes ΩG* using the Adams spectral sequence for the Thom spectrum MG or, for G = Spin, Pin±, Spinc, the Anderson–Brown–Peterson splitting of MSpin into a wedge of Eilenberg–MacLane spectra and connective KO-theory summands. For example, the anomaly of a 4k+2 dimensional chiral fermion coupled to a Spinc structure is the mod 2 index of the Dirac operator, which corresponds to the generator of ΩSpinc4k+2 ≅ ℤ/2. The anomaly of a 4k dimensional Weyl fermion with Spin structure is the ℤ-valued index, detected by the Â-genus in ΩSpin4k. For time-reversal symmetric systems with Pin− structure, the anomaly in dimension 4k+1 is the Arf–Brown–Kervaire invariant, living in ΩPin−4k+1 ≅ ℤ/2. The complete classification for the Standard Model gauge group SU(3)×SU(2)×U(1) with various discrete symmetries was carried out by Wan–Wang and by García-Etxebarria–Montero using the Adams spectral sequence for MSpin × BG, revealing that all perturbative and non-perturbative anomalies cancel in 4 dimensions but non-trivial anomalies appear in 5 and 6 dimensions, constraining possible UV completions. This programme has turned cobordism theory into a computational engine for particle physics, with the cobordism groups ΩG* for G = Spin, Spinc, Pin±, Spin(ℂ), and their products with finite groups now tabulated through dimension 10 and beyond.

Cobordism and the Adams–Novikov Spectral Sequence for Topological Modular Forms: The tmf Resolution

The spectrum of topological modular forms tmf, constructed by Hopkins–Mahowald and Goerss–Hopkins–Miller, admits an Adams–Novikov spectral sequence whose E2-page is the cohomology of the moduli stack MFG of formal groups with coefficients in the sheaf of rings ω⊗* ⊗ I⊗* , where I is the ideal of the cusp. The E2-term can be computed via the chromatic fracture square, which relates tmf to its K(1)-localization (a summand of KO[[q]]) and its K(2)-localization (the Morava E-theory E2 with an action of the extended Morava stabilizer group G2). The homotopy groups π*tmf are known through a range of about 200 stems, largely due to the work of Behrens, Hill, Hopkins, Mahowald, and others using the tmf resolution: a tower of spectra tmf → tmf1(3) → tmf1(5) → … where tmf1(N) is the compactified moduli of elliptic curves with level N structure. Each layer is a Thom spectrum of a virtual bundle over a modular curve, so its homotopy can be attacked by the classical Adams spectral sequence. The connection to cobordism is twofold. First, the string orientation MString → tmf, constructed by Ando–Hopkins–Strickland, induces a map of Adams–Novikov spectral sequences from the one for MString (whose E2-page is ExtBP*BP(BP*, BP*MString)) to the tmf ANSS. This map detects the Witten genus and its refinements, and its kernel and cokernel are controlled by the chromatic filtration. Second, the homotopy groups of tmf themselves are cobordism groups of manifolds with string structure and a map to the moduli stack of elliptic curves, as made precise by the Stolz–Teichner program and the work of Bunke–Naumann on geometric models for tmf. The tmf ANSS differentials are deeply tied to the geometry of the moduli stack: the d3 differential corresponds to the Hasse invariant, the d5 to the modular form Δ, and higher differentials to the geometry of the cusp. The computation of π*tmf has revealed exotic elements such as the β-family elements that survive from the sphere, the κ-family detected by the cusp, and the η, ν, σ families from the image of J. The tmf resolution also provides a geometric interpretation of the chromatic splitting conjecture at height 2: the K(2)-local sphere splits as a wedge of K(2)-localizations of tmf1(N) for varying N, and the cobordism groups of string manifolds mapping to these moduli spaces control the splitting.

Worked computation: anomaly cancellation in 6d (1,0) SCFT
A six-dimensional (1,0) superconformal field theory with gauge group G = SU(2) and matter content consisting of nf hypermultiplets in the fundamental representation and na hypermultiplets in the adjoint representation is placed on a Spin manifold M6. The anomaly polynomial is I8 = (nf + 2na − 8) c2(F)2 + (nf + 2na − 8) p1(TM) c2(F) + … where F is the SU(2) field strength. Determine the condition on nf, na for the theory to be anomaly-free, using the fact that ΩSpin6(BSU(2)) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ generated by (ℂP3, trivial bundle) and (S3 × S3, instanton bundle).
The anomaly is the evaluation of I8 on the fundamental class of a 7-manifold bounding the 6-manifold with SU(2) bundle. By the Pontryagin–Thom construction, the anomaly is a homomorphism ΩSpin6(BSU(2)) → ℝ/ℤ. The two generators evaluate as follows: on ℂP3 with trivial bundle, ∫ c2(F)2 = 0, ∫ p1 c2(F) = 0, so this generator gives no constraint. On S3 × S3 with the instanton bundle (c2 = 1 on one S3 factor), ∫ c2(F)2 = 1, ∫ p1 c2(F) = 0. Thus the anomaly is (nf + 2na − 8) mod ℤ. Anomaly freedom requires nf + 2na = 8. This matches the known classification of 6d (1,0) SCFTs with SU(2) gauge group.
Exam strategy: anomaly–cobordism dictionary
Memorise the low-dimensional cobordism groups ΩGd for G = Spin, Pin±, Spinc up to d = 8. The anomaly in dimension d is a homomorphism ΩGd → U(1). If the group is finite cyclic, the anomaly is a root of unity; if it has a free ℤ summand, the anomaly is an integer-valued index. The Freed–Hopkins theorem says reflection-positive anomalies are exactly the torsion part. For computations, use the Anderson–Brown–Peterson splitting MSpin ≃ ∨ ΣkKO ∨ ∨ ΣkHℤ to reduce Spin cobordism to KO-theory and ordinary homology. For Pin−, use MPin− ≃ MSpin ∧ Σ−1Hℤ/2. Write down the relevant characteristic numbers (Â-genus, Arf invariant, Kervaire invariant, Pontryagin numbers) that detect the generators.

Cobordism and the Theory of Thom Spectra as E∞ Ring Spectra: The Ando–Blumberg–Gepner–Hopkins–Rezk Framework

The classical construction of the Thom spectrum MG for a map f: G → O produces a ring spectrum, but the modern understanding, due to Ando–Blumberg–Gepner–Hopkins–Rezk (ABGHR), places this in the context of E∞ ring spectra and the ∞-category of spaces. Given an E∞ map f: G → GL1(R) from an E∞ group G to the units of an E∞ ring R, the Thom spectrum Mf inherits an E∞ R-algebra structure. When R = 𝕊, the sphere spectrum, and G = BO, BSO, BSpin, BString, the resulting Thom spectra MO, MSO, MSpin, MString are E∞ ring spectra. The ABGHR theorem identifies the ∞-category of E∞ R-algebras under Mf with the ∞-category of E∞ R-algebras equipped with a trivialization of the composite G → GL1(R) → Pic(R). This universal property makes the string orientation MString → tmf an initial object among E∞ maps from MString to an E∞ ring where the string class vanishes. The E∞ structure is essential for power operations: the E∞ Dyer–Lashof operations on H*(MSpin; 𝔽2) and the E∞ power operations on MU*(-) are only defined because the Thom spectra are E∞. The ABGHR framework also explains the multiplicative structure of the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence: the E2-page ExtBP*BP(BP*, BP*Mf) is a module over the Hopf algebroid (BP*, BP*BP), and the E∞ structure on Mf induces a comodule algebra structure. For f = BString → BO, the vanishing of the fractional Pontryagin class p1/2 in H4(BString; ℤ) corresponds to the fact that the E∞ map MString → KO[[q]] factors through tmf. The units of MString, studied by Antieau–Meier and others, are related to the Picard group of the ∞-category of MString-modules, which classifies invertible string bordism theories. The ABGHR perspective also clarifies the relationship between Thom spectra and factorization homology: the factorization homology of a manifold M with coefficients in an En algebra A is the Thom spectrum of the map M → BGL1(A) classifying the tangent bundle, and when A = 𝕊 this recovers the classical Pontryagin–Thom construction.

Cobordism and the Cobordism Category as an (∞,n)-Category: The Cobordism Hypothesis in Full Generality

The cobordism hypothesis, proved by Lurie in the (∞,n)-categorical setting and by Baez–Dolan in the (∞,1) case, states that the symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category BordnG of n-dimensional manifolds with G-structure (where G → O(n) is a map of topological groups) is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category with duals generated by a single fully dualizable object. An object in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category is fully dualizable if it has duals at all levels up to n, meaning it admits evaluation and coevaluation maps satisfying the zig-zag identities up to coherent higher homotopies. The hypothesis implies that an n-dimensional topological quantum field theory with G-structure — a symmetric monoidal functor Z: BordnG → C to a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category C — is uniquely determined by the image of the point, which must be a fully dualizable object of C. For C = nVectℂ (the (∞,n)-category of n-vector spaces), fully dualizable objects are finite semisimple algebras for n=2, fusion categories for n=3, and so on. The connection to classical cobordism groups is recovered by taking the homotopy groups of the core (maximal ∞-groupoid) of BordnG: π0 of the core is the cobordism group ΩnG, π1 is the group of diffeomorphism classes of G-manifolds modulo cobordism, and higher homotopy groups are related to the mapping class groups. The (∞,n)-categorical structure also explains the Baas–Sullivan construction of cobordism of manifolds with singularities: a singularity of codimension k corresponds to a non-invertible k-morphism in the (∞,n)-category.
