The work of Galatius–Madsen–Tillmann–Weiss identifies the homology of the moduli space of Riemann surfaces with the homology of the (∞,1)-category Bord2fr of framed 2-manifolds, and the work of Ebert–Randal-Williams extends this to high-dimensional manifolds via the (∞,1)-category of manifolds with tangential structure. The cobordism hypothesis for invertible TQFTs (where C is a Picard (∞,n)-groupoid) recovers the Freed–Hopkins theorem: the group of invertible TQFTs is the Anderson dual of the cobordism spectrum. The full (∞,n)-categorical structure is also essential for the classification of topological phases of matter with defects: a defect of dimension d is a d-morphism in BordnG, and the fusion of defects is composition of morphisms.

Cobordism and the Classification of High-Dimensional Manifolds up to Diffeomorphism: The Work of Kreck, Galatius–Randal-Williams, and Ebert–Randal-Williams

The classification of smooth manifolds up to diffeomorphism in dimensions ≥ 5 proceeds via the surgery exact sequence, which fits into a long exact sequence of sets … → Ln+1(ℤ[π]) → S(X) → [X, G/O] → Ln(ℤ[π]), where S(X) is the structure set of manifolds homotopy equivalent to X, [X, G/O] is the set of normal invariants, and L*(ℤ[π]) are the Wall surgery obstruction groups. Kreck's modified surgery theory replaces G/O by a normal 1-type B → BO, a fibration with π1(B) = π1(X) and πi(B) = πi(G/O) for i ≥ 2, yielding a bijection between the structure set and a cobordism group of normal B-manifolds. This reduces the classification problem to the computation of cobordism groups Ωn(B) and the action of the surgery obstruction map. The work of Galatius–Randal-Williams on the homology of the moduli space of high-dimensional manifolds uses the cobordism category Bordnθ for a tangential structure θ: B → BO. They prove that the homology of the classifying space BDiff∂(W) of diffeomorphisms of a manifold W relative to its boundary stabilizes as the genus increases, and the stable homology is the homology of the infinite loop space Ω∞-1Mθ, where Mθ is the Thom spectrum of θ. This identifies the stable homology of mapping class groups with the homology of a Thom spectrum, a cobordism-theoretic object. Ebert–Randal-Williams extend this to the classification of manifolds up to diffeomorphism by analyzing the homotopy type of the moduli space Mnθ of n-manifolds with θ-structure. They show that the components of Mnθ are classified by the cobordism group Ωnθ, and the homotopy groups of each component are related to the homotopy groups of the Thom spectrum Mθ and the surgery obstruction groups. In particular, for simply connected manifolds of dimension 2n ≥ 6, the diffeomorphism classification is governed by the intersection form (a cobordism invariant in Ω2nSO) and the surgery obstruction in L2n(ℤ) ≅ ℤ (the signature). For non-simply connected manifolds, the Farrell–Jones conjecture in L-theory predicts that the assembly map H*(Bπ; Lℤ) → L*(ℤ[π]) is an isomorphism, which would reduce the computation of surgery obstructions to the homology of the group π with coefficients in the L-theory spectrum. The interplay between cobordism groups, surgery obstruction groups, and the homology of mapping class groups is the central theme of modern high-dimensional manifold topology.

  • Confusing the Anderson dual of the sphere spectrum with the sphere spectrum itself; the anomaly group is the Anderson dual, not π*𝕊.
  • Assuming the tmf ANSS E2-page is simply ExtBP*BP(BP*, BP*); it is the cohomology of the moduli stack MFG with coefficients in the sheaf ω⊗* ⊗ I⊗* .
  • Treating the ABGHR theorem as only about ring spectra; it is a universal property in the ∞-category of E∞ algebras, essential for power operations.
  • Believing the cobordism hypothesis only applies to topological field theories; it applies to any symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category with duals, including n-vector spaces and fusion categories.
  • Overlooking that Kreck's modified surgery uses the normal 1-type B, not the fundamental group of the manifold alone; π1(B) = π1(X) but higher homotopy groups differ from G/O.
  • Thinking the Galatius–Randal-Williams theorem computes the full homology of BDiff(W); it computes the stable homology as genus → ∞, which is the homology of Ω∞-1Mθ.
  • Confusing the cobordism group Ωnθ with the structure set S(X); the structure set maps to the normal invariants [X, G/O], which is a subset of Ωn(B) for the normal 1-type B.

Cobordism and the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer Index Theorem for Manifolds with Boundary

The Atiyah–Patodi–Singer (APS) index theorem extends the Atiyah–Singer index theorem to compact manifolds with boundary by introducing a boundary correction term expressed through the eta invariant of a self-adjoint elliptic operator on the boundary. For a Dirac operator D on a spin manifold M with boundary ∂M = N, the index is given by ind(D) = ∫_M Â(TM) − (η(D_N) + h)/2, where Â is the Â-genus form, D_N is the induced Dirac operator on N, η is the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer eta invariant, and h = dim ker D_N. The eta invariant η(D_N) = ∑λ≠0 sign(λ) |λ|-s|s=0 (analytic continuation) measures the spectral asymmetry of D_N. This formula reveals a deep connection between the index of a manifold with boundary and the cobordism class of its boundary: if N bounds a spin manifold M, then the index of D on M is an integer, forcing η(D_N) to be congruent modulo 2ℤ to the Â-genus of M. Consequently, the reduced eta invariant ξ(N) = (η(D_N) + h)/2 mod ℤ defines a homomorphism ξ: ΩSpin4k-1 → ℝ/ℤ from the spin cobordism group to the circle group. This homomorphism is injective on the torsion subgroup of ΩSpin4k-1 and its image is precisely the subgroup of ℝ/ℤ detected by the KO-theoretic Adams e-invariant. The APS theorem thus identifies the eta invariant as a secondary invariant refining the Â-genus, and it provides the analytical foundation for the Stolz exact sequence relating spin cobordism, KO-theory, and the group of manifolds with positive scalar curvature. In the context of the cobordism hypothesis, the APS index theorem corresponds to the fact that the spin bordism spectrum MSpin is an E_∞ ring spectrum and the index map MSpin → KO is a map of E_∞ ring spectra whose homotopy fiber encodes the eta invariant.

Cobordism and the Gromov–Lawson–Rosenberg Conjecture on Positive Scalar Curvature

The Gromov–Lawson–Rosenberg (GLR) conjecture asserts that for a closed spin manifold M with fundamental group π, the existence of a metric of positive scalar curvature is equivalent to the vanishing of the Rosenberg index α(M) ∈ KO_n(C*_r π), where C*_r π is the reduced group C*-algebra and the index takes values in the KO-theory of this algebra. The conjecture is known to hold for many classes of groups (e.g., groups satisfying the Baum–Connes conjecture with coefficients) but fails in general due to counterexamples by Schick and others involving groups with torsion. The connection to cobordism is mediated by the Stolz exact sequence: … → ΩSpin_n(Bπ) → KO_n(C*_r π) → S_n(Bπ) → ΩSpinn-1(Bπ) → …, where S_n(Bπ) is the group of spin manifolds with positive scalar curvature up to spin cobordism over Bπ. The map ΩSpin_n(Bπ) → KO_n(C*_r π) is the assembly map in KO-theory twisted by the spin orientation, and its kernel consists precisely of those spin cobordism classes that contain a metric of positive scalar curvature. The surgery exact sequence of Gromov–Lawson and Schoen–Yau shows that if two spin manifolds are spin cobordant and one admits positive scalar curvature, then so does the other, provided the cobordism is a spin cobordism. This makes the set of positive scalar curvature cobordism classes a subgroup of ΩSpin_n(Bπ). The Stolz sequence identifies the obstruction to positive scalar curvature as the image of the fundamental class [M] under the assembly map. For simply connected manifolds of dimension ≥ 5, the GLR conjecture reduces to the statement that a simply connected spin manifold admits positive scalar curvature iff its Â-genus vanishes, which is a theorem of Gromov–Lawson and Stolz. In higher dimensions, the conjecture is closely tied to the Baum–Connes conjecture: if π satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture with coefficients, then the assembly map KO_*(Bπ) → KO_*(C*_r π) is an isomorphism, and the GLR conjecture holds. The counterexamples arise from groups where the assembly map fails to be injective, allowing non-zero elements in the kernel that do not correspond to positive scalar curvature cobordism classes. The modern perspective, due to Botvinnik–Gilkey–Stolz and others, uses the Adams spectral sequence for KO-theory and the chromatic filtration to analyze the torsion in ΩSpin_* and its image under the index map.

Worked Problem: Eta Invariant and Spin Cobordism
Let N be a closed spin 3-manifold. Show that the reduced eta invariant ξ(N) ∈ ℝ/ℤ is a spin cobordism invariant, and compute ξ(S3) and ξ(L(3,1)) where L(3,1) is the lens space with its unique spin structure.
The APS index theorem for a spin 4-manifold M with boundary ∂M = N states ind(D_M) = ∫_M Â(TM) − ξ(N). Since ind(D_M) ∈ ℤ and ∫_M Â(TM) ∈ ℤ (the Â-genus of a closed spin 4-manifold is an integer by the integrality theorem), ξ(N) mod ℤ is independent of the choice of bounding M. If N = ∂M1 = ∂M2, then M1 ∪_N (-M2) is a closed spin 4-manifold, and the difference ξM1(N) − ξM2(N) = Â(M1 ∪_N (-M2)) ∈ ℤ. Hence ξ(N) mod ℤ is a well-defined homomorphism ξ: ΩSpin3 → ℝ/ℤ. For S3, the Dirac operator has spectrum symmetric about 0, so η = 0, h = 0, and ξ(S3) = 0. For L(3,1) with its unique spin structure, the Dirac spectrum can be computed via representation theory of SU(2). The eta invariant is η = 1/3 (or 2/3 depending on orientation), h = 0, so ξ(L(3,1)) = 1/6 mod ℤ. This generates the group ΩSpin3 ≅ ℤ/24, where the generator has ξ = 1/24. The lens space L(3,1) represents 4 times the generator, giving ξ = 4/24 = 1/6.
Exam Strategy: Eta Invariant Computations
When asked to compute eta invariants for lens spaces L(p,q), use the formula η = (1/p) ∑k=1p-1 cot(πk/p) cot(πqk/p) for the signature operator, or the Atiyah–Donnelly–Singer formula for the Dirac operator. For spin structures on L(p,1) with p odd, there is a unique spin structure and ξ = (p2−1)/24p mod ℤ. Memorize that ΩSpin3 ≅ ℤ/24 detected by ξ, ΩSpin4 ≅ ℤ generated by the K3 surface with Â = 2, and ΩSpin5 = ΩSpin6 = ΩSpin7 = 0. The APS theorem is the bridge between analytical index theory and cobordism groups — always identify which operator (Dirac, signature, Dolbeault) and which cobordism group (Spin, SO, U, SU) is involved.

Cobordism and the Classification of Exotic Spheres via the Kervaire–Milnor Exact Sequence

The classification of exotic spheres — smooth manifolds homeomorphic but not diffeomorphic to the standard sphere S^n — is one of the crowning achievements of cobordism theory. Kervaire and Milnor (1963) showed that the group Θ_n of h-cobordism classes of oriented homotopy n-spheres (n ≥ 5) fits into an exact sequence 0 → bPn+1 → Θ_n → Coker J_n → 0, where bPn+1 is the subgroup of homotopy spheres that bound parallelizable manifolds, and Coker J_n is the cokernel of the stable J-homomorphism J: π_n(SO) → π_n^S. The group bPn+1 is cyclic of order 22k-2(22k-1−1) numerator of B2k/4k for n = 4k−1, and is trivial or ℤ/2 for other dimensions. The Kervaire invariant one problem asks whether there exists a framed manifold of dimension 2^k−2 with Kervaire invariant 1; this is equivalent to asking whether the element θ_k ∈ π2^k−2^S has order 2. The Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel theorem (2016) resolved this by showing θ_k exists only for k ≤ 6 (dimensions 2, 6, 14, 30, 62, 126). The connection to cobordism is through the framed cobordism group Ω_nfr ≅ π_n^S. The J-homomorphism maps π_n(SO) → π_n^S, and its image consists of framed manifolds that bound spin manifolds. The cokernel Coker J_n classifies exotic spheres that do not bound parallelizable manifolds. The exact sequence is derived from the surgery exact sequence for S^n and the identification of the structure set S(S^n) with Θ_n. The group bPn+1 is identified with the kernel of the map Θ_n → π_n^S/Im J given by the Pontryagin–Thom construction. In modern language, the Kervaire–Milnor exact sequence is the long exact sequence in homotopy groups associated to the fiber sequence MSpin → MFr → KO⟨8⟩, where KO⟨8⟩ is the 8-connected cover of KO. The Adams spectral sequence for the sphere spectrum computes π_n^S, and the image of J is detected by the α-family in the Adams E2-page. The beta family and higher Greek letter elements correspond to exotic spheres not in bPn+1. The order of Θ_n is known for n ≤ 64 and for many higher dimensions through the work of Wang–Xu, Isaksen–Wang–Xu, and others using the Adams spectral sequence and motivic homotopy theory.

Cobordism and the String Orientation of Topological Modular Forms

The string orientation of topological modular forms (TMF) is a map of E_∞ ring spectra MString → TMF that refines the Witten genus. A string structure on a manifold M is a lift of the classifying map of the stable tangent bundle M → BSO to BString, the 3-connected cover of BSO (π1 = π2 = π3 = 0). The string cobordism ring Ω_*String is the homotopy ring of the Thom spectrum MString. The Witten genus is a ring homomorphism Ω_*String → MF^*(SL2(ℤ)) to the ring of integral modular forms, defined by the index of the Dirac operator on the free loop space LM. The sigma-orientation of Ando–Hopkins–Strickland constructs a map MString → tmf (the connective cover of TMF) that lifts the Witten genus to a map of E_∞ ring spectra. This orientation is built using the theory of elliptic curves and power operations: for an elliptic curve E over a ring R, the formal group law of E gives a map MU → E, and the string condition ensures this map factors through MString. The homotopy groups of TMF are computed via the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence with E2-page ExtMU_*MUs,t(MU_*, MU_*MString) which is isomorphic to the cohomology of the moduli stack Mell of elliptic curves with coefficients in the sheaf ω⊗t/2 ⊗ I⊗s, where I is the ideal of the cusp. The string orientation induces a map on homotopy groups π_*MString → π_*TMF whose kernel consists of string manifolds with vanishing Witten genus. The image is the subring of TMF generated by modular forms of level 1. The Stolz–Teichner program conjectures that the string orientation is related to 2-dimensional supersymmetric quantum field theories: a string manifold M should correspond to a 2D supersymmetric sigma model with target M, and the Witten genus is the partition function of this theory. The sigma-orientation is the first step in constructing the conjectured map from the 2D bordism category of string manifolds to the category of topological modular forms. The homotopy groups π_n TMF for n < 0 vanish, and for n ≥ 0 they are built from modular forms and torsion detected by the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence. The string cobordism groups Ω_nString are known for n ≤ 20: they are ℤ in dimensions 0, 8, 16 (generated by products of the K3 surface), and finite torsion in other dimensions, with the first non-trivial torsion in dimension 15 (ℤ/2) and dimension 19 (ℤ/2 ⊕ ℤ/2).

Cobordism and the Comparison Between Algebraic and Topological Cobordism

Algebraic cobordism Ω_*alg, introduced by Levine and Morel, is the universal oriented cohomology theory on smooth quasi-projective varieties over a field k of characteristic 0. It is defined as the cobordism group of cycles with a formal group law given by the universal formal group law over the Lazard ring ℒ. The topological cobordism ring MU_* is also the Lazard ring by Quillen's theorem. There is a natural comparison map Ω_*alg(k) → MU_* induced by sending a smooth projective variety X to its underlying complex manifold X(ℂ) with its canonical almost complex structure. This map is a ring homomorphism respecting the formal group laws. The main conjecture, proved by Levine–Morel and later refined by Hoyois, is that for k = ℂ, the map Ω_*alg(ℂ) → MU_* is an isomorphism after inverting the exponential characteristic (which is 1 for ℂ). In positive characteristic, the map is more subtle: the algebraic cobordism ring Ω_*alg(k) maps to the topological cobordism ring of the associated topological space via the étale realization functor. The work of Marc Hoyois and others shows that the algebraic cobordism spectrum MGL in the motivic stable homotopy category SH(k) has a realization to the topological MU spectrum. The motivic Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for MGL converges to the motivic stable homotopy groups, and its E2-page involves the motivic Hopf algebroid (MGL*,*, MGL*,*MGL). The comparison with topological cobordism is mediated by the Betti realization functor Re_B: SH(ℂ) → SH, which sends MGL to MU. The fiber of this map is related to the motivic cohomology of the base field. The algebraic cobordism of a point Ω_*alg(k) is the Lazard ring ℒ for any field of characteristic 0, but for fields of positive characteristic, there are additional relations coming from the failure of resolution of singularities. The work of Annala and others computes the algebraic cobordism of finite fields and relates it to the topological cobordism of the associated classifying spaces. The comparison map is also central to the program of Voevodsky and Morel to relate algebraic K-theory to topological K-theory via the motivic spectral sequence. In the context of the cobordism hypothesis, algebraic cobordism corresponds to the (∞,n)-category of algebraic varieties with a suitable notion of duality, and the comparison map is induced by the analytification functor.

  • Confusing the APS eta invariant with the Atiyah–Singer index; the eta invariant is a boundary correction term, not an index itself, and takes values in ℝ/ℤ, not ℤ.
  • Assuming the Gromov–Lawson–Rosenberg conjecture holds for all groups; it fails for groups where the Baum–Connes assembly map is not injective, and the counterexamples involve torsion in KO-theory of C*-algebras.
  • Thinking bPn+1 is the entire group of exotic spheres Θ_n; the cokernel of J can be non-trivial, and the Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel theorem shows θ_k exists only for k ≤ 6.
  • Believing the string orientation MString → TMF is an isomorphism on homotopy groups; it is not — the kernel contains string manifolds with vanishing Witten genus, and the image is a proper subring of π_*TMF.
  • Treating algebraic cobordism Ω_*alg as identical to topological MU_* for all fields; they agree over ℂ after inverting the exponential characteristic, but differ in positive characteristic due to resolution of singularities issues.
  • Overlooking that the Kervaire–Milnor exact sequence uses the stable J-homomorphism π_n(SO) → π_n^S, not the unstable J-homomorphism; the stable stems π_n^S are the framed cobordism groups Ω_nfr.
  • Confusing the Witten genus (a modular form) with the Â-genus (an integer); the Witten genus is the index of the Dirac operator on the loop space, and it vanishes for string manifolds that bound string manifolds, but the Â-genus is the index of the Dirac operator on the manifold itself.

Cobordism and the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer Index Theorem: Eta Invariants as Cobordism Invariants

The Atiyah–Patodi–Singer (APS) index theorem extends the Atiyah–Singer index theorem to manifolds with boundary by introducing a boundary correction term, the eta invariant η(D) of a self-adjoint elliptic operator D on the boundary. For a compact spin manifold W with boundary ∂W = M, the index of the Dirac operator D_W with APS boundary conditions is

ind(D_W) = ∫_W Â(TW) − η(D_M)/2,

where Â(TW) is the Â-genus form and η(D_M) = ∑λ≠0 sign(λ) |λ|-s|s=0 is the analytic continuation of the eta function. The eta invariant is not a homotopy invariant but a cobordism invariant: if M bounds a spin manifold W, then η(D_M) ≡ 2∫_W Â(TW) (mod ℤ). This defines a homomorphism η: Ω4k-1Spin → ℝ/ℤ whose image is the rho invariant ρ(M) = η(D_M) mod ℤ. The APS theorem implies that the kernel of η consists precisely of spin manifolds that bound spin manifolds with vanishing Â-genus, i.e., the image of the map Ω4kSpin → Ω4k-1Spin induced by taking boundaries. The rho invariant takes values in ℚ/ℤ for rational homology spheres and detects subtle torsion in spin cobordism groups. For example, the Brieskorn sphere Σ(2,3,7) has ρ = 1/28 mod ℤ and generates a ℤ/28 summand in Ω7Spin. The APS index theorem also underlies the relative index theorem for families, where the eta form η̃ ∈ Ωodd(B)/im(d) on the parameter space B captures the variation of η along the family, and its cohomology class is the Chern–Simons form of the index bundle.

Cobordism and the Segal–Tom Dieck Splitting: Equivariant Cobordism and Fixed Point Data

The Segal–Tom Dieck splitting decomposes the equivariant stable homotopy groups of a G-space X into a sum over conjugacy classes of subgroups H ≤ G of the non-equivariant stable homotopy groups of the fixed point spaces X^H modulo the action of the Weyl group W_G H = N_G H / H. For equivariant cobordism, this yields an isomorphism

Ω_*^G(X) ≅ ⊕(H) Ω_*W_G H(E W_G H ×W_G H X^H),

where the sum runs over conjugacy classes of subgroups H of G, and Ω_*^W denotes the W-equivariant cobordism groups. When X = pt, this reduces to Ω_*^G ≅ ⊕(H) Ω_*W_G H(B W_G H). The splitting is induced by the geometric fixed point functor Φ^H: G-Spectra → W_G H-Spectra, which sends a G-spectrum E to the W_G H-spectrum (E ∧ tilde{E}H)^H, where tilde{E}H is the cofiber of EH_+ → S0. For the equivariant Thom spectrum MG = Thom(ξ_G) of a virtual G-representation ξ_G, the fixed point spectrum (MG)^H is the Thom spectrum of the fixed point representation ξ_G^H over the classifying space B W_G H. This allows computation of Ω_*^G via the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence for each summand. For G = ℤ/2, the splitting gives Ω_*ℤ/2 ≅ Ω_* ⊕ Ω_*ℤ/2(Bℤ/2), where the second summand captures the Smith invariants of ℤ/2-actions. The splitting fails for non-compact Lie groups but holds for finite groups and compact Lie groups with finite Weyl groups. A key application is the computation of the equivariant cobordism ring of a point for G = U(1), where Ω_*U(1) ≅ Ω_*[[x]] with x the Euler class of the standard representation, and the fixed point data recovers the formal group law of complex cobordism.

Worked Problem: Eta Invariant of the Lens Space L(3,1)
Compute the rho invariant ρ(L(3,1)) for the lens space L(3,1) = S3/(ℤ/3) with its canonical spin structure, using the APS index theorem and the fact that L(3,1) bounds the disk bundle of the line bundle O(−3) over ℂP1.
The lens space L(3,1) is the boundary of the 4-manifold W = D(O(−3) → ℂP1), the disk bundle of the line bundle O(−3) over ℂP1. The spin structure on L(3,1) extends to W because c1(O(−3)) = −3 is odd, so w2(W) = c1 mod 2 = 1 ≠ 0, but the restriction to the boundary is trivial. The Â-genus of W is ∫_W Â(TW) = ∫_W (1 − p1/24 + …). For a 4-manifold, Â = 1 − p1/24. The Pontryagin class p1(W) = c12 − 2c2 = 9 − 0 = 9 (since c2 = 0 for a line bundle). The fundamental class [W] pairs with p1 to give ∫_W p1 = 9 ∫ℂP1 c1(O(1))2 = 9. Thus ∫_W Â = −9/24 = −3/8. By APS, η(DL(3,1)) = 2∫_W Â = −3/4 mod ℤ. The rho invariant is ρ = η mod ℤ = 1/4 mod ℤ. However, the Dirac operator on L(3,1) has spectrum symmetric about 0 except for a single zero mode, so the reduced eta invariant is η = 1/4. The rho invariant ρ(L(3,1)) = 1/4 ∈ ℚ/ℤ generates the ℤ/4 summand in Ω3Spin ≅ ℤ/4 detected by the APS invariant.
Exam Strategy: Eta Invariants and Rho Invariants
When asked to compute rho invariants, always identify a bounding spin manifold W and compute ∫_W Â(TW). The rho invariant is 2∫_W Â mod ℤ. For lens spaces L(p,q), use the disk bundle of O(−p) over ℂP1; the integral is −p/8 mod ℤ, giving ρ = p/4 mod ℤ for p odd. Remember that η takes values in ℝ/ℤ, not ℤ, and the APS boundary condition projects onto non-negative eigenspaces of the boundary Dirac operator. The rho invariant is the image of η in ℚ/ℤ for rational homology spheres.

Cobordism and the Cobordism Ring of Manifolds with Corners: The Baas–Sullivan Construction and the ∞-Category of Bordisms

Manifolds with corners generalize manifolds with boundary by allowing local models [0,∞)^k × ℝn−k. The Baas–Sullivan cobordism theory Ω_*⟨C⟩ for a singularity pattern C (e.g., manifolds with corners, manifolds with faces) is defined by allowing singularities modeled on C in the cobordisms. For the pattern of manifolds with corners, the Baas–Sullivan spectrum M⟨C⟩ is the Thom spectrum of the universal stable normal bundle over the classifying space B⟨C⟩ of manifolds with corners. The homotopy groups π_* M⟨C⟩ = Ω_*⟨C⟩ fit into a long exact sequence relating them to ordinary cobordism groups via the corner forgetful map and the boundary map ∂: Ω_n⟨C⟩ → Ωn−1⟨C⟩. The ∞-category Bord_n⟨C⟩ of n-dimensional manifolds with corners has objects (n−1)-manifolds with corners, 1-morphisms n-manifolds with corners, and higher morphisms given by diffeomorphisms and isotopies. The cobordism hypothesis with corners (Lurie, Ayala–Francis) states that the symmetric monoidal ∞-category Bord_n⟨C⟩ is the free symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category with duals generated by a single fully dualizable object. This implies that an n-dimensional topological quantum field theory with corners is equivalent to a fully dualizable object in the target (∞,n)-category. The Baas–Sullivan construction is recovered by taking the group completion of the maximal ∞-groupoid of Bord_n⟨C⟩, yielding the Thom spectrum M⟨C⟩. For the pattern of manifolds with faces (where corners are products of boundaries), the spectrum M⟨faces⟩ is equivalent to the suspension spectrum of the classifying space BΓ_n of Haefliger structures, and its homotopy groups are the cobordism groups of manifolds with faces, which are isomorphic to the stable homotopy groups of spheres via the Pontryagin–Thom construction for manifolds with faces.

Cobordism and the Cobordism Classification of Manifolds with G₂ and Spin(7) Holonomy: The Joyce Invariants

Compact manifolds with holonomy G₂ (dimension 7) or Spin(7) (dimension 8) are Ricci-flat and admit parallel spinors, making them central to M-theory and string compactifications. The Joyce invariants are topological invariants that classify such manifolds up to cobordism. For a G₂-manifold M7, the associative 3-form φ and coassociative 4-form *φ define a G₂-structure. The cobordism group Ω7G₂ of manifolds with G₂-structure fits into an exact sequence

0 → Ω7Spin → Ω7G₂ → H3(M; ℤ) ⊕ H4(M; ℤ) → 0,

where the map to cohomology records the classes [φ] and [*φ]. The Joyce invariant ν(M) ∈ ℤ/48 is defined via the η-invariant of the Dirac operator twisted by the G₂-structure: ν(M) = 2η(D_M) mod 48. For Spin(7)-manifolds N8, the Cayley 4-form Φ defines a Spin(7)-structure, and the cobordism group Ω8Spin(7) maps to Ω8Spin ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ (generated by signature and Â-genus) with kernel detected by the ν-invariant ν(N) = Â(N) − σ(N)/8 mod ℤ, where σ is the signature. The Joyce–Karigiannis invariant for G₂-manifolds with boundary relates the bulk ν-invariant to the boundary eta invariant via an APS-type formula. These invariants are computable via the topological modular forms orientation: the string orientation MString → TMF factors through MG₂ and MSpin(7), and the induced map on homotopy groups sends the Joyce invariants to modular forms of weight 3 and 4 respectively. The K3 surface has Spin(7) holonomy and generates Ω8Spin(7) ≅ ℤ, with ν = 2. The Joyce manifold constructed by resolving T7/Γ has ν = 1 and generates the torsion summand in Ω7G₂. The cobordism ring of G₂-manifolds is not a polynomial ring; it has torsion detected by the ν-invariant and relations coming from the fact that φ ∧ *φ = 7 vol.

Cobordism and the Cobordism of Manifolds with Positive Scalar Curvature: The Gromov–Lawson–Rosenberg Conjecture and the Index Theorem

The Gromov–Lawson–Rosenberg (GLR) conjecture asserts that a closed spin manifold M^n with fundamental group π admits a metric of positive scalar curvature (psc) if and only if the Rosenberg index α(M) ∈ KO_n(C^*π) vanishes, where C^*π is the group C^*-algebra. The index α(M) is the image of the fundamental class [M] under the assembly map A: KO_n(Bπ) → KO_n(C^*π) composed with the map induced by the classifying map M → Bπ. The cobordism invariant α: Ω_nSpin(Bπ) → KO_n(C^*π) is a homomorphism from the spin cobordism group of Bπ. The GLR conjecture is known to hold for groups satisfying the Baum–Connes conjecture with coefficients (e.g., a-T-menable groups, hyperbolic groups) but fails in general: there exist groups π (e.g., certain discrete subgroups of Sp(n,1)) for which the assembly map is not injective, and spin manifolds with α(M) = 0 that do not admit psc metrics. The obstruction is measured by the relative index theorem for the Dirac operator on the universal cover tilde{M}: if M bounds a spin manifold W with π1(W) = π, then the APS index of the Dirac operator on W with boundary M gives an element in KOn+1(C^*π) that must vanish for M to admit psc. The Stolz theorem (1992) proves that for simply connected spin manifolds of dimension ≥ 5, the Â-genus is the only obstruction to psc, i.e., Ω_nSpin, psc = ker(Â: Ω_nSpin → ℤ) for n ≥ 5. The cobordism group of psc spin manifolds Ω_nSpin, psc fits into an exact sequence

Ωn+1Spin(Bπ) → Ω_nSpin, psc(Bπ) → Ω_nSpin(Bπ) → KO_n(C^*π),

where the first map is the boundary map for the surgery exact sequence. The positive mass theorem and the Schoen–Yau / Gromov–Lawson surgery technique show that psc is preserved under surgery in codimension ≥ 3, which is the geometric input for the cobordism classification.

  • Confusing the APS eta invariant (a real number mod ℤ) with the Atiyah–Singer index (an integer); the eta invariant is a boundary correction, not an index itself.
  • Assuming the Segal–Tom Dieck splitting holds for all compact Lie groups; it requires finite Weyl groups and fails for groups like U(n) with infinite Weyl groups.
  • Treating the Baas–Sullivan cobordism of manifolds with corners as identical to ordinary cobordism; the corner forgetful map has a non-trivial kernel detected by iterated boundary maps.
  • Believing the Joyce invariant ν(M) for G₂-manifolds is the same as the Â-genus; ν is a mod 48 invariant defined via the eta invariant, while Â is an integer for spin 8-manifolds and vanishes for 7-manifolds.
  • Assuming the Gromov–Lawson–Rosenberg conjecture holds for all groups; counterexamples exist where the Baum–Connes assembly map is not injective, and the obstruction lies in the cokernel of the assembly map.
  • Confusing the Rosenberg index α(M) ∈ KO_n(C^*π) with the Mishchenko–Fomenko higher signature; α is a KO-theory index, while higher signatures are in L-theory.
  • Overlooking that the Segal–Tom Dieck splitting for equivariant cobordism involves the Weyl group action on fixed point sets, not just the fixed point sets themselves; the summand is W_G H-equivariant cobordism of E W_G H ×W_G H X^H.

Cobordism and the Relative Pontryagin–Thom Construction for Manifolds with Boundary

The classical Pontryagin–Thom construction identifies the cobordism group ΩnG of closed n‑manifolds with a G‑structure with the stable homotopy group πn(MG) of the Thom spectrum MG. For manifolds with boundary one needs a relative version that keeps track of the boundary data. Let (W, ∂W) be a compact (n+1)‑manifold with a G‑structure on its stable normal bundle. The relative Pontryagin–Thom map sends the relative cobordism class of (W, ∂W) to an element of the relative stable homotopy group πn+1(MG, M∂G), where M∂G is the Thom spectrum of the G‑structure restricted to the boundary. This yields a long exact sequence of cobordism groups

⋯ → ΩnG(∂) → Ωn+1G(W, ∂W) → Ωn+1G → Ωn-1G(∂) → ⋯

which is the algebraic counterpart of the geometric exact sequence of a pair. The construction proceeds by embedding (W, ∂W) into (ℝN × [0,∞), ℝN × {0}) for large N, taking a tubular neighbourhood of the interior, and collapsing the complement to a point. The resulting map of Thom spaces represents the relative class. When G = O (unoriented) the relative groups are the homology groups of the Thom spectrum MO with coefficients in the boundary spectrum, and the boundary map coincides with the Thom isomorphism composed with the inclusion of the boundary. For G = SO the oriented relative groups detect the kernel of the forgetful map ΩnSO → ΩnO and are instrumental in the proof that the oriented cobordism ring is polynomial on the complex projective spaces ℂP2i. In surgery theory the relative construction provides the normal invariant of a degree‑one normal map (M, ∂M) → (X, ∂X); the surgery obstruction lives in the relative L‑group Ln+1(π1(X)) and the exact sequence of the pair matches the algebraic surgery exact sequence of Ranicki. A common computational tool is the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence for the relative theory, whose E2 page is Hp(W, ∂W; ΩqG) and which converges to Ωp+qG(W, ∂W). The differentials are determined by the action of the Steenrod algebra on the Thom class of MG, and the first non‑trivial differential often detects the Stiefel–Whitney or Pontryagin classes of the normal bundle.

Cobordism and the Cobordism of Foliated Manifolds: Haefliger Structures and the Classifying Space BΓq

A codimension‑q foliation on a manifold M is given by a Haefliger structure, i.e. a lift of the classifying map of the tangent bundle to the Haefliger classifying space BΓq. The space BΓq is the classifying space of the Haefliger groupoid Γq of germs of diffeomorphisms of ℝq fixing the origin. Two foliated manifolds (M0, ℱ0) and (M1, ℱ1) are foliated cobordant if there exists a foliated manifold (W, ℱ) with boundary ∂W = M0 ⊔ M1 such that the foliation on W restricts to the given foliations on the boundary. The set of foliated cobordism classes forms a group ΩnFolq under disjoint union. Haefliger showed that the Pontryagin–Thom construction extends to this setting: the Thom spectrum MΓq of the universal Haefliger bundle over BΓq represents foliated cobordism, so that ΩnFolq ≅ πn(MΓq). The homotopy type of MΓq is highly non‑trivial; for q = 1 it is equivalent to the sphere spectrum, reflecting the fact that every 1‑dimensional foliation is cobordant to a suspension of a circle action. For q ≥ 2 the groups contain rich torsion detected by the Godbillon–Vey class and other secondary characteristic classes. Thurston’s h‑principle for foliations implies that the inclusion of the space of foliations into the space of Haefliger structures is a homology isomorphism in a range, which allows the computation of low‑dimensional foliated cobordism groups via the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence for MΓq. The E2 term is Hp(BΓq; ΩqO) and the differentials are governed by the action of the Gel’fand–Fuks cohomology of the Lie algebra of formal vector fields. In particular, the Godbillon–Vey invariant appears as a differential d3 on the class dual to the generator of H3(BΓq; ℝ). The foliated cobordism ring Ω*Folq is not a polynomial ring; it contains nilpotent elements coming from the non‑trivial Massey products in the cohomology of BΓq. The relationship with contact cobordism is mediated by the Boothby–Wang construction: a contact manifold with a regular Reeb flow yields a foliated manifold whose leaves are the fibres of a principal S1‑bundle, and this induces a homomorphism ΩnContact → Ωn-1Fol1 that is an isomorphism in dimensions ≤ 5 after inverting 2.

Worked Example: Computing a Low‑Dimensional Foliated Cobordism Group
Compute Ω3Fol2 using the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence for MΓ2 and the known homology of BΓ2.
The E2 page is E2p,q = Hp(BΓ2; ΩqO). The unoriented cobordism groups ΩqO are ℤ/2 for q = 0,1,2 and 0 for q = 3. The homology of BΓ2 in low degrees is H0 = ℤ, H1 = 0, H2 = ℤ/2 (generated by the Godbillon–Vey class), H3 = 0. Thus the only possibly non‑zero terms with total degree 3 are E23,0 = 0, E22,1 = H2(BΓ2; ℤ/2) = ℤ/2, E21,2 = 0, E20,3 = 0. The differential d2: E22,1 → E20,2 is induced by the Steenrod square Sq2 on the Thom class of MO; it is an isomorphism because the Godbillon–Vey class is the image of the Stiefel–Whitney class w2 under the Haefliger map. Hence d2 kills the ℤ/2 in (2,1). No other differentials hit or leave this term, so E∞2,1 = 0. Consequently Ω3Fol2 = 0.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a cobordism group of foliated manifolds, first identify the relevant Haefliger classifying space BΓq and write down the Atiyah–Hirzebruch E2 page using the known unoriented cobordism coefficients. Remember that the first non‑trivial differential is often d2 = Sq2 or d3 = the Godbillon–Vey class. Compute only the terms that can contribute to the target degree; higher differentials rarely affect low‑dimensional groups. If the problem mentions a regular contact manifold, use the Boothby–Wang homomorphism to reduce to a 1‑dimensional foliation where the groups are known to be ℤ/2 in even dimensions and 0 in odd dimensions after inverting 2.

Cobordism and the Cobordism of CR Manifolds: The Tanaka–Webster Connection and the Bochner–Kobayashi Invariant

A CR (Cauchy–Riemann) structure of hypersurface type on a (2n+1)‑manifold M is a codimension‑1 distribution H ⊂ TM equipped with a complex structure J: H → H such that the Levi form L(X,Y) = dθ(JX,Y) is non‑degenerate for a local contact form θ annihilating H. The Tanaka–Webster connection is the unique linear connection on H preserving J and the Levi metric, with torsion determined by the Levi form. Two CR manifolds (M0, H0, J0) and (M1, H1, J1) are CR‑cobordant if there exists a CR manifold with boundary (W, H, J) whose boundary is the disjoint union of the given CR manifolds with the induced structures. The CR cobordism groups Ω2n+1CR form a graded ring under disjoint union and Cartesian product. The Pontryagin–Thom construction for CR manifolds uses the classifying space BCRn of CR structures, which is homotopy equivalent to the homotopy fibre of the map BU(n) → BO(2n) induced by the inclusion U(n) → O(2n). The Thom spectrum MCRn represents CR cobordism, so Ω*CR ≅ π*(MCR). The low‑dimensional groups are known: Ω1CR = 0, Ω3CR = ℤ/2 (detected by the Cartan invariant), Ω5CR = ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2 (the ℤ summand is the Chern–Simons invariant of the Tanaka–Webster connection, the ℤ/2 is the Kervaire semi‑characteristic). The Bochner–Kobayashi invariant β(M) ∈ ℤ/8 is defined for a compact strictly pseudoconvex CR 3‑manifold as the η‑invariant of the Kohn Laplacian on (0,1)‑forms modulo integers. It satisfies β(∂W) = 0 for any CR 4‑manifold W with boundary, so it descends to a homomorphism Ω3CR → ℤ/8 whose image is the subgroup ℤ/2 generated by the standard CR structure on S3. The invariant is related to the index theorem for the ∂̄b complex: the index of the Kohn Laplacian on a CR manifold with boundary equals the integral of the Tanaka–Webster curvature plus the η‑invariant of the boundary. In higher dimensions the CR cobordism ring maps to the complex cobordism ring MU* via the forgetful map that sends a CR manifold to its underlying almost complex structure on the contact distribution; the kernel is detected by the secondary invariants coming from the Tanaka–Webster torsion. The Landweber exact functor theorem applies to the CR cobordism theory after inverting 2, yielding a homology theory that is a module over MU* and whose coefficient ring is MU*[β]/(2β, β2).

Cobordism and the Cobordism of Sasakian Manifolds: Transverse Kähler Geometry and the Boothby–Wang Fibration

A Sasakian structure on a (2n+1)‑manifold M is a contact metric structure (η, ξ, Φ, g) such that the cone C(M) = M × ℝ+ with metric dr2 + r2g is Kähler. Equivalently, the Reeb vector field ξ generates a foliation ℱξ whose leaf space (when the flow is regular) is a Kähler orbifold, and the transverse geometry is Kähler. The Boothby–Wang theorem states that a compact regular Sasakian manifold is the total space of a principal S1‑bundle over a compact Kähler manifold (or orbifold) with Euler class equal to the Kähler class. Two Sasakian manifolds (M0, η0, ξ0, Φ0, g0) and (M1, η1, ξ1, Φ1, g1) are Sasakian cobordant if there exists a Sasakian manifold with boundary (W, η, ξ, Φ, g) restricting to the given structures on ∂W = M0 ⊔ M1. The Sasakian cobordism groups Ω2n+1Sas form a graded ring. The Pontryagin–Thom construction for Sasakian manifolds uses the classifying space BSasn of Sasakian structures, which fits into a fibration BSasn → BSO(2n+1) → BΓ1 where the last map classifies the Reeb foliation. The Thom spectrum MSasn represents Sasakian cobordism. The forgetful map to contact cobordism Ω*Sas → Ω*Contact is surjective in dimensions ≤ 7 and its kernel is generated by the Boothby–Wang construction: a Sasakian manifold that bounds a Sasakian manifold with a regular Reeb flow is contact cobordant to zero. The transverse Kähler geometry yields a homomorphism Ω2n+1Sas → Ω2nU (complex cobordism) sending a Sasakian manifold to the transverse Kähler orbifold; this map is a ring homomorphism after inverting 2. The η‑invariant of the transverse Dirac operator defines a homomorphism Ω4k+1Sas → ℝ/ℤ that detects torsion not seen by the transverse Kähler map. For example, the standard Sasakian structure on S2n+1 generates a ℤ summand in Ω2n+1Sas mapped to the generator of Ω2nU ≅ ℤ (the complex projective space ℂPn). The Sasakian cobordism ring is not polynomial; it contains nilpotent elements coming from the non‑trivial Massey products in the cohomology of BSasn. The relationship with 3‑Sasakian and hyperkähler geometry is mediated by the quaternionic Boothby–Wang fibration: a 3‑Sasakian manifold is a principal Sp(1)‑bundle over a quaternionic Kähler orbifold, and the induced map on cobordism groups factors through the Spin(7) cobordism groups discussed earlier.

  • Confusing the relative Pontryagin–Thom construction with the absolute one; the relative version requires a Thom spectrum of the pair and yields a long exact sequence, not just a single isomorphism.
  • Assuming the Haefliger classifying space BΓq has the homology of a point; its cohomology contains the Godbillon–Vey class and Gel’fand–Fuks classes that drive non‑trivial differentials in the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence.
  • Treating the Bochner–Kobayashi invariant as an integer; it is a mod 8 invariant defined via the η‑invariant of the Kohn Laplacian, and only its mod 2 reduction detects the ℤ/2 summand in Ω3CR.
  • Believing the forgetful map from Sasakian cobordism to contact cobordism is injective; it has a large kernel generated by Boothby–Wang fibrations over Kähler orbifolds that bound Sasakian manifolds.
  • Overlooking that the transverse Kähler map Ω*Sas → Ω*U is only a ring homomorphism after inverting 2; at the prime 2 there are additional torsion interactions with the Reeb foliation.
  • Assuming the CR cobordism ring is a polynomial algebra over ℤ; it contains nilpotent elements from the Tanaka–Webster torsion and the Landweber exact functor theorem only applies after inverting 2.
  • These traps highlight the most common conceptual pitfalls in advanced cobordism theory.

Cobordism and the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer Index Theorem for Manifolds with Boundary

The Atiyah–Patodi–Singer (APS) index theorem extends the classical Atiyah–Singer index theorem to compact manifolds with boundary by introducing the eta invariant of a self‑adjoint elliptic operator on the boundary. For a Dirac operator D on a compact spin manifold W with boundary M, the index of D with APS boundary conditions equals the integral of the Â‑genus over W minus one half of the eta invariant η(D|_M). This formula shows that the combination ∫_W Â − ½ η(D|_M) is a cobordism invariant of the pair (W, M). In particular, if two closed spin manifolds M₀ and M₁ are spin cobordant via a spin manifold W, then the difference of their eta invariants equals twice the integral of the Â‑genus over W. Consequently the reduced eta invariant η̄(M) = η(M) mod ℤ defines a homomorphism from the spin cobordism group ΩSpin4k−1 to ℝ/ℤ. The APS theorem also provides a geometric interpretation of the KO‑theoretic α‑invariant: for a spin manifold M, α(M) ∈ KO−dim M(pt) coincides with the index of the Dirac operator on any spin manifold bounding M, and the eta invariant detects the torsion part of α. The theorem has been refined to families of manifolds with boundary, yielding the Bismut–Cheeger eta form and the index theorem for families, which underlies the construction of the determinant line bundle on the moduli space of Riemannian metrics. In the context of cobordism, the APS theorem shows that the spin cobordism ring ΩSpin_* maps to the ring of eta invariants, and the kernel of this map is precisely the image of the Â‑genus on boundaries. This relationship is central to the proof of the Gromov–Lawson–Rosenberg conjecture for spin manifolds with fundamental group of positive scalar curvature, where the obstruction lives in the KO‑theory of the group C*‑algebra and is detected by the higher eta invariants of the APS type.

Cobordism and the Stolz Exact Sequence for Positive Scalar Curvature

Stephan Stolz constructed a long exact sequence that relates the spin cobordism groups ΩSpin_*, the real K‑theory groups KO_*, and the groups of manifolds admitting metrics of positive scalar curvature. For a simply connected spin manifold M of dimension n ≥ 5, the obstruction to the existence of a positive scalar curvature metric lies in the kernel of the map ΩSpin_n → KO_n induced by the Â‑genus. Stolz proved that the sequence … → ΩSpinn+1 → KOn+1 → P_n → ΩSpin_n → KO_n → … is exact, where P_n denotes the cobordism group of spin manifolds with positive scalar curvature modulo those that bound spin manifolds with positive scalar curvature. The map ΩSpin_n → KO_n is the α‑invariant; its image consists of the KO‑theoretic indices of Dirac operators. The connecting homomorphism KOn+1 → P_n sends a KO‑class to the obstruction class of a manifold representing that class. This exact sequence implies that a spin manifold admits a positive scalar curvature metric if and only if its α‑invariant vanishes and it is null‑cobordant in the positive scalar curvature sense. The sequence also incorporates the surgery exact sequence of Gromov–Lawson, showing that the obstruction group for positive scalar curvature on spin manifolds is a direct summand of the spin cobordism group. In dimensions 8k+1 and 8k+2 the KO‑groups are ℤ/2, and the corresponding ℤ/2‑valued invariants are the Arf invariant of the associated quadratic form on the middle homology. The Stolz sequence has been generalized to manifolds with fundamental group, where the target KO‑theory is replaced by the KO‑theory of the group C*‑algebra, and the positive scalar curvature obstruction becomes the higher ρ‑invariant of Piazza–Schick.

Worked Example: Computing the α‑Invariant of a Spin 8‑Manifold
Let M be a closed spin 8‑manifold with Â‑genus 2 and Pontryagin numbers p₁²[M] = 4, p₂[M] = 6. Compute the α‑invariant α(M) ∈ KO−8(pt) ≅ ℤ and determine whether M can admit a metric of positive scalar curvature.
The KO‑group KO−8(pt) is isomorphic to ℤ, generated by the class of the Bott element. For a spin 8‑manifold the α‑invariant equals the Â‑genus. The Â‑genus in dimension 8 is given by Â(M) = (7p₁² − 4p₂)/5760. Substituting the given Pontryagin numbers: (7·4 − 4·6)/5760 = (28 − 24)/5760 = 4/5760 = 1/1440. However the Â‑genus of a spin manifold is an integer; the given numbers must be scaled so that the Â‑genus equals 2 as stated. Therefore α(M) = 2 ∈ ℤ. Since α(M) ≠ 0, the α‑invariant does not vanish. By the Stolz exact sequence, a non‑zero α‑invariant obstructs the existence of a positive scalar curvature metric on M. Hence M cannot admit a metric of positive scalar curvature.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the obstruction to positive scalar curvature on a spin manifold, first compute the α‑invariant via the Â‑genus or the index of the Dirac operator. If the α‑invariant is non‑zero, the obstruction is immediate. If it vanishes, check whether the manifold is null‑cobordant in the positive scalar curvature sense using the Stolz exact sequence; this often reduces to verifying whether a certain KO‑class lifts to a spin cobordism class. Memorise the low‑dimensional KO‑groups: KO₀=ℤ, KO₁=ℤ/2, KO₂=ℤ/2, KO₃=0, KO₄=ℤ, KO₅=0, KO₆=0, KO₇=0, and their 8‑periodicity.

Cobordism and the Algebraic K‑Theory of Thom Spectra

The algebraic K‑theory of a Thom spectrum Mξ associated to a stable spherical fibration ξ over a space X provides a bridge between manifold cobordism and the algebraic K‑theory of the underlying ring spectrum. For the universal Thom spectra MO, MSO, MSpin, MU, the algebraic K‑theory groups K_*(Mξ) have been computed rationally and at odd primes using the cyclotomic trace map to topological cyclic homology (TC). The Dundas–Goodwillie–McCarthy theorem identifies the relative K‑theory of a map of ring spectra with the relative TC, allowing the computation of K_*(Mξ) via the Bökstedt–Hsiang–Madsen calculation of TC of Thom spectra. In particular, K_*(MU) ⊗ ℚ is a polynomial algebra on generators in degrees 2, 4, 6, … corresponding to the complex cobordism generators ℂP^n. The torsion in K_*(MU) is closely related to the torsion in the stable homotopy groups of spheres via the Hurewicz map MU_* → π_*^S. For MSpin, the algebraic K‑theory captures the Stolz exact sequence: the map K_*(MSpin) → KO_* induced by the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro orientation coincides with the α‑invariant on the image of the Hurewicz map. The fiber of this map is the algebraic K‑theory of the connective cover of MSpin, which encodes the higher obstructions to positive scalar curvature. Recent work of Clausen–Mathew–Morrow shows that the p‑adic completion of K_*(Mξ) can be expressed in terms of the p‑adic étale cohomology of the moduli stack of formal groups associated to ξ, linking algebraic K‑theory of Thom spectra to the chromatic filtration. This perspective explains why the algebraic K‑theory of MU detects the beta family in the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence and why the redshift conjecture predicts that K(MU) has chromatic height one higher than MU itself.

Cobordism and the Homotopy Groups of the Thom Spectrum MSpin

The homotopy groups π_*(MSpin) are the spin cobordism groups ΩSpin_*. Their computation proceeds via the Adams spectral sequence (ASS) with E₂‑term ExtA(1)s,t(H^*(MSpin; 𝔽₂), 𝔽₂), where A(1) is the subalgebra of the Steenrod algebra generated by Sq¹ and Sq². Anderson, Brown, and Peterson showed that the ASS for MSpin collapses at E₂ for stems up to dimension 20, and they identified the 2‑primary part of ΩSpin_* as a polynomial algebra on generators in dimensions 1, 2, 4, 5, 8, 9, … modulo the relation that the generator in dimension 1 squares to zero. The odd‑primary components are detected by the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (ANSS) for MSpin, whose E₂‑term is ExtBP_*BPs,t(BP_*(MSpin), BP_*). The BP‑homology of MSpin is a free BP_*‑module on the Thom class and the Stiefel–Whitney classes w₂, w₄, … expressed in terms of the universal p‑typical formal group law. The ANSS reveals that the odd torsion in ΩSpin_* is concentrated in dimensions congruent to 1 or 2 mod 8 and is of exponent p. The Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence, a variant of the ANSS using the spectrum BP⟨1⟩, gives a more tractable computation and shows that the spin cobordism ring is generated by the manifolds ℍP², the K3 surface, and the Bott manifold in dimension 8, together with the Arf invariant manifolds in dimensions 1 and 2 mod 8. The Hurewicz map ΩSpin_* → π_*^S is injective in a range and its image is the kernel of the J‑homomorphism on the 2‑primary component. The computation of π_*(MSpin) is essential for the classification of exotic spheres via the Kervaire invariant, because the Kervaire invariant one problem reduces to detecting a certain element in π2k(MSpin) that maps to the Kervaire element in π2k^S.

Cobordism and the Classification of Manifolds with G₂ Holonomy via Cobordism Invariants

A compact 7‑manifold with a torsion‑free G₂ structure has holonomy contained in G₂ and admits a parallel 3‑form φ and a parallel 4‑form *φ. The deformation theory of such structures is governed by the moduli space of torsion‑free G₂ structures, which is locally isomorphic to H³(M; ℝ). Cobordism invariants provide obstructions to the existence of G₂ metrics and classify G₂ manifolds up to G₂ cobordism. The G₂ cobordism group ΩG₂7 is a subgroup of the spin cobordism group ΩSpin7 ≅ 0, so every spin 7‑manifold bounds a spin 8‑manifold. However, the G₂ condition imposes additional constraints: the φ‑class [φ] ∈ H³(M; ℤ) must be integral and satisfy a certain quadratic refinement given by the Chern–Simons functional of the associated Spin(7) connection on the cone. The work of Crowley–Nordström shows that the G₂ cobordism group fits into an exact sequence 0 → H³(M; ℤ)/torsion → ΩG₂7 → ΩSpin7 → 0, and since ΩSpin7 = 0, ΩG₂7 ≅ H³(M; ℤ)/torsion. In particular, the φ‑class provides a complete invariant for G₂ cobordism. For 8‑manifolds with Spin(7) holonomy, the relevant cobordism group is ΩSpin(7)8, which maps to ΩSpin8 ≅ ℤ² generated by the Â‑genus and the Pontryagin number p₂. The Spin(7) cobordism invariants are the Â‑genus and the integral of the Cayley 4‑form Φ over the fundamental class. The classification of G₂ and Spin(7) manifolds up to cobordism is therefore reduced to the computation of these characteristic numbers, which can be evaluated using the index theorem for the Dirac operator coupled to the associated vector bundles. The relationship with M‑theory compactifications makes these cobordism groups physically relevant: the torsion‑free G₂ manifolds that bound Spin(7) manifolds correspond to anomaly‑free configurations in seven‑dimensional supergravity.

  • Confusing the APS eta invariant with the ordinary eta invariant of a closed manifold; the APS invariant requires a bounding manifold and a product structure near the boundary.
  • Assuming the Stolz exact sequence holds for non‑simply‑connected manifolds without replacing KO‑theory by the KO‑theory of the group C*‑algebra.
  • Treating the algebraic K‑theory of a Thom spectrum as the same as the topological K‑theory of the underlying space; they differ by the cyclotomic trace and the Dundas–Goodwillie–McCarthy theorem.
  • Believing the Adams spectral sequence for MSpin collapses at E₂ in all dimensions; it only collapses in a finite range and higher differentials appear beyond dimension 20.
  • Overlooking that the φ‑class in G₂ cobordism is only defined up to torsion; the free part of H³(M; ℤ) classifies G₂ cobordism, while torsion components are invisible to the φ‑class.
  • Assuming the Spin(7) cobordism invariants are only the Â‑genus; the Cayley 4‑form integral provides an independent invariant that detects exotic Spin(7) structures.
  • Neglecting the role of the Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence in computing odd‑primary torsion in spin cobordism; the classical Adams spectral sequence at odd primes is insufficient.

Cobordism and the Cobordism of Foliated Manifolds: Haefliger Structures and the Godbillon–Vey Invariant

The cobordism theory of foliated manifolds, developed by Haefliger in the 1970s, classifies smooth foliations up to foliated cobordism. A foliated cobordism between two closed foliated manifolds (M₁, ℱ₁) and (M₂, ℱ₂) is a compact foliated manifold with boundary (W, ℱ) such that ∂W = M₁ ⊔ M₂ and ℱ restricts to ℱ₁ and ℱ₂ on the boundary components. The set of foliated cobordism classes of codimension‑q foliations forms an abelian group ΩFolq under disjoint union. Haefliger showed that ΩFolq is isomorphic to the homotopy groups of the Haefliger classifying space BΓq, the classifying space for codimension‑q Haefliger structures (micro‑flexible foliations). The Pontryagin–Thom construction for foliations identifies ΩFolq with π*(MΓq), where MΓq is the Thom spectrum of the universal normal bundle over BΓq. The rational cohomology of BΓq is a polynomial algebra on the Godbillon–Vey classes gv2i+1} ∈ H2i+1(BΓq; ℝ) for i ≥ 1 with 2i+1 ≤ q, together with the Gelfand–Fuks classes c2i} ∈ H2i(BΓq; ℝ). The Godbillon–Vey invariant gv(ℱ) = ∫M η ∧ (dη)q for a defining 1‑form η of a codimension‑1 foliation is the primary non‑trivial cobordism invariant; Thurston proved that every real number occurs as the Godbillon–Vey number of some codimension‑1 foliation on S³, so the Godbillon–Vey invariant detects a ℝ‑summand in ΩFol1 ⊗ ℝ. For higher codimensions, the Haefliger–Thurston theorem states that the inclusion BΓq → BO(q) induces an isomorphism on rational cohomology in degrees * ≤ q/2, so the rational foliated cobordism groups are detected by Pontryagin numbers of the normal bundle in this range. The torsion in ΩFolq is more subtle: Hurder showed that the 2‑primary torsion in ΩFol1 contains a ℤ/2 summand detected by the Stiefel–Whitney number w₁²[M] of the normal bundle, and the odd‑primary torsion is related to the image of the J‑homomorphism in the stable homotopy groups of spheres via the map BΓq → BO(q) → BO. The computation of ΩFolq for q ≥ 2 remains incomplete; the Adams spectral sequence for MΓq has E₂‑term ExtA(H*(MΓq), 𝔽₂) where the A‑module structure of H*(BΓq; 𝔽₂) is known only in a range. The Haefliger number of a foliation, defined as the evaluation of the fundamental class on the pullback of the universal Haefliger class, provides a complete invariant for foliated cobordism of codimension‑q foliations on simply‑connected manifolds when q = 1, but fails in higher codimensions due to the non‑injectivity of the Hurewicz map π*(MΓq) → H*(MΓq).

Cobordism and the Cobordism of CR Manifolds: The Kohn–Rossi Complex and CR Cobordism Groups

A CR manifold of hypersurface type is a (2n+1)‑dimensional manifold M equipped with a rank‑n complex subbundle H¹,⁰ ⊂ ℂ ⊗ TM satisfying H¹,⁰ ∩ H⁰,¹ = 0 and [H¹,⁰, H¹,⁰] ⊂ H¹,⁰ (integrability). The Levi form L: H¹,⁰ × H⁰,¹ → ℂ ⊗ TM / (H¹,⁰ ⊕ H⁰,¹) is a Hermitian form measuring the failure of H¹,⁰ to be a complex tangent bundle. Two CR manifolds (M₁, H₁) and (M₂, H₂) are CR cobordant if there exists a compact CR manifold with boundary (W, H) such that ∂W = M₁ ⊔ M₂ and H restricts to H₁, H₂. The CR cobordism groups ΩCR2n+1 form a graded ring under disjoint union and Cartesian product. The Kohn–Rossi complex (Ω0,qb(M), ∂̄b) computes the Kohn–Rossi cohomology HqKR(M), which is a CR invariant. For a strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold (Levi form positive definite), the Kohn–Rossi cohomology vanishes for 1 ≤ q ≤ n−1 by the Kohn–Rossi theorem, and H⁰KR(M) consists of CR functions while HⁿKR(M) is dual to H⁰KR(M) via the Kohn Laplacian □b. The CR index theorem of Boutet de Monvel and Guillemin expresses the Euler characteristic of the Kohn–Rossi complex as an integral of a characteristic form: χKR(M) = ∫M Tdb(M) ∧ θ, where Tdb is the Tanaka–Webster Todd form and θ is a contact form. This index is a CR cobordism invariant. The CR cobordism group ΩCR3 for 3‑dimensional CR manifolds is isomorphic to ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2, generated by the standard CR structure on S³ (detected by the CR index = 1) and the Rossi example (a compact strictly pseudoconvex CR 3‑manifold not bounding a Stein variety, detected by the ℤ/2 invariant). For higher dimensions, the CR cobordism groups ΩCR2n+1 are related to the complex cobordism groups ΩU2n via the CR–complex cobordism exact sequence ⋯ → ΩU2n+2 → ΩCR2n+1 → ΩU2n → ⋯ arising from the fact that a strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold bounds a Stein manifold with isolated singularities, whose resolution gives a complex cobordism. The Chern–Moser normal form for non‑degenerate CR structures shows that the CR curvature tensor provides obstructions to CR cobordism; the Chern–Moser invariants are polynomial in the curvature and its covariant derivatives, and their integrals over M give CR cobordism invariants. The Fefferman metric construction associates a Lorentzian conformal structure to a CR manifold, and the Fefferman–Graham obstruction tensor yields further invariants. The computation of ΩCR2n+1 for n ≥ 2 is open; the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for the CR Thom spectrum MCR has E₂‑term involving the BP‑homology of the classifying space BCR(n), which is not fully known.

Worked Problem: Godbillon–Vey Invariant of a Suspension Foliation
Let M = S¹ × S² with the foliation ℱ given by the fibers of the projection π: S¹ × S² → S¹. Compute the Godbillon–Vey invariant gv(ℱ) and determine whether (M, ℱ) is foliated cobordant to the trivial foliation on S¹ × S².
The foliation ℱ is defined by the 1‑form ω = dθ on S¹ pulled back to M. Since dω = 0, we can take η = 0 satisfying dη = ω. The Godbillon–Vey form is η ∧ dη = 0, so gv(ℱ) = ∫M 0 = 0. The trivial foliation on S¹ × S² (leaves = points × S²) also has gv = 0. However, the Haefliger number (the evaluation of the fundamental class on the pullback of the universal class in H³(BΓ₁; ℤ)) distinguishes them: the suspension foliation has Haefliger number 1 (it represents the generator of π₃(BΓ₁) ≅ ℤ), while the trivial foliation has Haefliger number 0. Therefore (M, ℱ) is not foliated cobordant to the trivial foliation, even though both have vanishing Godbillon–Vey invariant. This illustrates that gv is not a complete invariant for foliated cobordism in codimension 1.
Exam Strategy: Foliated vs. CR Cobordism
When a question asks for cobordism invariants of foliations, always distinguish between the Godbillon–Vey invariant (real‑valued, detects ℝ‑summands) and the Haefliger number (integral, detects the ℤ summand in π₃(BΓ₁)). For CR manifolds, the CR index (Kohn–Rossi Euler characteristic) is the primary integer invariant; the ℤ/2 Rossi invariant detects non‑fillable strictly pseudoconvex structures. Remember the exact sequence linking CR cobordism to complex cobordism: a CR manifold bounds a Stein variety iff its CR index vanishes and the Rossi invariant is zero.

Cobordism and the Cobordism of Sasakian Manifolds: Transverse Kähler Geometry and the Basic Hodge Numbers

A Sasakian manifold is a (2n+1)‑dimensional contact manifold (M, η) whose metric cone C(M) = M × ℝ⁺ with metric dr² + r²g is Kähler. Equivalently, M carries a transverse Kähler structure on the foliation ℱξ generated by the Reeb vector field ξ. The basic cohomology H*B(M/ℱξ) of the Reeb foliation is isomorphic to the orbifold cohomology of the Kähler quotient M/ξ (when the Reeb action is quasi‑regular). Two Sasakian manifolds (M₁, η₁) and (M₂, η₂) are Sasakian cobordant if there exists a compact Sasakian manifold with boundary (W, η) such that ∂W = M₁ ⊔ M₂ and η restricts to η₁, η₂. The Sasakian cobordism groups ΩSas2n+1 form a graded ring. The transverse Chern classes cBi ∈ H2iB(M/ℱξ) of the transverse holomorphic tangent bundle yield Sasakian cobordism invariants via integration over the fundamental class: ∫M cBi₁ ⋯ cBiₖ ∧ η for i₁ + ⋯ + iₖ = n. The basic Hodge numbers hp,qB = dim Hp,qB(M/ℱξ) are also cobordism invariants for quasi‑regular Sasakian manifolds, because they are the Hodge numbers of the Kähler orbifold quotient. The Sasaki–Einstein condition (Ricci‑flat transverse Kähler metric) implies cB1 = 0 in basic cohomology, so all transverse Chern numbers involving cB1 vanish. The Futaki invariant of the transverse Kähler structure, defined as F(X) = ∫M X(θ) ωⁿ ∧ η for a holomorphic vector field X on the quotient, is a Sasakian cobordism invariant when the Reeb action is regular. For irregular Reeb actions, the transverse Kähler–Ricci flow preserves the Sasakian cobordism class. The Sasakian cobordism group ΩSas3 is isomorphic to ℤ, generated by the standard Sasakian structure on S³ (the Hopf fibration S¹ → S³ → S²), detected by the transverse Chern number ∫S³ cB1 ∧ η = 1. For n = 2 (dimension 5), ΩSas5 contains a ℤ² summand generated by S⁵ (with the standard Sasakian structure from S¹ → S⁵ → ℂP²) and the Sasaki–Einstein manifold T¹,¹ = SU(2) × SU(2) / U(1), distinguished by the transverse Chern numbers cB1² and cB2. The Sasakian Thom spectrum MSas is the Thom spectrum of the universal transverse tangent bundle over the classifying space BSas(n) of Sasakian structures. The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for MSas has E₂‑term ExtBP*BP(BP*(MSas), BP*), where BP*(MSas) is a free BP*‑module on the Thom class and the transverse Chern classes. The Landweber exact functor theorem applies to MSas because the transverse formal group law is the universal formal group law, so MSas is a Landweber exact spectrum and its homology theories are computable via the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence. The Sasakian cobordism ring ΩSas* ⊗ ℚ is a polynomial algebra on generators in dimensions 3, 7, 11, … corresponding to the transverse Chern numbers of the standard Sasakian spheres S2n+1.

Cobordism and the Cobordism of Manifolds with Spin^h and Spin^e Structures: The Higher Spin Tower Beyond Spin^c

The higher spin groups Spinh(n) and Spine(n) fit into the Whitehead tower of BO(n) between Spinc(n) and String(n). They are defined as Spinh(n) = (Spin(n) × U(1)) / ℤ₂ and Spine(n) = (Spin(n) × Sp(1)) / ℤ₂, where the ℤ₂ quotient identifies the central elements (−1, −1). A Spinh structure on a manifold M is a lift of the structure group of the tangent bundle from SO(n) to Spinh(n); equivalently, it is a Spinc structure whose determinant line bundle L admits a square root L¹/² (i.e., c₁(L) ≡ 0 mod 4). A Spine structure is a Spinc structure together with a quaternionic line bundle H such that c₂(H) ≡ w₂(TM) mod 2. The Spinh cobordism groups ΩSpinh* and Spine cobordism groups ΩSpine* are the homotopy groups of the Thom spectra MSpinh and MSpine. The Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence for MSpinh has E₂‑term ExtBP*BP(BP*(MSpinh), BP*) where BP*(MSpinh) is a free BP*‑module on the Thom class and the Spinh characteristic classes qi ∈ H4i(BSpinh; ℤ) defined by qi = c2i(S⁺ ⊗ L¹/²) for the spinor bundle S⁺. The Spinh cobordism ring ΩSpinh* ⊗ ℚ is a polynomial algebra on generators in dimensions 4, 8, 12, … corresponding to the q‑genera qi[M]. The 2‑primary torsion in ΩSpinh* is detected by the KO‑theory orientation MSpinh → KO; the α‑invariant α: ΩSpinh8k+1, 8k+2 → ℤ/2 is the Arf invariant of the associated quadratic form on H4k+1(M; ℤ/2). The Spine cobordism ring ΩSpine* ⊗ ℚ is a polynomial algebra on generators in dimensions 4, 8, 12, … and 7, 11, 15, … corresponding to the quaternionic Pontryagin classes pi(H) and the quaternionic Chern classes c2i+1(H). The Spine Dirac operator coupled to the quaternionic line bundle H gives an index in KO⁻⁴(pt) ≅ ℤ, which is a Spine cobordism invariant. The Spinh and Spine Thom spectra are related by the fiber sequence MSpinh → MSpine → MSpinc induced by the group homomorphisms Spinh → Spine → Spinc. The Hurewicz map ΩSpinh* → π*S has image contained in the kernel of the J‑homomorphism on the 2‑primary component, and the Anderson–Brown–Peterson spectral sequence shows that the odd‑primary torsion in ΩSpinh* is of exponent p and concentrated in dimensions congruent to 1 or 2 mod 8. The Spinh cobordism group ΩSpinh4 ≅ ℤ is generated by K3 with its unique Spinh structure (since c₁ = 0 for K3), detected by the q₁‑genus = 2. The Spine cobordism group ΩSpine4 ≅ ℤ² is generated by K3 and ℍP², detected by the quaternionic Pontryagin numbers p₁(H)[M] and p₁(TM)[M].

Cobordism and the Cobordism of Manifolds with Ninebrane Structures: The Next Stage of the Whitehead Tower

The Ninebrane group Ninebrane(n) is the 3‑connected cover of String(n), sitting in the Whitehead tower ⋯ → Ninebrane(n) → String(n) → Spin(n) → SO(n). It is defined as the homotopy fiber of the map String(n) → K(ℤ, 8) classifying the fractional Pontryagin class p₂/6 ∈ H⁸(BString; ℤ). A Ninebrane structure on a manifold M is a String structure together with a trivialization of the p₂/6 class; equivalently, it is a lift of the structure group to Ninebrane(n). The Ninebrane cobordism groups ΩNinebrane* = π*(MNinebrane) are the homotopy groups of the Thom spectrum of the universal normal bundle over BNinebrane. The rational Ninebrane cobordism ring ΩNinebrane* ⊗ ℚ is a polynomial algebra on generators in dimensions 8, 12, 16, 20, 24, … corresponding to the Pontryagin numbers of the tangent bundle that survive the String and Ninebrane conditions. The first non‑trivial Ninebrane cobordism group is ΩNinebrane8 ≅ ℤ, generated by the Bott manifold (the total space of the S³‑bundle over S⁴ with clutching function the generator of π₃(SO(4)) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ), which admits a Ninebrane structure because its p₂/6 vanishes. The 2‑primary torsion in ΩNinebrane* is detected by the tmf (topological modular forms) orientation MNinebrane → tmf constructed by Hill, Hopkins, and Ravenel; the tmf‑Hurewicz map ΩNinebrane* → tmf* is injective in a range and its image is characterized by the vanishing of certain modular forms of weight k and level N. The odd‑primary torsion in ΩNinebrane* is computed via the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for MNinebrane at height 3; the E₂‑term is ExtBP*BP(BP*(MNinebrane), BP*) where BP*(MNinebrane) is a free BP*‑module on the Thom class and the Ninebrane characteristic classes ni ∈ H8i(BNinebrane; ℤ) defined by ni = p2i/6 (suitably normalized). The Ninebrane cobordism ring ΩNinebrane* is related to the String cobordism ring ΩString* by the fiber sequence MNinebrane → MString → Σ⁸ Hℤ induced by the map BString → K(ℤ, 8). This yields a long exact sequence ⋯ → ΩStringk+8 → ΩNinebranek → ΩStringk → ΩStringk+7 → ⋯ where the connecting map is the p₂/6 evaluation. The Ninebrane Dirac operator coupled to the Ninebrane bundle (a 3‑connected cover of the frame bundle) gives an index in tmf⁻⁸(pt), which is a Ninebrane cobordism invariant. The Witten genus of a Ninebrane manifold is a modular form of weight dim(M)/2 and level 1 with q‑expansion having integer coefficients; the String orientation of tmf refines the Witten genus to a map MString → tmf, and the Ninebrane orientation further refines it to a map MNinebrane → tmf⟨8⟩ where tmf⟨8⟩ is the 8‑connected cover of tmf. The chromatic height of MNinebrane is 3, so its Morava K‑theory K(3)* (MNinebrane) is non‑trivial and the K(3)‑local sphere LK(3) S⁰ receives a map from MNinebrane that detects the β‑family elements in the stable homotopy groups of spheres.

  • Confusing the Godbillon–Vey invariant (a real number) with the Haefliger number (an integer); the former detects ℝ‑summands in foliated cobordism, the latter detects the ℤ summand in π₃(BΓ₁).
  • Assuming the CR index (Kohn–Rossi Euler characteristic) is the only CR cobordism invariant; the Rossi ℤ/2 invariant detects non‑fillable strictly pseudoconvex structures that have CR index zero.
  • Treating the basic Hodge numbers of a Sasakian manifold as invariants of the contact structure alone; they depend on the transverse Kähler structure and are only defined for quasi‑regular Reeb actions.
  • Believing Spinh and Spine structures are equivalent to Spinc structures with extra data; Spinh requires the determinant line bundle to have a square root (c₁ ≡ 0 mod 4), while Spine requires a quaternionic line bundle with c₂ ≡ w₂ mod 2.
  • Overlooking that the Ninebrane condition p₂/6 = 0 is strictly stronger than the String condition p₁/2 = 0; many String manifolds (e.g., certain 8‑manifolds with p₂ ≠ 0) do not admit Ninebrane structures.
  • Confusing the tmf orientation of MString with the Ninebrane orientation; the latter factors through the 8‑connected cover tmf⟨8⟩ and detects β‑family elements at chromatic height 3.
  • Assuming the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for foliated, CR, or Sasakian Thom spectra collapses at E₂; higher differentials appear beyond the range of known computations, and the E₂‑terms involve Ext groups over BP*BP that are not fully determined.

Cobordism and the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer Index Theorem: Eta Invariants, Rho Invariants, and the Cobordism Classification of Manifolds with Boundary

The Atiyah–Patodi–Singer (APS) index theorem extends the Atiyah–Singer index theorem to manifolds with boundary by introducing a boundary correction term expressed through the eta invariant of a self-adjoint elliptic operator on the boundary. For a compact spin manifold W with boundary M = ∂W, the index of the Dirac operator DW with APS boundary conditions is

ind(DW) = ∫W Â(TW) − η(DM)/2,

where Â(TW) is the Â-genus form and η(DM) = ∑λ≠0 sign(λ)|λ|−s|s=0 is the analytic continuation of the eta function of the boundary Dirac operator. The rho invariant ρ(M, g) = η(DM) − η(DSn) (for a reference metric on the sphere) is a cobordism invariant of the pair (M, g) modulo boundaries of spin manifolds. It defines a homomorphism ρ: ΩSpinn−1(BΓ) → ℝ/ℤ for a discrete group Γ, where BΓ classifies flat bundles. The APS theorem implies that the relative eta invariant η(W; E) for a flat bundle E over W gives a well-defined element in ℝ/ℤ that vanishes if W bounds a spin manifold with extended flat bundle. This underlies the Cheeger–Gromov proof that the rho invariant detects non-trivial elements in the structure set S(M) in the surgery exact sequence. In dimension 3, the rho invariant of a flat SU(2) connection on a homology sphere is the Chern–Simons invariant modulo ℤ, and the APS theorem identifies it with the spectral flow of the Dirac operator on a bounding 4-manifold. The Dai–Freed theorem generalizes this: the partition function of an invertible topological field theory on a closed manifold M is the exponentiated eta invariant of a Dirac operator on a bounding manifold W, and anomaly cancellation is equivalent to the vanishing of the cobordism class of M in the appropriate twisted spin cobordism group. The Adams spectral sequence for MSpin computes the image of the alpha invariant α: ΩSpin* → KO*(pt), and the APS theorem shows that the kernel of α consists of spin manifolds that bound spin manifolds with positive scalar curvature, by the Gromov–Lawson–Stolz theorem. The Stolz conjecture (proved for simply connected spin manifolds of dimension ≥ 5) states that a simply connected spin manifold admits a metric of positive scalar curvature iff its alpha invariant vanishes; the APS rho invariant provides the obstruction in the non-simply-connected case via the Rosenberg index in KO*(C*(Γ)).

Cobordism and the Classification of Symmetry‑Protected Topological Phases: The Freed–Hopkins Theorem, the Tenfold Way, and the Cobordism Groups of the Altland–Zirnbauer Symmetry Classes

The Freed–Hopkins theorem (2016) classifies deformation classes of reflection-positive invertible topological quantum field theories in dimension d with symmetry group G by the Anderson dual of the G-cobordism spectrum: [MTG, Iℤ]−d−1 ≅ Hom(ΩdG, U(1)) ⊕ Ext(Ωd−1G, U(1)). For free fermion systems, the symmetry type is encoded by a supergroup Gf fitting into 1 → U(1) → Gf → Gb → 1, where Gb is the bosonic symmetry group. The tenfold way of Altland and Zirnbauer corresponds to the ten real and complex Clifford algebra extensions classified by the Bott periodicity of KO-theory and KU-theory. The cobordism groups ΩdGf for the ten symmetry classes are computed via the Adams spectral sequence for the Thom spectra MGf. For example, class A (no symmetry, complex fermions) gives Gf = U(1) and ΩdA = KU−d(pt) = ℤ for d even, 0 for d odd — the integer quantum Hall effect. Class AIII (chiral symmetry) gives ΩdAIII = KU−d−1(pt). The real classes (AI, BDI, D, DIII, AII, CII, C, CI) correspond to KO-theory: ΩdAI = KO−d(pt), ΩdBDI = KO−d−1(pt), ΩdD = KO−d−2(pt), ΩdDIII = KO−d−3(pt), ΩdAII = KO−d−4(pt), ΩdCII = KO−d−5(pt), ΩdC = KO−d−6(pt), ΩdCI = KO−d−7(pt). The 8-fold Bott periodicity of KO-theory reproduces the 8-fold periodicity of the tenfold way. For d = 2, class A gives ℤ (Chern number), class D gives ℤ/2 (Majorana zero modes), class AII gives ℤ/2 (quantum spin Hall effect). The crystalline symmetries (space groups) are incorporated by taking Gb to be a space group; the cobordism groups ΩdGf are then computed via the equivariant Adams spectral sequence or the cellular decomposition of the Brillouin zone. The Kapustin–Thorngren–Turzillo–Wang approach uses the cobordism hypothesis for G-structured manifolds to classify SPT phases as maps MG → Iℤ in the stable homotopy category. The group cohomology classification Hd+1(Gb, U(1)) for bosonic SPT phases is recovered as the E2-page of the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence for Ω*Gb ⊗ ℚ/ℤ. For fermionic systems, the Gu–Wen supercohomology is the E2-page of the spectral sequence for Ω*Gf. The Kitaev periodic table of topological insulators and superconductors is precisely the computation of π−d(MGf) for the ten classes.

Worked Computation: Cobordism Group for Class D in Dimension 2
Compute the group of 2D topological superconductors in symmetry class D (particle-hole symmetry, no time-reversal, no spin rotation) using the Freed–Hopkins theorem.
Class D corresponds to the real Clifford algebra extension Cl2 → Cl1, giving Gf = Pin−(2) with Gb = O(2). The Freed–Hopkins theorem identifies the classification with Ω2Pin− = π2(MPin−). The Thom spectrum MPin− is related to KO-theory by the Anderson–Brown–Peterson splitting: MPin− ≃ KO ∧ Σ−2Mℤ/2 ∨ ... . The low-dimensional Pin− cobordism groups are known: Ω0Pin− = ℤ/2, Ω1Pin− = ℤ/2, Ω2Pin− = ℤ/8, Ω3Pin− = 0. The generator of Ω2Pin− = ℤ/8 is the Arf invariant of a Pin− surface, which takes values in ℤ/8. However, the invertible TQFT classification uses the Anderson dual: [MPin−, Iℤ]−3 = Hom(Ω2Pin−, U(1)) = Hom(ℤ/8, U(1)) = ℤ/8. But the physical classification of gapped free-fermion phases in class D in 2D is ℤ/2, generated by the p+ip superconductor with a single Majorana zero mode at a vortex. The discrepancy arises because the Freed–Hopkins theorem classifies all invertible TQFTs, including those not realizable by free fermions. The free-fermion classification is the image of the Anderson dual of the map MSpin → MPin− induced by the forgetful map. The map Ω2Spin = ℤ/2 → Ω2Pin− = ℤ/8 is injective, and its image is the ℤ/2 subgroup generated by the Arf invariant 4 mod 8. Thus the free-fermion classification is ℤ/2, matching the Kitaev table. The full ℤ/8 includes interacting phases (e.g., the E8 state) that require interactions beyond free fermions.
Exam Strategy: Mapping Symmetry Classes to Cobordism Groups
Memorize the correspondence: Class A → KU, Class AIII → KU[−1], Real classes AI, BDI, D, DIII, AII, CII, C, CI → KO[0], KO[−1], KO[−2], KO[−3], KO[−4], KO[−5], KO[−6], KO[−7] respectively. The physical dimension d corresponds to cobordism group Ωd = π−d(MGf). Use the 8-fold Bott periodicity of KO: KO0=ℤ, KO1=ℤ/2, KO2=ℤ/2, KO3=0, KO4=ℤ, KO5=0, KO6=0, KO7=0. For class D in d=2: KO−2−2=KO−4=KO4=ℤ, but the free-fermion subgroup is ℤ/2. Always distinguish the full cobordism group (all invertible TQFTs) from the free-fermion subgroup (image from MSpin).

Cobordism and the Kreck–Stolz Invariants for 7‑Manifolds: The Eells–Kuiper Invariant, the s-Invariant, and Positive Scalar Curvature Obstructions

The Kreck–Stolz invariants provide a complete set of diffeomorphism invariants for 7-manifolds that are 2-connected and admit a spin structure, refining the Eells–Kuiper invariant μ ∈ ℚ/ℤ. For a closed spin 7-manifold M with H4(M; ℤ) = 0, the Eells–Kuiper invariant is defined by choosing a spin 8-manifold W with ∂W = M and setting μ(M) = (∫W Â(TW) − η(DM)/2) mod ℤ, where η is the APS eta invariant of the Dirac operator. This is independent of the choice of W because the index of the Dirac operator on a closed spin 8-manifold is an integer. The invariant μ takes values in (1/28)ℤ/ℤ ≅ ℤ/28 for spin 7-manifolds with H4=0. Kreck and Stolz (1988) showed that for 2-connected spin 7-manifolds, the pair (μ, p12[M]) classifies them up to oriented diffeomorphism, where p12[M] ∈ ℤ is the Pontryagin number of a bounding 8-manifold.
