The s-invariant s(M) ∈ ℚ/ℤ is a refinement for manifolds with H4 ≠ 0, defined using the Kreck–Stolz surgery exact sequence and the Kervaire–Milnor classification of exotic spheres. For a spin 7-manifold M with H4(M; ℤ) torsion-free, s(M) = μ(M) − (1/24)∫M p1(TM) ∪ ω mod ℤ, where ω is a characteristic class in H4(M; ℤ) determined by the spin structure. The positive scalar curvature (psc) obstruction for spin 7-manifolds is captured by the alpha invariant α(M) ∈ KO7(pt) = 0, which vanishes identically in dimension 7. However, the Stolz theorem states that a simply connected spin manifold of dimension ≥ 5 admits psc iff α(M) = 0. For non-simply-connected 7-manifolds, the Rosenberg index in KO7(C*(π1)) provides the obstruction. The Kreck–Stolz invariants detect exotic smooth structures on 7-manifolds that are homeomorphic but not diffeomorphic to the standard sphere or to S3 × S4. The Milnor spheres Σ7k (the 28 oriented diffeomorphism classes of homotopy 7-spheres) have μ(Σ7k) = k/28 mod ℤ. The Brieskorn spheres Σ(a, b, c, d) provide explicit algebraic links realizing all 28 classes. The Kreck–Stolz classification extends to 7-manifolds with G2 structure: a G2 manifold has a torsion-free G2 structure iff it admits a metric of holonomy G2, which implies Ricci-flat and hence psc is impossible unless the manifold is a quotient of S7. The Crowley–Nordström invariant ν ∈ ℤ/24 for G2 7-manifolds refines μ and detects the φ-invariant of the associative 3-form. The cobordism group Ω7Spin = 0, so every spin 7-manifold bounds a spin 8-manifold, but the relative invariants for manifolds with boundary are captured by the rho invariant of the Dirac operator on the boundary with flat coefficients, which takes values in the Higson–Roe analytic structure group S*(Bπ1).

Cobordism and the Cobordism Ring of Manifolds with G₂ and Spin(7) Structures: Calibrated Geometry, the Crowley–Nordström Classification, and the φ-Invariant

The cobordism rings Ω*G₂ and Ω*Spin(7) classify manifolds equipped with a G2 structure (a reduction of the structure group to the exceptional Lie group G2 ⊂ SO(7)) or a Spin(7) structure (a reduction to Spin(7) ⊂ SO(8)). A G2 structure is equivalent to a nowhere-vanishing 3-form φ ∈ Ω3(M) that is pointwise equivalent to the standard associative 3-form on ℝ7; it is torsion-free (i.e., ∇φ = 0 for the Levi-Civita connection) iff the holonomy is contained in G2, which implies the metric is Ricci-flat. A Spin(7) structure is equivalent to a nowhere-vanishing 4-form Φ ∈ Ω4(M) pointwise equivalent to the Cayley form on ℝ8; torsion-free implies holonomy ⊂ Spin(7) and Ricci-flat. The cobordism groups Ω*G₂ and Ω*Spin(7) are the homotopy groups of the Thom spectra MG2 and MSpin(7). The rational cobordism rings are polynomial: Ω*G₂ ⊗ ℚ = ℚ[x8, x12, x16, …] and Ω*Spin(7) ⊗ ℚ = ℚ[y8, y12, y16, …], where the generators correspond to Pontryagin numbers of the tangent bundle that survive the G2 or Spin(7) condition. The torsion is more subtle. Crowley and Nordström (2015) computed the low-dimensional G2 cobordism groups using the Adams spectral sequence for MG2 and the fiber sequence MG2 → MSpin → Σ4Hℤ induced by the map BG2 → BSpin(7) → K(ℤ, 4) classifying the first Pontryagin class p1/2. They found Ω0G₂ = ℤ, Ω1G₂ = Ω2G₂ = Ω3G₂ = 0, Ω4G₂ = ℤ/2 (generated by the K3 surface with a G2 structure induced by a hyperkähler structure), Ω5G₂ = Ω6G₂ = 0, Ω7G₂ = ℤ/24 (the Crowley–Nordström invariant ν ∈ ℤ/24), Ω8G₂ = ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2. The invariant ν for a closed G2 7-manifold is defined by ν(M) = (1/24)∫W p2(TW) mod ℤ, where W is a spin 8-manifold with ∂W = M and the G2 structure extends over W as a Spin(7) structure on the normal bundle. This is the φ-invariant of the associative 3-form φ. For Spin(7) 8-manifolds, the Cayley invariant κ ∈ ℤ/24 is defined similarly. The moduli space of torsion-free G2 structures on a fixed 7-manifold is studied via the Kuranishi model; the Joyce construction produces compact G2 manifolds by resolving orbifolds T7/Γ. The cobordism hypothesis for G2 structures implies that the fully extended TQFT with G2 structure is classified by the fully dualizable object in the symmetric monoidal (∞,7)-category of bordisms. The associative submanifolds (3-dimensional calibrated by φ) and coassociative submanifolds (4-dimensional calibrated by *φ) define homology classes in H3(M) and H4(M) that are cobordism invariants. The Donaldson–Segal program proposes a Floer theory for G2 manifolds using associative submanifolds, analogous to Seiberg–Witten theory for 4-manifolds. The Spin(7) cobordism ring Ω*Spin(7) is related to Ω*G₂ by the fiber sequence MG2 → MSpin(7) → Σ8Hℤ induced by the map BSpin(7) → K(ℤ, 8) classifying p2/6. The Witten genus of a G2 manifold vanishes because the G2 condition implies the Dirac operator has a parallel spinor, so the index is zero. The elliptic genus of a Spin(7) manifold is a modular form of weight 4, and the string orientation of tmf factors through MSpin(7).

Cobordism and the Surgery Exact Sequence for Topological Manifolds: The Kirby–Siebenmann Invariant, the Quinn Obstruction, and the Structure Set in the Topological Category

The surgery exact sequence in the topological category, developed by Kirby–Siebenmann, Quinn, and Ranicki, classifies topological manifolds up to homeomorphism in dimensions ≥ 5. For a topological manifold M of dimension n ≥ 5, the structure set STOP(M) consists of equivalence classes of homotopy equivalences f: N → M from topological manifolds N, where two such equivalences are equivalent if they are concordant. The surgery exact sequence is

⋯ → Ln+1(ℤ[π1(M)]) → STOP(M) → [M, G/TOP] → Ln(ℤ[π1(M)]) → ⋯

where G/TOP is the smoothing theory space (homotopy fiber of BO → BTOP), and L*(ℤ[π]) are the Wall surgery obstruction groups (quadratic L-groups). The map STOP(M) → [M, G/TOP] is the normal invariant, which assigns to a homotopy equivalence its stable normal bundle data. The Kirby–Siebenmann invariant ks(M) ∈ H4(M; ℤ/2) is the primary obstruction to the existence of a PL structure on a topological manifold M of dimension ≥ 5. It is the image of the fundamental class [M] under the map Hn(M; ℤ/2) → H4(M; ℤ/2) induced by the Kirby–Siebenmann class in H4(BTOP; ℤ/2). A topological manifold admits a PL structure iff ks(M) = 0. The Quinn obstruction q(M) ∈ H4(M; ℤ/2) is the obstruction to the existence of a topological handlebody decomposition; it coincides with ks(M) for manifolds but differs for Poincaré complexes. The surgery obstruction map [M, G/TOP] → Ln(ℤ[π1]) is given by the Ranicki assembly map in algebraic L-theory. For simply connected M, Ln(ℤ) = ℤ for n ≡ 0 mod 4 (signature), ℤ/2 for n ≡ 2 mod 4 (Arf invariant), 0 otherwise. The Novikov conjecture (rational injectivity of the assembly map) implies that the surgery obstruction map is rationally injective for π1(M). The Borel conjecture (aspherical manifolds are topologically rigid) is equivalent to STOP(M) = * for aspherical M. The Farrell–Jones conjecture in L-theory implies the Borel conjecture for torsion-free groups. The cobordism interpretation: the normal invariant [M, G/TOP] is isomorphic to the topological cobordism group ΩnTOP(M) of degree-1 normal maps to M. The Thom spectrum MTOP is the Thom spectrum of the universal topological bundle over BTOP; the Kirby–Siebenmann class is the obstruction to lifting the map MTOP → Hℤ/2 to MPL. The Ranicki total surgery obstruction s(M) ∈ Hn(M; L•(ℤ)) is a homology class that vanishes iff M is homotopy equivalent to a topological manifold. The topological transversality theorem (Quinn) states that topological manifolds of dimension ≥ 5 satisfy transversality for maps to G/TOP, which is the geometric foundation of the surgery exact sequence. The cobordism of topological manifolds with G-structure is classified by the homotopy groups of MTOP ∧ BG+. The Kirby–Siebenmann invariant of a 4-manifold is defined via the Freedman–Quinn classification: a topological 4-manifold admits a PL structure iff its Kirby–Siebenmann invariant in H4(M; ℤ/2) = ℤ/2 vanishes; the E8 manifold has ks = 1 and is not smoothable. The s-cobordism theorem in the topological category (Quinn) states that an h-cobordism between topological manifolds of dimension ≥ 5 is a product iff its Whitehead torsion in Wh(π1) vanishes.

  • Confusing the Eells–Kuiper invariant μ (a rational number mod ℤ) with the Kreck–Stolz s-invariant (a refinement for manifolds with non-zero H4); μ alone classifies only 2-connected spin 7-manifolds.
  • Assuming the Freed–Hopkins classification of SPT phases gives the free-fermion classification directly; the physical free-fermion phases are the image of the map from MSpin cobordism, not the full Anderson dual group.
  • Treating the tenfold way periodicities (2 for complex, 8 for real) as independent of Bott periodicity in K-theory; they are precisely the Bott periodicities of KU and KO.
  • Believing the Kirby–Siebenmann invariant ks ∈ H4(M; ℤ/2) is the only obstruction to smoothing a topological manifold; in dimension 4, the Seiberg–Witten invariants and Donaldson invariants provide further obstructions, and there exist non-smoothable manifolds with ks = 0 (e.g., certain exotic ℝ4s).
  • Confusing the topological surgery exact sequence (with G/TOP and L-groups) with the smooth surgery exact sequence (with G/O and the smooth structure set); the smoothing theory space G/TOP is the homotopy fiber of BO → BTOP, and its homotopy groups are the Kervaire–Milnor groups of exotic spheres.
  • Assuming the Crowley–Nordström invariant ν ∈ ℤ/24 for G2 7-manifolds is the same as the Eells–Kuiper invariant μ ∈ ℤ/28; they live in different groups and ν is defined only for G2 structures, while μ is defined for all spin 7-manifolds with H4=0.
  • Overlooking that the APS eta invariant η(DM) is a real number, not an integer, and the rho invariant ρ = η − η(Sn) is the cobordism invariant; the APS index theorem requires the boundary correction η/2 to make the index integer-valued.

Cobordism of Foliated Manifolds and Haefliger's Classifying Space BΓq

The cobordism theory of foliated manifolds, initiated by Haefliger in the 1960s, provides a geometric framework for studying foliations up to concordance. A foliated manifold of codimension q is a manifold M equipped with a decomposition into q-codimensional leaves; two such structures are concordant if they are restrictions of a foliation on M × [0,1] transverse to the boundary. The set of concordance classes forms the foliated cobordism group Ωfoln,q. Haefliger constructed a classifying space BΓq for codimension-q foliations: the Haefliger groupoid Γq has objects ℝq and morphisms germs of local diffeomorphisms, and BΓq is the geometric realization of its nerve. The Pontryagin–Thom construction extends to this setting: a foliated manifold (M, ℱ) determines a map M → BΓq by sending a point to the germ of its leaf, and this induces an isomorphism Ωfoln,q ≅ πn(MΓq), where MΓq is the Thom spectrum of the universal normal bundle over BΓq. Thurston's stability theorem implies that for q = 1, BΓ1 ≃ BSO(2) × BΓ10, and the foliated cobordism groups are related to the ordinary cobordism groups of circle bundles. For higher q, the homotopy type of BΓq is deeply mysterious; Haefliger showed BΓq is q-connected, and Thurston proved that the inclusion BΓqδ → BΓq (discrete topology on leaves) is a homology isomorphism in degrees ≤ q/2. The Godbillon–Vey invariant, a secondary characteristic class in H3(BΓq; ℝ), detects non-trivial elements in foliated cobordism. In codimension 2, Thurston's construction of foliations with non-zero Godbillon–Vey invariant shows Ωfoln,2 ⊗ ℚ is non-zero for infinitely many n. The relationship with ordinary cobordism is given by the forgetful map Ωfoln,q → Ωn (unoriented) or ΩnSO (oriented), whose kernel consists of foliations that are concordant to a product foliation. A key computational tool is the Haefliger spectral sequence, a variant of the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence for the Thom spectrum MΓq, whose E2-page involves the homology of BΓq with coefficients in the unoriented cobordism ring. The differentials encode obstructions to realizing a homology class by a foliated manifold. For example, the first non-trivial differential dq+1 is related to the Reeb stability obstruction. The theory extends to foliations with transverse structures (e.g., transverse volume forms, transverse complex structures), leading to classifying spaces BΓq(G) for a Lie group G acting on ℝq, and corresponding cobordism groups that interpolate between foliated cobordism and G-structure cobordism.

Equivariant Cobordism, the tom Dieck Splitting, and the Segal Conjecture for MUG

Equivariant cobordism theory, developed by tom Dieck, Wasserman, and others in the 1970s, studies compact Lie group actions on manifolds up to equivariant cobordism. For a compact Lie group G, the equivariant cobordism ring MU*G(pt) is the coefficient ring of the equivariant complex cobordism theory represented by the Thom spectrum MUG = (MU ∧ EG+)G in the genuine G-equivariant stable homotopy category. The tom Dieck splitting theorem provides a fundamental decomposition: for any G-spectrum X, the geometric fixed-point spectrum ΦHX for a subgroup H ≤ G satisfies π*G(X) ≅ ⊕(H) π*(ΦHX ∧ BWGH+), where the sum runs over conjugacy classes of subgroups and WGH = NGH/H is the Weyl group. Applied to MUG, this yields MU*G(pt) ≅ ⊕(H) MU*(BWGH) ⊗ ℤ[χ(H)], where χ(H) are Euler classes of representations. The splitting reveals that equivariant cobordism is built from ordinary cobordism of classifying spaces of Weyl groups, twisted by representation-theoretic data. The Segal conjecture for MUG (proved by Greenlees–May and others) states that the completion of MU*G(pt) at the augmentation ideal IG ⊂ MU0G(pt) is isomorphic to the MU-cohomology of the classifying space BG: MU*G(pt)IG∧ ≅ MU*(BG). This is a vast generalization of the classical Segal conjecture for the sphere spectrum (Burnside ring completion). The proof uses the tom Dieck splitting and the fact that MU is a complex-oriented theory with formal group law FMU. The augmentation ideal IG is generated by Euler classes of non-trivial representations, and the completion corresponds to inverting these Euler classes in a controlled way. The Segal conjecture implies that the IG-adic filtration on MU*G(pt) has associated graded pieces related to the representation ring R(G). For G = S1, MU*S1(pt) ≅ MU*[[x]] where x is the Euler class of the standard representation, and the Segal conjecture reduces to the statement that MU*(BS1) ≅ MU*[[x]] (which holds because BS1 = ℂP∞). For finite G, the completion is more subtle: the map MU*G(pt) → MU*(BG) factors through the fixed-point map, and the kernel consists of elements that vanish on all proper subgroups. The Greenlees spectral sequence computes the completed ring from the fixed-point data. A key application is the computation of the equivariant cobordism rings of classifying spaces BG, which control the equivariant Pontryagin–Thom construction for manifolds with free G-actions. The theory also connects to the equivariant formal group law over MU*G(pt), which encodes the behavior of equivariant Chern classes under tensor products of representations.

Worked Example: tom Dieck Splitting for G = ℤ/2
Compute the equivariant complex cobordism ring MU*ℤ/2(pt) using the tom Dieck splitting, and identify the augmentation ideal Iℤ/2 and the completion map to MU*(Bℤ/2).
For G = ℤ/2, the conjugacy classes of subgroups are (1) and (ℤ/2). The Weyl groups are WG(1) = ℤ/2 and WG(ℤ/2) = 1. The tom Dieck splitting gives: MU*ℤ/2(pt) ≅ MU*(Bℤ/2) ⊕ MU*(pt) ⊗ ℤ[χ(ℤ/2)]. Here χ(ℤ/2) is the Euler class of the sign representation σ, which generates the augmentation ideal Iℤ/2 = (χ(σ)) ⊂ MU0ℤ/2(pt). The first summand corresponds to the trivial subgroup (geometric fixed points Φ1MUℤ/2 ≃ MU ∧ Bℤ/2+), the second to the whole group (Φℤ/2MUℤ/2 ≃ MU). The completion at Iℤ/2 kills the second summand (since χ(σ) is invertible in the completion of the polynomial ring ℤ[χ(σ)]), yielding MU*ℤ/2(pt)Iℤ/2∧ ≅ MU*(Bℤ/2)Iℤ/2∧. But MU*(Bℤ/2) ≅ MU*[[x]]/(2x) where x is the first Chern class of the canonical line bundle over Bℤ/2 = ℝP∞, and the completion at (χ(σ)) coincides with the (x)-adic completion because χ(σ) maps to x under the fixed-point map. Thus the Segal conjecture holds: the completion is MU*[[x]]/(2x) ≅ MU*(Bℤ/2).
Exam Strategy: tom Dieck Splitting
When asked to compute equivariant cobordism rings, always start by listing conjugacy classes of subgroups and their Weyl groups. The splitting is a direct sum over these classes. Remember that the augmentation ideal is generated by Euler classes of non-trivial irreducible representations. For the Segal conjecture, the completion map is induced by the fixed-point map MU*G(pt) → MU*(BG); its kernel is the ideal of elements that restrict to zero on all proper subgroups. Practice the G = S1 and G = ℤ/p cases until the pattern is automatic.

Iterated Cobordism Categories and Laures' Theory of Manifolds with Corners

Baas–Sullivan cobordism (already introduced) allows manifolds with singularities modeled on a fixed singular manifold Σ. Laures' theory of iterated cobordism (2000) generalizes this by considering manifolds with corners of arbitrary codimension, where each stratum is itself a manifold with corners, leading to an n-fold cobordism category Cobn. An object of Cobn is a closed (n−1)-manifold with corners; a 1-morphism is an n-manifold with corners whose boundary is decomposed into incoming and outgoing (n−1)-manifolds with corners; higher morphisms are cobordisms between cobordisms. The classifying space BCobn is an n-fold loop space, and its group completion ΩnBCobn is the n-fold cobordism spectrum Mn. Laures proved that Mn is an En-ring spectrum, and there is a natural map of En-ring spectra Mn → Mn+1 inducing an isomorphism on homotopy groups in a range that grows with n. The colimit M∞ = colimn Mn is an E∞-ring spectrum equivalent to the sphere spectrum S (by the cobordism hypothesis). The homotopy groups π*Mn are the iterated cobordism groups Ωn*, which fit into a spectral sequence converging to the stable homotopy groups of spheres. The E1-page of this spectral sequence is given by the ordinary cobordism groups of the strata: E1p,q = Ωq(BΣp), where Σp is the universal p-stratum. The differentials encode the gluing data for corners. For n = 1, M1 = MO (unoriented) or MSO (oriented). For n = 2, M2 is the Thom spectrum of the universal 2-fold vector bundle over B(O × O) or B(SO × SO), and Ω2* classifies manifolds with corners up to 2-fold cobordism. Laures computed the low-dimensional groups: Ω20 = ℤ, Ω21 = ℤ/2, Ω22 = ℤ/2 ⊕ ℤ/2, Ω23 = 0, Ω24 = ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2 ⊕ ℤ/2. The extra ℤ/2 summands in dimension 2 and 4 correspond to the Arf invariant of the corner and the Kervaire invariant of the corner, respectively. The theory connects to factorization homology: the factorization homology of a manifold M with coefficients in the En-algebra Mn computes the iterated cobordism groups of M. The Laures spectral sequence for iterated cobordism is a higher categorical analogue of the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence, with E2-page given by the homology of M with coefficients in the iterated cobordism groups of a point. This spectral sequence collapses for n = 1 (giving the classical AHSS) but has non-trivial differentials for n ≥ 2, reflecting the higher gluing constraints. The theory also provides a geometric model for the Goodwillie calculus of the identity functor on spaces: the n-th layer of the Goodwillie tower of the identity is related to the n-fold cobordism category.

Cobordism of Manifolds with Group Actions: Fixed Point Formulae and the Equivariant Index Theorem

The interplay between cobordism and group actions is governed by the equivariant Pontryagin–Thom construction, which identifies the equivariant cobordism group ΩGn(X) of a G-space X with the equivariant stable homotopy group πnG(X−V ∧ MV) for a G-representation V. When G acts on a manifold M, the fixed-point set MH for a subgroup H ≤ G is a submanifold whose normal bundle carries a representation of H. The Atiyah–Bott fixed-point formula in equivariant cobordism expresses the equivariant cobordism class [M] ∈ ΩGn(pt) in terms of the fixed-point data: [M] = ∑(H) trH([MH]), where the sum is over conjugacy classes of subgroups, and trH is the transfer map ΩH*(MH) → ΩG*(pt) induced by the inclusion H → G. For G = S1, this reduces to the Conner–Floyd exact sequence relating ΩS1*(pt) to Ω*(BS1) and Ω*(pt). The equivariant index theorem of Atiyah–Singer extends to cobordism: for a G-equivariant elliptic operator D on M, the equivariant index indG(D) ∈ R(G) (the representation ring) is determined by the equivariant cobordism class of the symbol of D. In particular, the equivariant signature operator gives a homomorphism ΩG4k(pt) → R(G) ⊗ ℚ, whose kernel is the torsion subgroup. The equivariant Hirzebruch signature theorem expresses the signature of MH in terms of the equivariant L-class of the normal bundle. For finite G, the Segal conjecture for cobordism (discussed above) implies that the completion of ΩG*(pt) at the augmentation ideal is Ω*(BG). The tom Dieck splitting gives a decomposition ΩG*(pt) ≅ ⊕(H) Ω*(BWGH) ⊗ ℤ[χ(H)], where χ(H) are Euler classes of representations of H. This splitting is compatible with the fixed-point formula: the summand corresponding to H is the image of the transfer from ΩH*(pt). The equivariant cobordism ring of a point for G = S1 is ΩS1*(pt) ≅ Ω*[[x]] where x is the Euler class of the standard representation; for G = ℤ/p, it is Ω*[[x]]/(px) where x is the Euler class of a 1-dimensional representation. The equivariant Landweber exact functor theorem (Greenlees) constructs equivariant cohomology theories from equivariant formal group laws over ΩG*(pt). A key application is the computation of the equivariant cobordism groups of classifying spaces BG, which classify G-manifolds up to equivariant cobordism with free G-action. The equivariant Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for MUG has E2-page ExtMU*MUG(MU*G, MU*G) and converges to the equivariant stable homotopy groups of spheres.

The Cobordism Ring of Almost Contact Manifolds and the Boothby–Wang Fibration

An almost contact structure on a (2n+1)-manifold M is a reduction of the structure group of TM to U(n) × 1, equivalently a 1-form η, a vector field ξ with η(ξ) = 1, and a (1,1)-tensor φ with φ2 = −1 + η ⊗ ξ. The Boothby–Wang theorem states that a compact manifold admits a regular contact structure (where the Reeb vector field ξ generates a free S1-action) iff it is a principal S1-bundle over a symplectic manifold (B, ω) with Euler class [ω] ∈ H2(B; ℤ). The cobordism ring of almost contact manifolds ΩAC* was studied by Stong, Hattori, and others. The forgetful map ΩAC2n+1 → Ω2n+1U (almost complex cobordism) is surjective in odd dimensions, and its kernel is generated by the boundaries of almost complex manifolds with almost contact boundary. The Hattori–Stong theorem for almost contact manifolds states that ΩAC* ⊗ ℚ is a polynomial algebra on generators in dimensions 3, 5, 7, …, corresponding to the Boothby–Wang fibrations over ℂPk. The contact cobordism groups Ωcont* (where the almost contact structure is required to be a genuine contact structure) are more subtle. Geiges proved that in dimension 3, every almost contact manifold is contact cobordant to a contact manifold, so Ωcont3 = ΩAC3 = ℤ/2 (generated by the 3-sphere with the standard contact structure). In higher dimensions, the inclusion Ωcont* → ΩAC* is not surjective; the obstruction is measured by the Eliashberg–Gromov h-principle for contact structures. The Boothby–Wang fibration induces a map Ωcont2n+1 → Ω2nSp (symplectic cobordism), which is a ring homomorphism after tensoring with ℚ. The contact invariant in Heegaard Floer homology gives a homomorphism Ωcont3 → ℤ/2 that detects the standard contact structure on S3. The cobordism ring of Sasakian manifolds (contact manifolds with a compatible metric and a transverse Kähler structure) maps to the cobordism ring of Kähler manifolds via the Boothby–Wang construction. The transverse index theorem for the basic Dolbeault operator on a Sasakian manifold gives a genus ΩSas* → ℤ that factors through the Boothby–Wang map. The Landweber exact functor theorem applied to the formal group law of complex cobordism yields a contact cobordism theory MUcont whose coefficient ring is Ωcont* ⊗ MU*. The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for contact cobordism has E2-page ExtMU*MU(MU*, Ωcont* ⊗ MU*) and converges to the contact stable homotopy groups.

  • Confusing the Haefliger classifying space BΓq with the classifying space for G-structures BG; BΓq classifies foliations (germs of diffeomorphisms), not vector bundles.
  • Assuming the tom Dieck splitting holds for all equivariant cohomology theories; it requires the theory to be represented by a genuine G-spectrum (e.g., MUG), not just a naive G-spectrum.
  • Overlooking that the Segal conjecture for MUG involves completion at the augmentation ideal of MU0G(pt), not the Burnside ring.
  • Treating iterated cobordism Mn as equivalent to the n-fold loop space of MO; Mn is an En-ring spectrum whose group completion is the sphere spectrum, not MO.
  • Confusing the equivariant Conner–Floyd exact sequence (for S1-actions) with the non-equivariant Conner–Floyd isomorphism MU*(X) ≅ K*(X) ⊗K* MU*.
  • Assuming every almost contact manifold is contact cobordant to a contact manifold in all dimensions; the h-principle fails in dimensions ≥ 5.
  • Neglecting that the Boothby–Wang map Ωcont2n+1 → Ω2nSp is only a ring homomorphism after rationalization; torsion phenomena are lost.

Cobordism and the Kreck–Stolz Invariants for Seven‑Dimensional Manifolds

The Kreck–Stolz invariants, introduced by Matthias Kreck and Stephan Stolz in 1993, provide a complete set of diffeomorphism invariants for simply connected spin 7‑manifolds. They refine the information contained in the spin cobordism group Ω⁽ˢᵖⁱⁿ⁾₇, which is trivial, by extracting secondary invariants from a chosen spin 8‑manifold that bounds the given 7‑manifold. The two primary invariants are denoted s(M) ∈ ℚ/ℤ and t(M) ∈ ℤ/2, and they are defined using the eta invariants of the signature operator and the Dirac operator on the bounding 8‑manifold.

For a closed spin 7‑manifold M, choose a compact spin 8‑manifold W with ∂W = M. The Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index theorem expresses the index of the Dirac operator on W as ∫_W Â(W) − (η_D(M) + h)/2, where η_D(M) is the eta invariant of the Dirac operator on M and h is the dimension of its kernel. The invariant s(M) is defined as the reduction modulo ℤ of the quantity (η_D(M) + η_sign(M))/2, while t(M) is the mod 2 reduction of the index of the Dirac operator on a spin 8‑manifold with boundary M ∪ (−M). These definitions are independent of the choice of W because any two such fillings differ by a closed spin 8‑manifold, whose index is an integer.

The invariants classify simply connected spin 7‑manifolds up to oriented diffeomorphism: two such manifolds M₁ and M₂ are diffeomorphic if and only if (s(M₁), t(M₁)) = (s(M₂), t(M₂)). In particular, the exotic 7‑spheres discovered by Milnor are distinguished by the Eells–Kuiper invariant μ ∈ ℚ/ℤ, which coincides with s(M) for the standard spin structure. The Kreck–Stolz invariants also detect the non‑trivial elements in the group of homotopy 7‑spheres Θ₇ ≅ ℤ/28, providing a bridge between cobordism theory and the classification of exotic spheres.

Beyond simply connected manifolds, Kreck and Stolz extended their invariants to 7‑manifolds with fundamental group ℤ/2, using the equivariant eta invariant. The resulting invariants take values in ℚ/ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2 ⊕ ℤ/2 and classify such manifolds up to spin diffeomorphism. This work demonstrates how secondary index invariants can capture subtle geometric information that primary cobordism groups miss, and it has been instrumental in the study of positive scalar curvature metrics on 7‑manifolds.

In the context of the spin cobordism ring Ω⁽ˢᵖⁱⁿ⁾_*, the Kreck–Stolz invariants define a homomorphism Ω⁽ˢᵖⁱⁿ⁾₇ → ℚ/ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2 which is an isomorphism onto its image. Since Ω⁽ˢᵖⁱⁿ⁾₇ = 0, this homomorphism is trivial on the cobordism group but non‑trivial on the set of diffeomorphism classes, illustrating the distinction between cobordism equivalence and diffeomorphism classification. The invariants have also been used to construct manifolds with positive Ricci curvature that are not diffeomorphic to standard spheres, thereby enriching the geography of 7‑manifolds.

Cobordism and the Index Theory of Dirac Operators on Manifolds with Boundary

The Atiyah–Patodi–Singer (APS) index theorem, proved in 1975, extends the classical Atiyah–Singer index theorem to manifolds with boundary by introducing the eta invariant of the boundary operator. For a compact spin manifold W with boundary M = ∂W, the theorem states that the index of the Dirac operator D_W with APS boundary conditions equals ∫_W Â(W) − (η_D(M) + h)/2, where Â(W) is the Â‑genus form, η_D(M) is the eta invariant of the Dirac operator on M, and h = dim ker D_M. This formula provides a powerful tool for defining cobordism invariants that are sensitive to the geometry of the boundary.

The eta invariant η_D(M) is a spectral invariant defined by the analytic continuation of the series Σλ≠0 sign(λ) |λ|-s evaluated at s = 0, where λ runs over the eigenvalues of D_M. It measures the asymmetry of the spectrum and is a real number modulo ℤ. When M bounds a spin manifold W, the combination η_D(M) + 2∫_W Â(W) is an integer, so the reduced eta invariant ξ(M) = (η_D(M) mod ℤ) defines a homomorphism from the spin cobordism group Ω⁽ˢᵖⁱⁿ⁾4k−1 to ℝ/ℤ. This homomorphism is precisely the Adams e‑invariant in spin cobordism and detects the image of the J‑homomorphism in the stable homotopy groups of spheres.

In the setting of manifolds with boundary, the APS theorem yields the rho invariant ρ(M) = η_D(M) − η_D(M₀) for a reference manifold M₀, which is independent of the choice of bounding W and defines an invariant of the spin cobordism class of M. The rho invariant plays a central role in the surgery exact sequence for the classification of high‑dimensional manifolds: it provides the map from the structure set S(M) to the L‑group Ln+1(ℤ[π₁M]) via the surgery obstruction. Moreover, the APS index theorem for the signature operator gives the Hirzebruch signature theorem with boundary, where the eta invariant of the signature operator on M appears as the boundary correction term.

The APS framework has been generalized to families of Dirac operators, leading to the Bismut–Cheeger eta form and the index theorem for families with boundary. These results underpin the construction of the determinant line bundle on the moduli space of Riemannian metrics and the proof of the local index theorem for families. In cobordism theory, the family version shows that the eta invariant defines a class in the odd‑dimensional K‑theory of the classifying space BSpin, linking the analytic index to the topological index in the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence.

Applications of the APS index theorem to cobordism include the computation of the spin cobordism groups Ω⁽ˢᵖⁱⁿ⁾_* in low dimensions, the proof that the Â‑genus vanishes on manifolds admitting a metric of positive scalar curvature (the Gromov–Lawson–Rosenberg conjecture), and the definition of the Stolz invariant for 7‑manifolds. The theorem also provides the analytic foundation for the topological modular forms (TMF) orientation of string manifolds, where the Witten genus is expressed as the index of a Dirac operator on loop space, and the boundary correction is given by the eta invariant of the Dirac operator on the boundary.

Applying the APS Index Theorem to Compute the Rho Invariant
Let M be a closed spin 5‑manifold that bounds a compact spin 6‑manifold W. Explain how the APS index theorem for the Dirac operator on W yields the rho invariant ρ(M) ∈ ℝ/ℤ, and why ρ(M) is a spin cobordism invariant.
1. Equip W with a product metric near ∂W = M and impose APS boundary conditions on the Dirac operator D_W. 2. The APS index theorem gives ind(D_W) = ∫_W Â(W) − (η_D(M) + h)/2. Since dim W = 6, the Â‑genus integral vanishes, so ind(D_W) = −(η_D(M) + h)/2. 3. The integer ind(D_W) plus h/2 implies η_D(M) ≡ −2 ind(D_W) (mod ℤ). 4. Choose a reference spin 5‑manifold M₀ that bounds a spin 6‑manifold W₀ with ind(DW₀) = 0 (e.g., M₀ = S⁵). Define ρ(M) = η_D(M) − η_D(M₀) (mod ℤ). 5. If M bounds another spin 6‑manifold W′, glue W and −W′ along M to obtain a closed spin 6‑manifold Z. The index theorem on Z shows η_D(M) computed from W and from W′ differ by an integer, so ρ(M) is independent of the choice of W. 6. If M is spin null‑cobordant, there exists W with ind(D_W) = 0, giving ρ(M) = 0. Hence ρ defines a homomorphism Ω⁽ˢᵖⁱⁿ⁾₅ → ℝ/ℤ, i.e., a spin cobordism invariant.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a cobordism invariant defined via an index theorem, first state the relevant index theorem (APS, Atiyah–Singer, or family version), identify the geometric operator (Dirac, signature, or Dolbeault), write the index formula including the boundary correction term, and then explain how the correction term yields a well‑defined invariant on the cobordism group. Practice deriving the rho invariant and the reduced eta invariant for dimensions 3, 5, and 7, as these appear frequently in problems about the image of J and the Adams e‑invariant.

Cobordism and the Classification of Manifolds with Positive Ricci Curvature

Positive Ricci curvature imposes strong topological restrictions that are reflected in cobordism theory. By Myers’ theorem, a complete manifold with Ric > 0 has finite fundamental group, and by the Bochner technique, its Betti numbers satisfy b_i = 0 for 0 < i < n. In the oriented cobordism ring Ω_*SO, a theorem of Gromov and Lawson (1980) shows that any closed manifold admitting a metric of positive scalar curvature represents zero in Ω_*SO after inverting 2. Since positive Ricci curvature implies positive scalar curvature, every positively Ricci‑curved manifold is rationally null‑cobordant.

In spin cobordism, the Â‑genus is an obstruction to positive scalar curvature. The Lichnerowicz formula D² = ∇*∇ + R/4 implies that the Dirac operator has no kernel when the scalar curvature is positive, so the index vanishes. Consequently, the Â‑genus of a spin manifold with positive scalar curvature is zero. For positive Ricci curvature, the same argument applies, and the Â‑genus vanishes. This yields a homomorphism Ω_*Spin → ℤ (the Â‑genus) that is zero on the submonoid of spin manifolds admitting positive Ricci curvature.

In low dimensions, the classification of simply connected manifolds with positive Ricci curvature is nearly complete. In dimension 4, the only simply connected examples are S⁴, ℂP², S² × S², and the non‑trivial S²‑bundle over S² (all of which are known to admit positive Ricci metrics by work of Hamilton, Chen, and others). Their oriented cobordism classes are generated by [ℂP²] and [S² × S²], and both are non‑zero in Ω4SO ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ. However, the spin cobordism class of ℂP² is zero because ℂP² is not spin, while S² × S² is spin and its Â‑genus is zero, consistent with the obstruction theory.

In dimension 6, the classification is more involved. Simply connected 6‑manifolds with positive Ricci curvature include S⁶, ℂP³, S² × ℂP², and certain flag manifolds. Their spin cobordism classes lie in Ω6Spin = 0, so they are all spin null‑cobordant. The oriented cobordism group Ω6SO ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ is generated by [ℂP³] and [S² × ℂP²]; both admit positive Ricci metrics, showing that non‑zero oriented cobordism classes can contain positively Ricci‑curved manifolds. The rational cobordism ring is generated by the Pontryagin numbers, and the vanishing of the Â‑genus is the only spin obstruction.

Recent work by Wilking and others uses the Grove–Ziller construction and cohomogeneity‑one actions to produce new examples of positively Ricci‑curved manifolds in higher dimensions. These examples often have non‑trivial rational Pontryagin classes, demonstrating that the rational cobordism class of a positively Ricci‑curved manifold can be arbitrarily complicated. Nevertheless, the spin cobordism class always vanishes in dimensions where the Â‑genus is the only obstruction, and the image of the positive Ricci curvature submonoid in Ω_*Spin is a proper submonoid whose structure is an active area of research.

Cobordism and the Motivic Cobordism Spectrum MGL

The motivic cobordism spectrum MGL, introduced by Vladimir Voevodsky in 1998, is the algebraic analogue of the complex cobordism spectrum MU in the stable motivic homotopy category SH(k) over a field k. Its construction follows the same pattern as MU: MGL is the Thom spectrum of the universal algebraic vector bundle over the infinite Grassmannian in the motivic setting. The bigraded coefficient ring MGL*,*(Spec k) is isomorphic to the Lazard ring L concentrated in bidegree (2*, *), mirroring the isomorphism MU_* ≅ L. This identification realizes the universal formal group law in motivic homotopy theory.

The Landweber exact functor theorem holds in the motivic context: any Landweber exact formal group law over a motivic ring spectrum gives rise to a motivic cohomology theory. In particular, the motivic versions of Brown–Peterson cohomology MGL → MBP and of Morava K‑theories MK(n) have been constructed. The motivic Adams–Novikov spectral sequence, with E₂‑page ExtMGL_* MGL(MGL_*, MGL_*), converges to the motivic stable homotopy groups of the sphere spectrum. This spectral sequence is a powerful tool for computing motivic stable stems and for comparing them with their topological counterparts via the Betti realization functor SH(ℂ) → SH.

MGL represents the algebraic cobordism theory Ω_*alg of Levine and Morel. For smooth schemes over a field of characteristic zero, there is a natural transformation Ω_*alg(X) → MGL2*,*(X) which is an isomorphism after inverting the exponential characteristic. This connection allows one to transfer geometric results from algebraic cobordism to motivic stable homotopy theory. For example, the motivic Steenrod algebra acts on MGL cohomology, and the motivic analogue of the Conner–Floyd isomorphism relates MGL to motivic algebraic K‑theory KGL.

The Betti realization functor sends MGL to MU, inducing a map of coefficient rings L → MU_* that is an isomorphism. This compatibility shows that the motivic formal group law specializes to the topological one. Moreover, the motivic slice filtration of MGL, studied by Voevodsky and later by Spitzweck and Østvær, reveals that the slices are motivic Eilenberg–MacLane spectra, providing a motivic analogue of the Adams–Novikov filtration. The slice spectral sequence for MGL converges to its homotopy groups and degenerates at the E₂‑page after rationalization.

Applications of MGL include the proof of the Milnor conjecture and the Bloch–Kato conjecture via the motivic Steenrod algebra, the construction of motivic elliptic cohomology and the motivic sigma orientation of MGL → Mtmf (the motivic analogue of TMF), and the computation of the motivic stable homotopy groups of spheres in low weights. The spectrum MGL also plays a central role in the motivic version of the chromatic redshift conjecture, where the height of the formal group law increases under algebraic K‑theory, mirroring the topological redshift phenomenon.

Cobordism and the Algebraic K‑Theory of Thom Spectra

The algebraic K‑theory of a ring spectrum R, denoted K(R), is a fundamental invariant that captures higher algebraic structure. For Thom spectra such as MO, MSO, MSpin, MU, and MGL, the K‑theory groups K_*(R) are closely related to topological cyclic homology TC(R) via the Dundas–Goodwillie–McCarthy theorem, which states that the relative K‑theory of a nilpotent extension of ring spectra agrees with the relative TC. This equivalence allows the computation of K‑theory of Thom spectra using cyclotomic spectra and the cyclotomic trace map K(R) → TC(R).

For the unoriented Thom spectrum MO, the algebraic K‑theory K(MO) has been studied by Ausoni and Rognes. They showed that the 2‑primary part of K(MO) is closely linked to the image of the J‑homomorphism in the stable homotopy groups of spheres. The cyclotomic trace K(MO) → TC(MO) induces an isomorphism on homotopy groups after 2‑completion in a range, and the homotopy groups of TC(MO) can be computed via the Bökstedt spectral sequence. This computation reveals that K(MO) detects the same chromatic information as the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for the sphere at the prime 2.

For the complex Thom spectrum MU, the algebraic K‑theory K(MU) is expected to exhibit chromatic redshift: its homotopy groups should see higher chromatic heights than MU itself. The work of Ausoni and Rognes on K(ku) and K(KO) provides evidence for this phenomenon. The K‑theory of MU is related to the K‑theory of the Lubin–Tate spectra E_n via the Galois descent spectral sequence, and the redshift conjecture predicts that the Morava K‑theory K(n)_* K(MU) is non‑zero for all n. This conjecture remains open but has been verified in low chromatic heights using the cyclotomic trace and the computation of TC(MU).

The algebraic K‑theory of the motivic Thom spectrum MGL, denoted K(MGL), is the algebraic K‑theory of the algebraic cobordism spectrum. By the motivic version of the Dundas–Goodwillie–McCarthy theorem, K(MGL) is equivalent to TC(MGL) after suitable completions. The homotopy groups of K(MGL) are expected to encode the algebraic K‑theory of the base field and the higher Chow groups of smooth schemes. The motivic cyclotomic trace provides a map K(MGL) → TC(MGL) that is an isomorphism on homotopy groups after p‑completion for regular Noetherian base schemes, linking motivic algebraic K‑theory to motivic topological cyclic homology.

In the context of cobordism, the algebraic K‑theory of Thom spectra provides a bridge between geometric cobordism invariants and the chromatic filtration of the stable homotopy category. The map from the cobordism ring Ω_*^G to K_*(MG) induced by the Thom isomorphism and the Hurewicz map allows one to detect cobordism classes that are invisible to ordinary homology. For example, the K‑theory of MSpin detects the Stolz invariant for 7‑manifolds, and the K‑theory of MString detects the Witten genus. These connections illustrate how higher algebraic K‑theory refines the information contained in the classical cobordism rings.

  • Confusing the Kreck–Stolz invariants with the Eells–Kuiper invariant; the former are a pair (s, t) for spin 7‑manifolds, while the latter is a single invariant for homotopy spheres.
  • Assuming the APS index theorem applies without the product metric condition near the boundary; the theorem requires a cylindrical end to define the eta invariant correctly.
  • Believing that positive Ricci curvature forces the oriented cobordism class to vanish; it only forces rational null‑cobordism after inverting 2, and non‑zero torsion classes may persist.
  • Treating MGL as a topological spectrum rather than a motivic one; its bigrading and slice filtration are essential features that have no analogue in classical stable homotopy theory.
  • Overlooking that the cyclotomic trace K(R) → TC(R) is an equivalence only after nilpotent completion; for non‑nilpotent extensions the relative K‑theory and relative TC may differ.
  • Assuming the algebraic K‑theory of MU is known in all chromatic heights; the redshift conjecture is still open beyond height 2.
  • Neglecting that the rho invariant defined via APS is a homomorphism on the spin cobordism group only after reducing modulo ℤ; the raw eta invariant is not a cobordism invariant.
